The verb "becoming" is also significant: the reference need not be confined to one Brahmāṇḍa but may indicate the whole root *prakṛti* from which such worlds arise.

In the second line, if "being a beginning" is taken as "being a cause," it may refer to certain eternal attendants who, according to the chapter on divine weapons and ornaments in the Viṣṇu Purāṇa that underlies Śrī Deśika's teaching, participate causally in functions connected with the *līlā-vibhūti*. If all the Nityasūris are intended, "being primordial" can simply mean "existing from beginningless time."

Since creation has already been mentioned in the opening, the third line naturally turns to the causes of dissolution, which fits the phrase "time of the end." There "beginning" may therefore signify an agent of destruction. In the Brahma-sūtra passage interpreted as teaching the consumer or destroyer, Śrībhāṣyakāra derives from a verbal root the sense "eater," that is, the agent of dissolution; that semantic possibility may also be remembered here.

Even if all the celestial beings are included, no difficulty follows. "You have declared their ending time": You have fixed their lifespans by statements such as "those who know the day of Brahmā understand it as lasting a thousand yugas" and "all worlds up to Brahmā's are subject to return, O Arjuna; but having reached Me, O son of Kuntī, there is no rebirth." Because You have made a common law for all celestial beings rather than letting each act however he pleases, none can escape that limit.

Alternatively, instead of taking "O beginning of time" as a vocative, one may divide it as "the time that has become the beginning" and read the phrase as "You who are primordial Time." Parāśara too declares Bhagavān, the inner ruler of time, to be "beginningless Time."

## Pāsuram 9

### Tamil Mūlam

தாதுலாவுகொன்றைமாலை துன்னுசெஞ்சடைச்சிவன்  
நீதியால்வணங்குபாத நின்மலா நிலாயசீர்  
வேதவாணர்கீதவேள்வி நீதியான கேள்வியார்  
நீதியால்வணங்குகின்ற நீர்மை நின்கணின்றதே.

### IAST Transliteration

*tātulāvukoṉṟaimālai tuṉṉuceñcaṭaiccivaṉ*  
*nītiyālvaṇaṅkupāta niṉmalā nilāyacīr*  
*vētavāṇarkītavēḷvi nītiyāṉa kēḷviyār*  
*nītiyālvaṇaṅkukiṉṟa nīrmai niṉkaṇiṉṟatē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Stainless One, whose feet Śiva - with dense red matted hair and garlands of blossoming koṉṟai - worships according to proper rule! The settled greatness of being worshiped according to the ancient norm by Vedic masters, by those devoted to Sāman-song and sacrifice, and by great men versed in disciplined inquiry, abides in You alone.

### English Translation of the Commentary

One might object: "The Vaiṣṇava religion is not the only tradition that flourishes with prestige in the world; Śaivism too is widely honored. How then can such unique superiority be assigned to You?" The Āḻvār replies that the matter can easily be determined from the conduct of authoritative elders themselves.

**Word-by-word gloss.** "Garland of koṉṟai in which the flower-parts move and shine" - garlands made of koṉṟai blossoms. "Śiva with close, red matted hair" - Śiva whose dense locks are reddish. "By rule" - according to the proper method. "O One whose feet are worshiped" - whose divine feet are bowed to by him. "O stainless One" - One opposed to every defect. "With established excellence" - possessing stable qualities and predominance of sattva. "Vedic masters" - those devoted to studying and teaching the Veda. "Song and sacrifice" - great sacrifices accompanied by Sāman chant. "Those whose learning is governed by reason" - eminent persons who have heard and mastered Mīmāṃsā and related disciplines supported by sound reasoning. "By rule" - according to the ancient tradition of valid authority. "The condition of being worshiped" - the status of being reverently adored. "In You alone" - it is firmly established only in You.

It is true that many people worship Śiva. Yet Śiva himself, through his own conduct, continually teaches that he is not to be regarded as the Supreme Reality. Considering the loftiest flowers appropriate only for You, the Lord of the entire universe, he has chosen the relatively humble koṉṟai as the flower suitable for his own adornment and worship. By intense austerity he also makes known to the world that he himself performs service to You. From this one understands that he is not inherently free from all defects, that he performs severe austerities in order to remove defects belonging to him, and that for this purpose he rejects as far as possible even enjoyments that come to him and practices detachment. You, whom he worships, are the truly Stainless One, and even his impurities are removed by Your grace.

One should not object: "If he is thus a Śrīvaiṣṇava devotee, should he not display the appropriate marks? How can he smear himself with ashes and exalt himself?" He worships according to the rule applicable to his own office. Because he follows a particular devotional path taught in the Atharvaśiras, his use of ashes is legitimate within that discipline. His teaching of other doctrines likewise occurs because You, the Supreme Lord, have appointed him as an authorized functionary to bewilder those of demonic disposition. The command is expressed in a traditional verse to the effect: "O mighty-armed Rudra, create deluding scriptures; show a path requiring little effort and quickly display its fruit." He acts in obedience to that divine assignment. Thus no fault belongs either to him in fulfilling his appointed function or to You who remain stainless.

Although many people, either relying on such delusive teachings or acting independently, propagate Śaivism, Your superiority is firmly established by the noblest authorities who speak the truth. Śaiva Āgamas are the source of specifically Śaiva doctrine. The Vedas, however, repeatedly proclaim You as the Supreme Deity. For those who have truly studied the Vedas, this determination is easy. When some fail to reach it, the causes are absence of predominating sattva, refusal to remain impartial and enter into proper determination, obstinate attachment to a prior position, and desire for limited fruits.

The Vedic masters of established excellence know the matter clearly. As the Gītā says, "Among the Vedas I am the Sāmaveda," they perform, without desire for personal reward, great sacrifices such as the Soma rites accompanied by Sāman chant, which is itself a manifestation of You. Through such practice sattva becomes predominant. Equipped with sound reasoning, having correctly heard and understood disciplines such as Karma-Mīmāṃsā and Brahma-Mīmāṃsā, and conducting themselves in accordance with the knowledge of reality they have acquired, such Vedic persons all know and worship You alone as the Supreme Deity. Therefore there is no basis for accepting, on the strength of Śaiva Āgamas contrary to the Veda, the assertion that Śiva is the Supreme Deity.

In this connection one should contemplate Yamunācārya's teaching to the effect: "Those of demonic disposition cannot refute You, whose supremacy is established by Your character, form, deeds, supreme purity of sattva, by the powerful sāttvika scriptures, and by the judgments of renowned knowers of the highest divine truth." One should also recall sayings attributed to Vyāsa, Parāśara, and others: "There is no scripture higher than the Veda, and no deity higher than Keśava"; "For those who seek to know reality by reasons facing every side, the one Supreme Reality is the great yogin Hari, Nārāyaṇa"; and "From Viṣṇu the universe has arisen, and in Him alone it abides."

The introductory explanation given by earlier teachers may also be accepted: because the Lord is worshiped both by persons such as Rudra who desire other fruits and by *paramaikāntins* who desire the Lord alone, the verse intends to establish Him as the Supreme Deity.

If "established excellence" is taken as qualifying the Veda, the phrase teaches the superiority of the Veda as something stable and permanent, unlike inference or sectarian Āgamas that can vary, and as free from human defects. "Learning" (*kēḷvi*) may mean inquiry (*jijñāsā*), including the Mīmāṃsā beginning with "the desire to know dharma"; more generally it may mean what has been heard and thus the disciplines that must be received through tradition. "Governed by reason": Mīmāṃsā is famously structured by reasoning, and the separate emphasis on *nīti* may also indicate the assistance of a purified Nyāya discipline. It is equally fitting to understand "learning according to rule" as hearing and the other spiritual disciplines performed in the manner taught by the Veda. When the Vedas are properly studied and investigated by the principles taught by Vyāsa and the other sages, it becomes certain beyond doubt that Viṣṇu, the cosmic-form Lord, is the object worshiped in all sacrifices and that He alone is the Supreme Deity.

## Pāsuram 10

### Tamil Mūlam

தன்னுளே திரைத்தெழுந் தரங்கவெண்தடங்கடல்  
தன்னுளே திரைத்தெழுந்தடங்குகின்றதன்மைபோல்  
நின்னுளேபிறந்திறந்து நிற்பவும் திரிபவும்  
நின்னுளேயடங்குகின்ற நீர்மைநின்கணின்றதே.

### IAST Transliteration

*taṉṉuḷē tiraitteḻun taraṅkaveṇtaṭaṅkaṭal*  
*taṉṉuḷē tiraitteḻuntaṭaṅkukiṉṟataṉmaipōl*  
*niṉṉuḷēpiṟantiṟantu niṟpavum tiripavum*  
*niṉṉuḷēyaṭaṅkukiṉṟa nīrmainiṉkaṇiṉṟatē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

Just as the vast, pure ocean rises within itself as surging waves and those very waves subside again within itself, so all that stands and all that moves is born within You, dies, and finally subsides within You. This nature of being the place into which all dissolves abides in You alone.

### English Translation of the Commentary

In the eighth pāsuram, saying that the Lord is the cause of dissolution could still have been understood merely in the sense that, like Rudra and others, He is an efficient cause of destruction. This verse therefore explicitly teaches that the Lord is also the material ground into which everything dissolves. He is not merely the impeller of Rudra and others when they perform acts of destruction; He alone possesses the status, which they do not possess, of being the substrate or material cause of final reabsorption.

Therefore one cannot infer from Vedic statements such as "Rudra alone stood, there was no second" that Śiva is the Supreme Deity, as though the world in the great dissolution actually became Śiva or Brahmā. Rudra, who is not the Self of all, cannot be the place of dissolution of all. The Subāla Upaniṣad and related texts clearly teach that Nārāyaṇa alone is the Self of all. Since there must be a material ground of dissolution distinct from Rudra, one cannot say that Rudra alone exists in the final dissolution. The Śruti states very explicitly, "Nārāyaṇa alone existed; not Brahmā, not Īśāna." Thus the text excludes Rudra and teaches that Nārāyaṇa alone remains at the time of dissolution. To show that this is what the Vedic masters can establish without controversy, the Āḻvār declares Nārāyaṇa to be the place of final reabsorption.

**Word-by-word gloss.** "Rising in itself in waves" - swelling and surging within itself. "With waves" - possessed of billows. "Pure, spacious ocean" - the clear and vast sea. "Within itself" - in itself alone. "Rising in waves and subsiding" - surging as waves and then becoming still again. "Like that" - in the same manner. "Those that stand and those that move" - immobile plants and moving beings. "Within You" - in You alone. "Being born and dying" - coming into existence and perishing. "Within You" - again, in You alone. "The nature of subsiding" - the essential capacity to be the locus of their dissolution. "In You alone" - that nature abides only in You.

Following the sense of the latter half, the opening half should be understood as "just as the waves that rise in the ocean subside in the ocean." The ocean is the illustration for the Lord; the mobile and immobile beings are illustrated by waves. Rising corresponds to birth; subsiding corresponds first to death and then to final dissolution. The wind that causes the waves to rise can be taken as the illustration for the Lord's resolve that causes sentient beings to enter manifested creation.

The expression "the vast pure ocean whose waves rise within itself" is carefully chosen. The single word "ocean" includes both the upper aspect that appears as waves and the deeper body of water beneath them. In the same way the word "You" can denote Brahman qualified by subtle sentient and insentient beings in the causal state. Just as waves qualify the ocean, the entities that stand and move qualify Brahman. The adjective "vast" can point especially to the lower, stable body of the sea beneath the waves; that aspect is the closest illustration for the Lord as sustaining ground. "Pure" expresses the stainlessness of the unchanging depth and thereby indicates that the Lord remains free from defects and transformations.

Some take "pure/white" literally as the color white and ask how this can illustrate the dark, ocean-hued Lord. One reply is that whiteness is natural to water. It may then be objected that even if pure, undifferentiated water has such a color, the actual ocean commonly appears otherwise. Although deep sea appears blue, water thrown upward in spray looks white. Yet the more important point here is not color but purity: the word is best understood as emphasizing the stainlessness already described. The repetition of "rising in waves" clarifies that when the ocean is said to become calm, it is specifically its wave-aspect that subsides.

In applying the illustration, the Āḻvār does not say "You become those that stand and move and then subside within Yourself." He deliberately uses a different construction in order to reject doctrines such as those of Bhāskara and Yādava which fail to preserve the distinction of the three realities and suppose that the substance Brahman itself literally becomes the sentient and insentient universe, just as ocean-water becomes a wave. The natural sense here is instead: You are one reality, and the things that stand and move are realities distinct from You, though wholly dependent upon You. According to our doctrine, words such as "standing" and "moving" ultimately extend through the individual selves to Brahman as their Inner Controller; this will become clearer later.

"Dying" refers to the intermediate dissolutions that occur during the manifested creation, when beings lose gross bodies and remain associated with subtler elemental conditions. "Subsiding" refers to the later aggregate dissolution. Thus both forms of dissolution occur in the Lord. Even if Rudra functions as an agent of death within creation, beings do not dissolve into Rudra's own essential nature. After Rudra himself reaches his end, the final aggregate dissolution is into Nārāyaṇa; there is no room for doubt on this point.

The Upaniṣadic comparison says, "As flowing rivers enter the ocean...." When many rivers fall into the sea, the waters remain in reality distinct in origin yet become undifferentiated in appearance. Likewise the Lord is distinct and the individual souls are distinct. As the lower body of water supports the water above it, the Lord supports the souls. As the upper water, governed by wind, assumes many forms as waves, souls, governed by the Lord's resolve in accordance with their karma, receive many names and forms. Wave after wave rises; when the wind ceases, the sea becomes calm and wave-less. In the same way, when the Lord does not exercise the resolve for creation, the succession of births that had risen again and again ceases and the sentient beings remain withdrawn. Then, as the lower still sea alone visibly remains when the waves subside, at the great dissolution Nārāyaṇa alone remains in the condition in which He can be manifestly spoken of. This is the intended meaning.

## Pāsuram 11

### Tamil Mūlam

சொல்லினாற்றொடர்ச்சிநீ சொலப்படும்பொருளும்நீ  
சொல்லினாற்சொலப்படாது தோன்றுகின்ற சோதிநீ  
சொல்லினாற்படைக்கநீ படைக்கவந்துதோன்றினார்  
சொல்லினாற்சுருங்க நின் குணங்கள்சொல்லவல்லரே.

### IAST Transliteration

*colliṉāṟṟoṭarccinī colappaṭumporuḷumnī*  
*colliṉāṟcolappaṭātu tōṉṟukiṉṟa cōtinī*  
*colliṉāṟpaṭaikkanī paṭaikkavantutōṉṟiṉār*  
*colliṉāṟcuruṅka niṉ kuṇaṅkaḷcollavallarē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You are the continuity and combination of words; You are also the meaning expressed by them. You are the Radiance that shines forth, yet cannot be exhausted by words. Because You created, Brahmā and the other creators appeared and, using Vedic words, came forth to create. Even if they employed every word, could they describe even briefly all Your qualities?

### English Translation of the Commentary

Having thoroughly described Śrīman Nārāyaṇa as the Inner Controller of all, the promoter of every true means, Lord of both vibhūtis, the One who incarnates in many ways in the world, creator of Brahmā and the whole universe, supporter of all, vastly superior even to Śiva, cause even of the Nityasūris and of Time, the One whose greatness is known through the gods, Veda, and Vedic authorities, and the material ground of the birth, continuance, and dissolution of the entire universe, the Āḻvār now reflects: "How much can I possibly say of such a You in this prabandham?" He declares that even Brahmā, Rudra, and the others cannot adequately speak of Him.

**Word-by-word gloss.** "The continuity of words is You" - all compositions formed by joining words are ultimately You. "The meaning expressed by them is You" - all the objects denoted by them are You in the qualified sense already explained. "Not expressed by words" - unable to be completely spoken by all forms of speech. "The Radiance that appears" - the Supreme Light apprehended through the manifestation of its own nature and qualities. "Because You created" - because You brought creation into being. "They appeared to create by words" - Brahmā and the other creators, dwelling in the cosmic egg, emerged to create the world by employing the words of the Veda. "Even with words, could they speak Your qualities in brief?" - even by uttering innumerable expressions, do they possess the power to summarize Your auspicious attributes? They do not.

In the phrase "continuity of words," the instrumental ending can be merely metrical. Earlier teachers also explain it as: "You are the One who, through Vedānta-śāstra, creates the continuing relation with Yourself." Unlike those who become attached to lordly prosperity or isolated self-enjoyment, persons who truly understand the supreme purport of Vedānta are made by You to enter into ever-deepening relation with You.

"You are the meaning expressed." As the maxim says, "name-words extend as far as their objects," all things have names and there are people who know those names. Yet they do not necessarily know that You, the Inner Controller of every object, are the ultimate referent. Each word directly denotes an object in the world and, through that object, denotes You as well. In accordance with Jaimini's principle that there is no contradiction in direct reference where appropriate, words can also denote You directly in contexts whose primary purport is the Lord.

"Not expressed by words" can first mean that because people do not know this extension of words to You, You remain unspoken by them. Moreover, You who establish and bear names are not Yourself confined by a name. The Upaniṣadic statement teaches: "Space is indeed the bearer of name and form; that which is within/beyond them is Brahman." Thus You are not subject, as ordinary persons are, to injunctions and prohibitions tied to social names and classifications, such as "a brāhmaṇa should sacrifice" or "a brāhmaṇa should not drink liquor."

Again, "not expressed by words" can mean that even if all the Vedas and all living beings speak to the utmost of their ability, Your greatness cannot be finished in speech.

"To create by words": the traditional teaching says that at the beginning Brahmā creates the names and forms of beings and the whole world of ritual actions from the very words of the Veda, and creates the gods and other classes accordingly. Thus Brahmā and the other creators, examining the descriptions received in the Veda, produce gods, sages, and the rest by uttering and applying the words appropriate to them.

"Could they speak?" Even if they formed innumerable sentences from an infinity of words, they could not finish describing all Your auspicious qualities. That is the force of the question.

## Pāsuram 12

### Tamil Mūlam

உலகுதன்னைநீபடைத்தி உள்ளோடுக்கிவைத்திமீண்டு  
உலகுதன்னுளேபிறத்தி ஓரிடத்தையல்லையால்  
உலகுநின்னோடொன்றிநிற்க வேறுநிற்றியாதலால்  
உலகு(கில்)நின்னையுள்ளசூழல் யாவருள்ளவல்லரே.

### IAST Transliteration

*ulakutaṉṉainīpaṭaitti uḷḷōṭukkivaittimīṇṭu*  
*ulakutaṉṉuḷēpiṟatti ōriṭattaiyallaiyāl*  
*ulakuniṉṉōṭoṉṟiniṟka vēṟuniṟṟiyātalāl*  
*ulaku(kil)niṉṉaiyuḷḷacūḻal yāvaruḷḷavallarē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You Yourself create the world; You draw it in and keep it within Yourself; then, astonishingly, You are born again within the very world You created. You are not confined to any one place. The world remains inseparably dependent upon You, yet You stand within it in another mode as though dependent upon it. Because of this, who can comprehend Your wondrous strategy with respect to the world?

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār now begins to enjoy the glory of the Lord's incarnations.

**Word-by-word gloss.** "You create the world" - You Yourself bring the universe into existence. "You draw it in and keep it within" - at dissolution You contract it and hold it within Your own body. "Again, within the world" - reversing that relation, within the very world created by You. "You are born" - You take birth as one of its inhabitants and descendants. "You are not one place" - You have all things as Your place and are not confined to a single locus. "The world stands one with You" - it remains inseparably established in dependence upon You through *apṛthak-siddhi*. "Yet You stand separately" - in incarnation You stand in it in a distinct embodied condition as though You were a dependent resident. "Therefore, Your design concerning the world" - this astonishing mode of Your relation to the universe. "Who can think it?" - no one can fully comprehend it.

You are the creator of all the beings in the world; yet You Yourself are produced, as though a child, through Daśaratha, Vasudeva, and others who belong to the very class of creatures You created. That is one wonder. You place the whole world inside Your belly; yet within that world You accept particular places as Your dwelling. That is another wonder. Because the world is thus united with and dependent on You, and You in turn unite Yourself with it in incarnation, Your true nature is not easy to comprehend. In the previous pāsuram the Āḻvār said that You cannot be fully spoken; here he says that You cannot even be fully conceived.

For "You are not one place," some give the philosophical interpretation: You cannot simply be called the cause, nor can You simply be called an effect. The first line stated two relations, and the second line gives two striking reversals of them. "You stand separately" is also explained as: You remain distinguished from the world by possessing an extraordinary, non-material divine auspicious form.

In the fourth line the elders read "world" just as in the first three and understand "in the world." The case ending in "You" can be metrically inert, like the ending in the second line; or one may understand "because" in the earlier ending and here read "the wondrous strategy that exists in You." The elders also accept the syntax: "Who can inwardly comprehend You who possess this wondrous strategy?"

## Pāsuram 13

### Tamil Mūlam

இன்னையென்று சொல்லலாவ தில்லையாது மிட்டிடைப்  
பின்னைகேள்வ னென்பருன் பிணக்குணர்ந்த பெற்றியோர்  
பின்னையாய கோலபோடு பேருமூரு மாதியும்  
நின்னையார் நினைக்கவல்லர் நீர்மையால் நினைக்கிலே.

### IAST Transliteration

*iṉṉaiyeṉṟu collalāva tillaiyātu miṭṭiṭaip*  
*piṉṉaikēḷva ṉeṉparuṉ piṇakkuṇarnta peṟṟiyōr*  
*piṉṉaiyāya kōlapōṭu pērumūru mātiyum*  
*niṉṉaiyār niṉaikkavallar nīrmaiyāl niṉaikkilē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

There is no way at all to say, "You are precisely such-and-such." Those of mature understanding who have grasped the world's disputes concerning You call You the beloved husband of slender-waisted Nappiṉṉai. Yet Your form, Your sacred name, Your divine abode, and the primordial form that lies behind them - who can truly conceive these? If anyone does conceive them as they are, it is only by Your gracious nature.

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār says that however apparently small or ordinary the births You assume may be, Your unique transcendence never leaves You.

**Word-by-word gloss.** "There is no way to say 'such is He'" - no one can define You in any single manner. "Those who have understood Your controversy" - learned persons of sattvic disposition who know the disputes in the world concerning You. "They call You the husband of slender-waisted Piṉṉai" - they speak of You as the beloved of Nappiṉṉai Pirāṭṭi. "Then the form that is Yours" - Your uniquely distinguished divine auspicious body. "Name, town, and beginning" - Your sacred name, divine abode, and primordial causal form. "Who can think of them?" - who has the power to know them exactly as they are? "If one thinks" - if someone reflects on the truth in the manner of scripture. "By Your nature alone" - such cognition occurs solely through Your grace.

You possess an essential nature, qualities, and majesty that no one can reach, and at the same time You assume forms resembling those of ordinary beings and mingle among them. Who, then, can define You by saying, "He is only such a one"? Yet there are people who deny You altogether and engage in quarrel about You. Persons of mature understanding, aware of that dispute, become captivated by Your *sauśīlya* and other qualities and say that You are the heroic cowherd, the lover of Nappiṉṉai, the simple maiden of the cowherd clan.

Once this is understood, one must also recognize that Your divine body, sacred names, supreme abode, and original form there are all unlike the material counterparts found here, and that You descend without abandoning that distinctive nature. Even if the secret of incarnation is established again and again through many scriptural authorities, who can truly hold it in mind? If firm and believing cognition arises in this matter, that too must occur only through Your grace. We have stated the point briefly; it may be expanded with the relevant authorities.

Some connect *yātum* with what follows and interpret: "Leaving everything else, they call You the beloved of Piṉṉai with the slender waist, the lover belonging to the cowherd community." The third line is also explained either with reference to all incarnations generally or specifically to the *vyūha* manifestations. The accusative ending in "You" can be merely metrical; it can also be understood as possessive, "Your...." Some supply an understood "unless" with the last line: "Unless one thinks through Your own gracious nature, who could otherwise think of You?"

## Pāsuram 14

### Tamil Mūlam

தூய்மையோகமாயினாய் துழாயலங்கல்மாலையாய்  
ஆமையாகி யாழ்கடல் துயின்ற ஆதிதேவ! நின்  
நாமதேயமின்னதென்ன வல்லமல்லவாகிலும்  
சாமவேதகீதனாய சக்ரபாணியல்லையே.

### IAST Transliteration

*tūymaiyōkamāyiṉāy tuḻāyalaṅkalmālaiyāy*  
*āmaiyāki yāḻkaṭal tuyiṉṟa ātitēva! niṉ*  
*nāmatēyamiṉṉateṉṉa vallamallavākilum*  
*cāmavētakītaṉāya cakrapāṇiyallaiyē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You have become the pure Yoga; You wear the garland of sacred tulasī. Becoming a tortoise, O Primal Deity who reclines in the deep ocean, we are not capable of defining and declaring the sacred name that embraces all Your modes. Even so, are You not the wielder of the discus, the One sung in the Sāmaveda?

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār says that even when You appear in an animal form, Your greatness cannot be finished in speech.

**Word-by-word gloss.** "You became pure yoga" - You are the perfectly pure yoga. "You who wear a garland made of sacred tulasī leaves." "Becoming a tortoise" - taking the Kūrma incarnation. "In the deep ocean" - in the profound sea. "O Primal Deity who reclines" - O God who is the cause of all and who graciously rests in yogic sleep. "Your name" - the sacred designation of You who are thus manifold. "We are not able to say 'it is exactly this'" - we have no power to define it exhaustively. "Even so, are You not the wielder of the discus sung in the Sāmaveda?" - at least this much can be said: You are the Lord with the divine discus, celebrated by the Sāman chanters.

"Pure yoga" can mean yoga performed without desire for limited reward, yoga directed to liberation. *Yoga* may mean meditation or a saving means: bhakti and prapatti. Because such yoga is a cause of purification for the individual self, it is itself described as pure. The tulasī garland, by contrast, indicates the Supreme Self as Lord of both vibhūtis and as the attainable goal. Thus the opening describes Him as both means and goal. One may also take *yoga* to mean the already-established saving means (*siddhopāya*), namely the Lord Himself. The second line then speaks of His incarnations undertaken to bestow various benefits.

On "becoming a tortoise": in pāsuram 35, Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai, citing a Śruti phrase concerning a moving tortoise, explains a tortoise-form at the time of dissolution. Here, however, the commentator understands specifically the Milk-Ocean Kūrma incarnation.

One may ask: "The Lord already reclined in the deep ocean before becoming the tortoise. How can the verse say 'becoming the tortoise, reclining in the ocean'?" Such non-chronological conjunction is common throughout the prabandhams: actions from different times are joined without intending temporal sequence; compare expressions such as "splitting the horse's mouth, passing between the twin trees, measuring the earth." Yet a temporal connection can also be found here, as Śrī Deśika's *Daśāvatāra Stotra* makes clear.

Deśika poetically prays that the worlds be protected by the waves produced by the breath of the Supreme Lord sleeping immovably in the tortoise form while Mount Mandara rotates upon His back; the Milk Ocean, set in motion by those waves, becomes like a swing, and upon His serpent couch within it the Lord sports eternally together with Śrī. Thus Kūrma and the Lord's oceanic repose are poetically continuous.

"O Primal Deity": the sense is that the Primal God assumed such an incarnation in response to the petitions of Brahmā and the other gods.

On "we cannot say what Your name is": here "name" is explained as including the qualities and activities of the Lord in the tortoise form. Even if the opening lines state only some names, who can know and define a designation that comprehensively communicates all Your forms, qualities, and deeds during the churning of the Ocean of Milk - pressing down Mandara from above, supporting it from below, encouraging the gods and demons, and Yourself participating in the churning? If we cannot adequately tell the history of even this single incarnation, how could we know a "name" capable of expressing all the qualities and activities of Your long-lasting manifestations and of Your primordial state?

"Are You not the discus-wielder sung in the Sāmaveda?" Since the Chāndogya Upaniṣad's meditation on the Person within the sun speaks of the lotus-eyed Lord, and that very Lord is the wielder of the discus, the expression can be understood in that way. In addition, the earlier portion of Sāmavedic tradition itself may directly celebrate Him as the discus-bearer. Śrī Deśika, in the *Saccaritra-rakṣā*, cites a Sāmavedic Upaniṣadic passage meaning: "O Lord even of Brahmā and all beings! Your purifying discus is spread everywhere; You, the Lord of the worlds, surround the bodies of all devotees through it." Other passages may likewise be consulted.

The verse may also recall the story that, when the nectar obtained from churning the ocean was being distributed to the gods, the disguised Siṃhikeya entered among them; the Lord severed his head with the discus, producing the being known as Rāhu.

## Pāsuram 15

### Tamil Mūlam

அங்கமாறு வேதகான்கு மாகிநின்றவற்றுளே  
தங்குகின்ற தன்மையாய் தடங்கடல் பணத்தலை  
செங்கணாகணைக்கிடந்த செல்வமல்குசீரினாய்  
சங்கவண்ணமன்னமேனி சார்ங்கபாணியல்லையே.

### IAST Transliteration

*aṅkamāṟu vētakāṉku mākiniṉṟavaṟṟuḷē*  
*taṅkukiṉṟa taṉmaiyāy taṭaṅkaṭal paṇattalai*  
*ceṅkaṇākaṇaikkiṭanta celvamalkucīriṉāy*  
*caṅkavaṇṇamaṉṉamēṉi cārṅkapāṇiyallaiyē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

Becoming the six limbs and the four Vedas, and abiding as the meaning within them; reclining upon the serpent-bed whose hoods and red eyes spread wide in the vast Milk Ocean; O One whose auspicious qualities overflow through the presence of Śrī, whose body is like the hue of a conch - are You not the wielder of Śārṅga?

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār now turns with delight to the form that reclines in the Ocean of Milk.

**Word-by-word gloss.** "Six limbs" - the six Vedāṅgas beginning with phonetics. "Four Vedas" - the Ṛgveda and the other three. "Having become and stood as these, abiding within them" - appearing as these scriptures and being their inner meaning; alternatively, being the One who must be known through them alone. "The vast ocean" - the expansive Milk Ocean. "The serpent-bed" - the beautiful body of Ādiśeṣa, with hoods spread and red eyes. "One who lies there" - the Lord reclining upon that couch. "One whose excellence overflows with wealth" - the One whose auspicious qualities become full through Periya Pirāṭṭiyār. "A body like the color of the conch" - a divine form comparable to a white conch. "Are You not Śārṅgapāṇi?" - You are indeed the bearer of the Śārṅga bow.

In Paramapada the Lord is praised by countless divine utterances. When He comes to the Ocean of Milk, He makes Himself accessible to the verbal service of Brahmā and the other gods by becoming, as it were, the very words of the six Vedāṅgas and four Vedas. He is also the cosmic-form Inner Controller of all the deities spoken of by those texts. In order that those gods may have an opportunity to submit their difficulties, He graciously reclines in the Milk Ocean.

Ādiśeṣa spreads his hoods above Him like a canopy, his eyes reddened and blazing with protective vigilance: countless fierce demons and rākṣasas have obtained enormous boons, and Śeṣa, as it were, fears what offense they might attempt against our Lord. On the lovely body of that serpent, made into a couch, the Lord shines, highly honored by Periya Pirāṭṭiyār and therefore overflowing in auspicious glory. That very Lord is Śārṅgapāṇi.

On "overflowing with wealth": as in expressions such as "the wealthy Nārāyaṇa," *wealth* here means Pirāṭṭi herself. In the Milk Ocean it is necessary to mention His union with her.

One might ask how the Lord famed for His dark form can be said to have a body resembling the color of a conch. The Lord is also described as white in the Kṛta age. Furthermore, as in the poetic description "whose charming form is starred by showers of spray from the waves of the Milk Ocean," the whiteness of milk spread all around Him can make His form appear conch-colored. Another interpretation takes "conch-colored swan" as alluding to His Haṃsa incarnation. Since scripture establishes that in the Haṃsa incarnation He taught the Vedas, the opening phrase "becoming the six limbs and four Vedas" fits that sense as well.

Some understand "Śārṅgapāṇi" as recalling the Rāma incarnation. It is equally possible to take it as the famous reclining Śārṅgapāṇi of Kuḍantai.

## Pāsuram 16

### Tamil Mūlam

தலைக்கணத் துகட்குழம்பு சாதிசோதி தோற்றமாய்  
நிலைக்கணங்கள் காணவந்து நிற்றியேலும் நீடிரும்  
கலைக்கணங்கள் சொற்பொருட் கருத்தினால் நினைக்கொணா  
மலைக்கணங்கள் போலுணர்த்து மாட்சி நின்றன் மாட்சியே.

### IAST Transliteration

*talaikkaṇat tukaṭkuḻampu cāticōti tōṟṟamāy*  
*nilaikkaṇaṅkaḷ kāṇavantu niṟṟiyēlum nīṭirum*  
*kalaikkaṇaṅkaḷ coṟporuṭ karuttiṉāl niṉaikkoṇā*  
*malaikkaṇaṅkaḷ pōluṇarttu māṭci niṉṟaṉ māṭciyē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

Appearing as a radiant manifestation among the highest hosts, the minute, the mixed middle orders, and the various classes, You descend so that even the groups of stationary beings may behold You. Even when You stand thus among them, Your greatness is like ranges upon ranges of mountains: the vast assemblies of sciences, whether by the direct meanings of their words or by their intended purport, can only indicate it, never fully conceive it. Such indeed is the majesty of Your majesty.

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār says that even the magnificence present in the Lord's seemingly smallest incarnations is as unreachable by speech as that of His great incarnations.

**Word-by-word gloss.** "The highest host" can mean the excellent class, namely the hosts of gods. "The particle/minute" means the exceedingly small or low. "The mixed mass" means an intermediate class. "Class" indicates these various species. "Appearing as light" means appearing within such births outwardly as individual selves do. "The stationary groups come to see" means even immobile plants are given occasion to experience Him when He descends to this world. "Even when You stand so" - even in such a lowly manifestation. "Your majesty" - Your greatness. "Long and vast assemblies of arts/sciences" - the great masses of śāstras. "By the intended sense of their verbal meanings" - by both direct denotation and the purport derived from it. "Unable to conceive" - incapable of measuring it as 'so much and no more.' "Like ranges of mountains" - they can only reveal immense piled-up masses that no one can survey in every detail. Such is the greatness they indicate.

You appear like the many kinds of individual souls born among divine hosts, among stationary species such as the aśvattha tree, and among human and animal classes, each experiencing the fruits of its karma. Though You are unknowable even to the unfailing immortals, You make Yourself perceptible even to plants, whose awareness is so hidden and slight that one might almost call them devoid of knowledge. Even while standing in such an apparently low condition, all the disciplines spread through every time and region of the world could assemble together and devote themselves solely to speaking of You, yet they would not finish. Seeing that direct verbal statement is insufficient, they may attempt at least to indicate Your greatness through implication and intended purport; still they can only point to it like mountain piled upon mountain, never display every part without remainder. Who then can know Your greatness as it really is?

In the Rāma incarnation the Lord even concealed His divine status from Himself in the mode expressed by "I regard Myself as a man." Yet in that very condition, trees and waters without manifest intellectual life are described as languishing when deprived of His presence: "even the trees are withered, though bearing flowers and shoots," "the rivers have heated waters," and the pools and lakes likewise suffered. Therefore when He was near, they must have flourished and rejoiced. If even such beings experienced the accessibility and gentle condescension of that incarnation, can the greatness produced by those qualities be fully known or spoken?

Among gods, animals, humans, and plants, the divine host is highest; hence it is called the "head-host." There are many divine groups - Vasus, Rudras, Ādityas, Maruts, Sādhyas, Siddhas, Gandharvas, Kinnaras, and others - so the plural idea of a host is appropriate. *Tukaḷ*, "dust" or a tiny particle, may indicate the beings smallest in manifest intelligence, such as plants whose faint cognitive manifestation is like the light of a firefly. The "mixed mass" may then denote the animal and human classes. The word "class" can be carried backward over all of them.

Alternatively, "mixed class" can mean births produced by a mixture of merit and sin; "particle/blemish class" a sinful birth; and "head-host" a meritorious birth. Or "mixed class" can mean species produced by union of male and female, in contrast to higher divine hosts not born in that fashion, while "particle" denotes the still lower order. Our doctrine accepts that even individually formed earth, water, and the like in differentiated creation are presided over by selves. Bhagavān can even manifest as a great mountain or as sacred waters such as Puṣkara. "Appearing as light" then means that He enters these births without outwardly appearing different from the jīvas inhabiting such forms.

Only the confused middle class, one might say, becomes confused about these incarnations; hence perhaps it is called the "mixed/confused class." In fact some animals possess natural discrimination, for example knowing what is proper food and what is not, in ways that can surpass human behavior. Even the still lower stationary beings seem to have been granted by the Lord a hidden power of responding to His presence.

Some take "appearing as light" to teach that in every incarnation the Lord appears with His non-material divine auspicious body. If one then says that even when He is born in a low species He shines as pure radiance and makes even trees aware, the conclusion that His majesty cannot be exhausted is certainly true. Yet the following phrase "even if You stand [thus]" is less natural on that reading. The intended contrast is rather: though You are in truth radiant, You stand within various classes in a manner resembling beings that know only a little of immediate necessities and nearby things and are described as possessing merely internal, obscured awareness together with pleasure and pain; even then Your majesty is immeasurable.

On "by the verbal meaning and purport": a word has its directly established meaning, or an extended meaning reached by *lakṣaṇā*; that is its verbal meaning. Beyond this lies the intended import or implication. Even where the stated meaning is one, many consequences can be understood from it. If scripture says that the Lord is the cause of the world, it follows without explicit repetition that He is omniscient and omnipotent. If it says that serving Him is liberation, one understands also the abode in which Lord and servants dwell, their divine bodies, the enjoyable objects and instruments of enjoyment, blissful experience, and other details. The Vedas cannot state everything explicitly and therefore convey many matters by intended purport, just as the sacred Aṣṭākṣara implies service to Bhagavān's devotees, dependence, and forms of *kaiṅkarya*.

"Unable to think" is a phonetic contraction of "unable to conceive." Some construe it adverbially, "because it cannot be conceived" or "without being able to conceive," and end the sentence with "the assemblies of sciences cannot conceive." More naturally it qualifies the mountain ranges: Your greatness is indicated as though by ranges that themselves cannot be mentally encompassed. "Your majesty" is the subject; "a majesty that makes known [only in such fashion]" is the predicate.

## Pāsuram 17

### Tamil Mūlam

ஏகமூர்த்தி மூன்றுமூர்த்தி நாலுமூர்த்தி நன்மைசேர்  
போகமூர்த்தி புண்ணியத்தின் மூாத்தியெண்ணில் மூர்த்தியாய்  
நாகமூர்த்திசயனமாய் நலம்கடற் கிடந்துமேல்  
ஆகமூர்த்தி யாயவண்ண மென்கொலாதி தேவனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*ēkamūrtti mūṉṟumūrtti nālumūrtti naṉmaicēr*  
*pōkamūrtti puṇṇiyattiṉ mūāttiyeṇṇil mūrttiyāy*  
*nākamūrtticayaṉamāy nalamkaṭaṟ kiṭantumēl*  
*ākamūrtti yāyavaṇṇa meṉkolāti tēvaṉē!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Primal Deity, cause of the universe! Reclining in the good Milk Ocean with the body of the serpent as Your couch, You then become the auspicious single form, the three forms, the four forms, forms for enjoyment, forms connected with merit, and forms without number - and finally become the arcā form. What is the wonder of this mode of Yours?

### English Translation of the Commentary

This pāsuram turns to contemplation of the Lord's *arcā* manifestations.

**Word-by-word gloss.** "O Primal Deity" - O Lord of all, cause of the universe. "With the serpent's body as the couch" - using the bodily portion of Ādiśeṣa as a bed, or possessing that serpent couch. "In the good ocean" - the auspicious Milk Ocean. "Reclining there." "Above/beyond that" - proceeding further. "The auspicious single form," "three forms," "four forms," "a form for enjoyment," "a form of merit," and "countless forms" - all the many divine manifestations. "Becoming an *arcā* form" - assuming the worshipful installed image. "What is this manner?" - why, or what a wonder, that You do so.

Earlier teachers explain the first three lines as an expansion of *para*, *vyūha*, and *vibhava*, after which the verse marvels: "Having become all these forms, why also become the arcā form?" That explanation may certainly be accepted, and with it the syntax through the word "above" is straightforward.

Yet those manifestations have already been treated in earlier pāsurams. We therefore understand this verse as taking them as the background for introducing *arcā*, and the first two lines as enumerating the many incarnational forms now present in arcā. That is appropriate in a pāsuram specifically about worshipful images. The forms installed as arcā are not unrelated new beings; the same *para*, *vyūha*, and *vibhava* forms graciously consent to stand as arcā.

Thus the "single form" can be Para-Vāsudeva. There is a reckoning of "three forms" as Saṅkarṣaṇa, Pradyumna, and Aniruddha. Since Vāsudeva also has a distinct *vyūha* manifestation in addition to Para-Vāsudeva, one can count four *vyūha* forms, yielding "four forms." The elders also explain "four forms" as the four principles connected with the functions of the *vyūhas*: Pradhāna, Puruṣa, Avyakta, and Time.

"Joined with goodness": unlike other manifestations that appear for a limited historical period, arcā forms remain permanently accessible for all people to serve. Scripture and Ācārya teaching describe the Lord in arcā as "worthy of worship, enduring all, the worshiped Self whose condition is wholly dependent on the worshiper." He tolerates all offenses and bestows sanctifying grace; therefore "joined with goodness" can qualify all arcā forms. Some attach it only to the "form of enjoyment."

The *vibhava* forms too possessed a special goodness because they could be seen with ordinary eyes in a human-like manner. Because such historical manifestations are accessible especially to those possessing accumulated merit, they can be called "forms of merit." Others restrict that phrase to the most eminent *vibhavas*, Rāma and Kṛṣṇa. Still others say the incarnations are "forms of merit" because, through their own conduct, they established dharma. The expression "form of enjoyment" can denote those manifestations whose very fruit is the enjoyment of the Lord.

"Countless forms" includes, in accordance with the Gītā's "As people approach Me, so do I respond to them," all forms such as Śrīnivāsa, Varada, Raṅganātha, and innumerable others.

The verse can also be interpreted wholly within arcā practice. In a household one may have a single worshipful form; in a public temple there can be distinctions such as the fixed principal image (*mūla-mūrti*), festival image (*utsava-mūrti*), reclining image, and others. A "bhoga-mūrti" is a form such as Bhoga Śrīnivāsa. In worship we offer various kinds of enjoyment: bathing and anointing directly touch the divine body; waving of lamps and similar acts are offered from a distance as ceremonials; food offerings are presented for consumption. Thus there are tactile, honorific, edible, and other modes of enjoyment. Some divine forms receive all these services; some are adorned and brought forth to grant merit to those who behold them; some repose upon a ceremonial couch. The categories may be examined in this fashion.

"Serpent body" specifies the bodily portion of Ādiśeṣa other than the hooded canopy. *Cayanam āy* means "becoming the couch"; *āy* is an absolutive form and may even bear a causative nuance, "making [it] into a couch." "Āga-mūrti" can be read through *yāga*, worship, hence "the form for ritual worship," the arcā-mūrti.

## Pāsuram 18

### Tamil Mūlam

விடத்தவா யொராயிரம் இராயிரம்கண் வெந்தழல்  
விடுத்துவீழ்விலாதபோகம் மிக்கசோதி தொக்கசீர்  
தொடுத்துமேல் விதானமாய பௌவநீ ரராவணை  
படுத்தபாயல் பள்ளிகொள்வ தென்கொல் வேலைவண்ண்னே!

### IAST Transliteration

*viṭattavā yorāyiram irāyiramkaṇ ventaḻal*  
*viṭuttuvīḻvilātapōkam mikkacōti tokkacīr*  
*toṭuttumēl vitāṉamāya pauvanī rarāvaṇai*  
*paṭuttapāyal paḷḷikoḷva teṉkol vēlaivaṇṇṉē!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord dark as the ocean! Above You, from a thousand poison-bearing mouths and two thousand eyes, hot flames pour forth; the body that does not fall below as the couch is arranged with masses of splendor and brilliant jewels, forming a canopy. Why do You choose to recline upon the spread-out bed of that serpent-couch in the ocean waters?

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār had just marveled that the Lord who reclines upon the serpent in the Milk Ocean becomes the many arcā forms. Now, when he examines the very manner of the serpent-couch itself, he finds that equally astonishing and turns to it directly.

**Word-by-word gloss.** "O ocean-colored One" - O Lord with the dark hue of the sea. "Above" - high over You as You recline. "A thousand venomous mouths" and "two thousand eyes" - the mouths and eyes of Ādiśeṣa. "Releasing fierce flame" - emitting hot fire. "The unfailing body" - the portion of the serpent's body not lying below as the couch. "With abundant radiance" - adorned with highly luminous jewels. "Accumulated beauty" - beauty densely arranged in a cluster. "Joined and made into a canopy" - the hoods spread above as a ceiling. "The serpent-couch." "In the waters of the ocean." "The bed spread out" - the lower bodily portion stretched as a mattress. "Why do You lie there?" - what astonishing reason is there for this repose?

You hear the pleas of the immortals and resolve to descend in order to free them from danger. Yet why do You Yourself lie in the middle of the Milk Ocean while Your bed, Ādiśeṣa, pours venom and flame from a thousand mouths and two thousand eyes? If You recline upon the serpent in the cool Milk Ocean to soothe the heat of Your divine body, which suffers out of compassion on seeing the distress of the jīvas, how can You endure a couch that emits poison and fire from so many mouths? Is the cool Milk Ocean a remedy for the heat of the flame, or is the flame somehow a remedy for the coldness of the Milk Ocean?

What danger could threaten You, the omnipotent giver of fearlessness? Why require the fortress of the Milk Ocean? Why must Ādiśeṣa use blazing fire to keep enemies such as demons and rākṣasas from approaching? Why lie as though asleep at all? The Āḻvār marvels at all these apparent contradictions. He also delights in the striking contrast between the Lord's dark divine body and the whiteness of the Milk Ocean around it.

"Unfailing enjoyment/body" could in another context mean one whose enjoyment of Bhagavān never ceases. Here, however, because the phrase is connected with the formation of the canopy, it is more fitting to understand *bhogam* as the serpent's body. That sense of *bhoga* is well known. "Abundant radiance" refers to the jewels on his heads. The radiant gems flash so as to dispel darkness, and the foreheads marked with clustered designs shine together; this dense ornamental beauty is "accumulated splendor." The ornamental mark can be understood as the *svastika*-like marking on the hood.

Some take "becoming a canopy" with an ellipsis of case relation and construe it as "above the hoods that became the canopy." But because Ādiśeṣa with one portion of his body becomes the couch and with another the canopy, it is quite natural simply to read "the serpent who became the canopy." Similar direct syntactic relations should be preferred where possible.

The verbal participles "releasing" and "arranging" both culminate in the final action of spreading out the couch. "O ocean-colored One" may mean not only "One with the color of the sea" but also "One whose very nature is ocean-like in depth and gravity." In that sense the Āḻvār says: how could someone like me ever understand the intention of One whose nature is as unfathomable as the ocean?

## Pāsuram 19

### Tamil Mūlam

புள்ளதாகி வேதநான்குமோதினாய் அதன்றியும்  
புள்ளின்வாய் பிளந்துபுட் கொடிப்பிடித்தி(த)பின்னரும்  
புள்ளையூர்தி ஆதலாலதென்கொல் மின்கொள்நேமியாய்  
புள்ளின்மெய்ப் பகைக்கடல் கிடைத்தல்காதலித்ததே.

### IAST Transliteration

*puḷḷatāki vētanāṉkumōtiṉāy ataṉṟiyum*  
*puḷḷiṉvāy piḷantupuṭ koṭippiṭitti(ta)piṉṉarum*  
*puḷḷaiyūrti ātalālateṉkol miṉkoḷnēmiyāy*  
*puḷḷiṉmeyp pakaikkaṭal kiṭaittalkātalittatē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O wielder of the radiant discus! You became that very bird, the Swan, and taught the four Vedas. Beyond that, though You split open the beak of the bird Bakāsura, You take the bird Garuḍa as Your banner, and thereafter make that bird Your vehicle. Since You show such affection for birds, why did You delight in reclining in the ocean upon the serpent, the bird's true enemy?

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār marvels once more, in another fashion, at the Lord's reclining upon the serpent.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “O wielder of the radiant discus!” — O One who possesses the divine discus filled with splendor. “Becoming that bird” — incarnating as that bird, the Swan. “You taught the four Vedas” — You instructed Brahmā in all four Vedas. “And not only that”: “the bird’s mouth” — the beak of the bird Bakāsura; “splitting it” — tearing it into two and destroying him. Yet “You hold a bird as Your banner” — You take Garuḍa himself as Your standard. “And afterwards,” furthermore, “the bird as Your vehicle” — You make Garuḍa Your mount. “Therefore,” when You possess such affection for a bird, “why is that?” — why is there something seemingly contrary to it? Namely, “toward the bird’s true enemy,” the serpent, “reclining in the ocean”: why did You become exceedingly fond of lying upon him in the ocean?

You did not remain one thing while the bird was another; You actually incarnated as a bird and taught all the Vedas to Brahmā. Thus You, the embodiment of the Veda, made a bird that too is identified with the Veda Your own banner. It is true that, despite such attachment to birds, You once split a bird’s beak. Let that instance stand aside. In general Your special affection for the bird is clear, for You also delighted in making Garuḍa Your vehicle. Why then, with all this, did You show such great love for the bird’s enemy and lie upon him in the ocean? Will You say that the serpent is not really his enemy? No: he is indeed described as the bird’s “true enemy,” as though enmity itself had taken bodily form. This is a poetic, relish-filled utterance (*rasokti*).

The Āḻvār’s deeper intent is this. You make Your whereabouts known at all times to Brahmā and the other gods and to those in this world by taking as Your banner Garuḍa, who flies at immense height; You stand bearing the divine discus in Your hand; You incarnate in forms such as the Haṃsa to give instruction; and You incarnate with astonishing strength of arm to destroy the wicked. What benefit, then, did You intend by reclining on the king of serpents in the Milk Ocean? Can fatigue incurred by one divine body be relieved by another divine body that lies down elsewhere? Indeed, can fatigue arise for You at all? Does reclining in the middle of the ocean itself confer some benefit on the subjects of the world? Who can even enjoy the sight of You there, when the immortals themselves cannot easily approach You? The pāsuram marvels through questions such as these.

In the expression *atanriyum*, the conjunctive particle *-um* is to be connected with “having split.” Some great teachers have stated that the preferable reading is *piṭitti* (“You hold”), and that reading deserves acceptance. Even if the text is read *piṭitta*, it can mean “even after having taken [him as Your banner].” The point is: not content merely to make Garuḍa Your standard, You also made him Your vehicle so that You might hasten swiftly to bestow grace.

“Meyppakai” means one who is genuinely an enemy. Since both Periya Tiruvaḍi, Garuḍa, and Ādiśeṣa are Nityasūris, one might doubt whether real hostility can exist between them; “true enemy” forestalls that doubt at the level of their assumed bodily species: once one appears as a bird, why should there not be the conventional hostility between bird and serpent? In reality there is no enmity between those Nityasūris. The inner sense is that the *mey*, the bodily form, merely appears in the guise of an enemy.

When did the Haṃsa incarnation occur? Just as the Lord assumed the form of Hayagrīva in order to destroy Madhu and Kaiṭabha and restore the Vedas to Brahmā, so, when an asura named Gomukha had stolen those Vedas from Brahmā, the Lord incarnated as a Fish, recovered them, then appeared as a Swan and, possessing the capacity symbolized by the swan to separate essence from what is non-essential, taught them in a beautiful voice. This account may also be seen in places such as Periyāḻvār Tirumoḻi 1.9.10.

## Pāsuram 20

### Tamil Mūlam

கூசமொன்று மின்றிமாசுணம்படுத்து வேலைநீர்  
பேசநின்ற தேவர்வந்து பாடமுன் கிடந்ததும்  
பாசநின்ற நீரில்வாழும் ஆமையான கேசவா!  
ஏசநின்று நீகிடந்த வாறுகூறு தேறவே.

### IAST Transliteration

*kūcamoṉṟu miṉṟimācuṇampaṭuttu vēlainīr*  
*pēcaniṉṟa tēvarvantu pāṭamuṉ kiṭantatum*  
*pācaniṉṟa nīrilvāḻum āmaiyāṉa kēcavā!*  
*ēcaniṉṟu nīkiṭanta vāṟukūṟu tēṟavē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Keśava, who became the turtle that lives in the waters it loves! Without the slightest hesitation, You once spread the excellent serpent in the waters of the ocean and reclined there while the gods, celebrated by the world as its lords, came and sang Your praise. But in that turtle-incarnation You lay in a way that invited ridicule. Tell me how You lay so, that I may understand it clearly.

### English Translation of the Commentary

Let it be that He reclined there in such majesty that all the gods came and praised Him. The Āḻvār now marvels instead: why did He assume a lowly birth and lie beneath the mountain as a turtle?

**Word-by-word gloss.** “O Keśava who became a turtle” — O Lord of Brahmā and Rudra, who incarnated as the Kūrma. “Living in the water that is beloved” — dwelling within the waters with attachment to them. “Without even a little shrinking or embarrassment.” “The ocean-water”; “the excellent serpent” — the noble serpent Ādiśeṣa; “spreading him out.” “The gods who stand so that the world speaks of them [as lords]” — Brahmā, Rudra, and the others who are celebrated as rulers of the worlds; “coming and singing” — approaching the shore and praising You. “You formerly lay” — You reclined there in the Milk Ocean. “But then,” in the Kūrma incarnation, “in a manner fit to be mocked” — lying in a way that all could deride. “Tell me the manner in which You lay, so that I may understand it clearly.”

“Without any hesitation” admits several shades of relish. You had no fear because You knew that no deceitful asura, however magical, could do anything to You, the supreme master of māyā. You had no concern that lying upon a serpent who emits poison and flame could injure You. You did not shrink from sleeping for however long in cold water. Your divine body is so delicate that even the touch of the flower-like hand of Lakṣmī can make it seem to wilt, yet You did not hesitate to let the roaring ocean’s waves and spray strike it. Nor did You feel any reluctance at abandoning immersion in the supreme bliss of Paramapada and floating in this little pool of an ocean within the realm of prakṛti. The phrase “without shrinking” may be carried syntactically forward and connected with each successive action.

“Pēca ninra” may be understood as in expressions like “Śiva who stands so as to be spoken of,” that is, those gods whose eminence the world proclaims. Earlier teachers also give another construction: “while the ocean-water spoke,” i.e. while the sea roared. On that reading, the roar of the ocean and the praise of the gods sound together, and the Lord is shown lying there with affection even for the sea itself.

The conjunctive *-um* in “having lain” normally calls for another corresponding *-um*. Accordingly some supply it mentally in “the manner in which [You also] lay.” Or one may understand the idiom as one does in a sentence such as “as soon as he went, he returned.” Then the sense is: no sooner had You lain in a way that all the great ones praised than You subsequently lay in a contrary way that made You liable to ridicule.

“Pāca ninra” may be construed as “in waters that became the object of Your affection, even more than Your Paramapada.” Alternatively it may qualify the turtle: “the turtle that became an object of special affection to me.” Another explanation takes *pāśa* literally as the noose, the weapon of Varuṇa, the presiding deity of water: “in the water where the noose-bearing Varuṇa abides.”

“O Keśava who became a turtle”: even if that particular turtle-form is no longer before me now, You who once became that turtle are here; therefore tell me what it meant. It is fitting enough that You reclined while Brahmā and the others bowed and praised You, without any diminution of Your transcendence. But a birth as a turtle, a creature unable to remain apart from water and apparently regarding life in water as its highest good, and Your lying in that condition, are outwardly matters that anyone might mock. “What is the meaning of this?” asks the Āḻvār. Compare the reference to the sleeping turtle in pāsuram 14.

Even when You came into the Milk Ocean and became supremely accessible, Your *saulabhya* was manifest but Your *sauśīlya*, Your gracious mingling with the lowly, had not yet appeared in its fullest brilliance. Therefore You were born as a turtle, went underneath wherever the churning mountain Mandara was ordered to move, supported it from below, and even delighted to be commanded and directed in that fashion by gods and asuras alike. The pāsuram is to be savored with this deeper intention.

## Pāsuram 21

### Tamil Mūlam

அரங்கனே! தரங்கநீர் கலங்கவன்று குன்று சூழ்  
மரங்கள் தேய மாநிலம் குலுங்கமா சுணம்சுலாய்  
நெருங்கநீ கடைந்தபோது நின்றசூர ரென்செய்தார்  
குரங்கையா ளுகந்த வெந்தை! கூறுதேற வேறிதே.

### IAST Transliteration

*araṅkaṉē! taraṅkanīr kalaṅkavaṉṟu kuṉṟu cūḻ*  
*maraṅkaḷ tēya mānilam kuluṅkamā cuṇamculāy*  
*neruṅkanī kaṭaintapōtu niṉṟacūra reṉceytār*  
*kuraṅkaiyā ḷukanta ventai! kūṟutēṟa vēṟitē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Raṅganātha! O my Father who delighted to take the monkey host as Your army! On that day, when You wound the serpent tightly around the mountain, so that the billowing ocean churned, the trees around the mountain were ground away, and the great earth itself trembled—when it was You who churned, what did those heroes, the gods and asuras who stood there, actually do? Tell me distinctly, so that I may understand. This gracious manner of Yours is indeed extraordinary!

### English Translation of the Commentary

The preceding Kūrma episode already suggests this truth: the work was really Yours, yet through Your extraordinary *sauśīlya* You arranged that the glory should accrue to those who had sought Your help. Recollecting the Rāma incarnation, in which the same gracious trait shone forth, the Āḻvār becomes delighted.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “O Raṅganātha!” — O Lord of Śrīraṅgam. “O my Father who delighted to rule/take the monkeys” — O my Lord who lovingly accepted the vānaras as Your army. “On that day,” when the gods prayed to You, “the excellent serpent,” Vāsuki, “winding him tightly around” — wrapping him firmly as the churning-rope; “the water with waves being agitated” — so that the ocean was churned into confusion; “the trees surrounding the mountain being worn away” — all the trees around Mandara being ground down; “the great earth trembling” — shaken by the force of the churning. “When You churned” — when You Yourself were the actual churner of the ocean — “what did the heroes who stood there do?” What, in truth, did the gods and asuras, the supposedly heroic participants, accomplish? “Tell me, so that I may understand.” “This is different indeed” — this gracious mode of Yours is singular and extraordinary.

In the previous pāsuram the Āḻvār called “Keśava!” and asked a question, but no reply came. He may then have reflected: “The Lord of Brahmā and Rudra is somewhere with that transcendent form; even if He answers, the sound may not reach my ears. Yet the very One who reclines in the Milk Ocean now reclines here before me as Raṅganātha. I shall ask Him directly here, and ask at greater length.” Hence the address “O Raṅganātha!”

It was You who placed Mandara on Garuḍa and brought the mountain to the ocean. It was You who wound the serpent around it. It was You who supplied such strength that neither the mountain nor Vāsuki would perish before the task was finished. Could the gods and asuras themselves really have churned with instruments possessing such power? Without You the mountain would not even have moved and no waves would have risen. Therefore You Yourself went beneath as the Tortoise and supported the mountain; above it You assumed an immense form and pressed it down; You entered into the bodies of the gods and asuras and empowered them; and, at the final point, You directly seized the churning-rope with Your own divine hands, churned vigorously, and brought forth the nectar. Those “heroes” merely stood there and witnessed it. Yet who received the fame? The story spread as though the gods had churned the ocean and as though they were the heroes. That was Your grace.

The Rāma incarnation offers the same example. In Janasthāna You destroyed Khara and the fourteen thousand rākṣasas with him in half a *muhūrta*. What monkey assisted You then? Later, because the monkeys wished to become Your army in gratitude for the immense benefit You conferred through their search for Sītā, You assigned them a great share in the battle and thereby glorified them. You even said in effect, “O lord of the monkey-host, if I wished, I could slay all of them with the tip of My finger.” The intent is: “I require no one. I alone can accomplish the entire destruction with the tip of My finger. Even now, if you wish, you may return with your army to Kiṣkindhā. Or enter the enemy army with this entire host and oppose it; I am capable of destroying them all even with you included.” Such was Your own power. Yet You honored those who had attached themselves to You, because Your great quality is to care not whether Your own independent glory seems diminished, so long as greatness comes to those who have sought refuge in You. The Āḻvār savors precisely this.

Some also construe “tell me—different indeed” as “tell me this in a specially distinct and detailed way.”

## Pāsuram 22

### Tamil Mūlam

பண்டுமின்று மேலுமாயோர் பாலனாகி ஞாலமேழும்  
உண்டுமண்டி யாலிலைத் துயின்ற வாதிதேவனே!  
வண்டுகிண்டு தண்டுழா யலங்கலாய்! கலந்தசீர்ப்  
புண்டரீக பாவைசேரு மார்ப! பூமிநாதனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*paṇṭumiṉṟu mēlumāyōr pālaṉāki ñālamēḻum*  
*uṇṭumaṇṭi yālilait tuyiṉṟa vātitēvaṉē!*  
*vaṇṭukiṇṭu taṇṭuḻā yalaṅkalāy! kalantacīrp*  
*puṇṭarīka pāvaicēru mārpa! pūminātaṉē!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Primal Deity, existing in the past, present, and future, who became a little infant, eagerly swallowed all seven worlds, and slept upon a banyan leaf! O wearer of a cool garland of holy basil into which bees burrow for honey! O One whose auspicious excellences are perfectly joined! O Lord whose chest is the abiding home of the lotus-maiden! O Lord of Bhūmi!

### English Translation of the Commentary

Is it merely a matter of protecting a few particular beings when they ask? We see that He protects all seven worlds as though they were His own possession. The Āḻvār celebrates that universal tenderness.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “In the past and now” — in past and present time; “and above/beyond,” that is, in future time also, ever-existing. “Becoming one little child.” “All seven worlds,” “eagerly swallowing” — taking them joyfully into His stomach. “Upon a banyan leaf,” “sleeping” — graciously entering yogic sleep. “O Primal Deity!” — O cause of the universe. “A cool holy-basil garland into which bees burrow” — a fresh and cooling garland of *tulasī* whose honey the bees pierce into the flowers to drink. “O One adorned with that garland!” “Joined excellences” — One possessing auspicious qualities perfectly consonant with You. “O chest sought and inhabited by the lotus-maiden” — O One whose divine chest is the permanent dwelling of Śrī, who lives on the lotus. “O Lord of Bhūmi!” — O Lord also of Bhūdevī. Everywhere Your love overflows.

At every subordinate dissolution that occurs during Brahmā’s night, You become the infant on the banyan leaf and preserve all the worlds within Your divine stomach. The form that is eternally Yours thus appears at that time as a tiny baby. Some explain the verbal form “being” as “being the protector”: because You are the protector at all times, You assume this form.

Each feature is an object of wonder. The immeasurably great form becomes tiny. Within one portion of that tiny form the seven worlds are swallowed and kept exactly as they are, so that they may be protected. A form carrying so vast a weight is itself supported by a tender banyan shoot floating on the flood of dissolution. And at that moment You sleep upon it without the least fear or anxiety. O Lord! Your play alone deserves the name “play.” Even in that condition the bees enter and move about in the *tulasī* garland that adorns You, and this itself becomes an object of delight to You. Is it not precisely so that this tenderness (*vātsalya*) may grow and overflow that the divine Consorts remain united with You?

The word *alaṅgal* can mean either petals/leaves or a garland. In an earlier expression, “tulasī-alaṅgal garland,” because the separate word “garland” was present, the elders took *alaṅgal* there as “leaves/petals.” Here it itself may mean the garland.

“Joined excellences”: *cīr* means auspicious qualities; their “being joined” means that they coexist in perfect mutual harmony. An older explanation instead takes the phrase as describing Śrī: “she whose nature is to remain forever inseparably united with the Lord.” Some understand it as “joined with beauty.”

The pāsuram comes to an end simply by addressing the Lord in many ways. The Āḻvār has plunged so deeply into the experience of the Lord’s *vātsalya* that he can no longer articulate what is in his heart. Bhaktisāra merely gives one hint of it in the middle through the word *maṇṭi*, “eagerly, impetuously,” describing the Lord’s swallowing of all the worlds, and then falls silent in immersion.

## Pāsuram 23

### Tamil Mūlam

வானிறத்தோர்சீயமாய் வளைந்தவாளெயிற்றவன்  
ஊனிறத்துகிர்த்தலம் அழுத்தினாய்உலாயசீர்  
நானிறத்தவேதநாவர் நல்லயோகினால்வணங்கு  
பானிறக்கடற்கிடந்த பற்பநாபனல்லையே.

### IAST Transliteration

*vāṉiṟattōrcīyamāy vaḷaintavāḷeyiṟṟavaṉ*  
*ūṉiṟattukirttalam aḻuttiṉāyulāyacīr*  
*nāṉiṟattavētanāvar nallayōkiṉālvaṇaṅku*  
*pāṉiṟakkaṭaṟkiṭanta paṟpanāpaṉallaiyē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You became the incomparable Lion of immense splendor and pressed the whole surface of Your claws into the flesh-filled chest of the curved, sword-bright-fanged Hiraṇya. Are You not that Padmanābha who reclines in the milk-white ocean, worshiped by those whose tongues bear the fourfold Veda of far-spreading glory through the excellent means of true yoga?

### English Translation of the Commentary

Greater even than the tenderness He showed toward the seven worlds was the tenderness He bore toward a single child born in an asura family. Without that child even asking Him, He assumed a supremely wondrous form in order to remove the child’s enemy and protect him. The Āḻvār marvels at this.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Of vast hue/splendor,” “becoming one unique lion” — assuming the incomparable Narasiṃha form. “The curved, sword-bright teeth” — the shining fangs of Hiraṇyāsura. “In the flesh-filled chest” of that asura, “the surface of the claws,” all of Your nails, “You pressed in.” “Of glory spread everywhere”; “those whose tongues bear the fourfold Veda”; “by excellent yoga” — by the superior means; “worshiped.” “The milk-colored ocean” — the ocean possessing both milk and greatness; “reclining there.” “Padmanābha” — the Supreme Lord whose navel bears the lotus from which four-faced Brahmā arises. “Are You not that very One?”

Some divide *vāṉiṟattu* as *vāl-niṟattu*, “of a white color,” taking *vāl* to mean whiteness. How can the cloud-dark Lord become white? They answer that just as He changed His form into that of a lion, He changed His color as well. If scriptural authority were produced for a lion’s color being white, this explanation could be admitted. Yet there is no need to prefer that division. Just as *ūṉ-niṟattu* in the second line means “in the flesh-filled [place],” so *vāṉ-niṟattu* can be divided naturally. *Vāṉ* can mean greatness: “of a majestic/divine character.” If the human portion of Narasiṃha is cloud-colored, *vāṉ* may even carry its familiar sense “cloud.” If *niṟam* means radiance, the expression gives “shining with immense brilliance.”

In the second line some explain *niṟam* in “ūṉ-niṟattu” as a vital region. Since *niṟam* can also mean the chest, and the tearing of the asura’s breast is celebrated, it is simpler to understand “in the flesh-filled chest.” By the same possibility, *niṟam* in the first line could refer to Narasiṃha’s divine chest. In “the surface of the claws,” *talam* means the inner or broad area; the word indicates that the nails were extended and spread wide.

“Far-spreading glory” may be connected with “Veda,” with “those whose tongues bear the Veda,” or with “Padmanābha.” The “fourfold character of the Veda” has several possible explanations. The elders say it may denote the four accents: *udātta*, *anudātta*, *svarita*, and *pracaya*. *Pracaya* is described as a special form of *udātta* occurring after a *svarita* syllable. Or the four are simply Ṛg, Yajus, Sāman, and Atharvan. These four have distinct functions corresponding respectively to the Hotṛ, Adhvaryu, Udgātṛ, and Brahman priests who assist the sacrificer. Again, the fourfold division may be injunction (*vidhi*), prohibition (*niṣedha*), explanatory praise or censure (*arthavāda*), and mantra; or mantra, Brāhmaṇa, Āraṇyaka, and Upaniṣad. It can also refer to the four human aims—dharma, artha, kāma, and mokṣa—or to the four classes mentioned in the Gītā, “the distressed, the seeker of knowledge, the seeker of wealth, and the knower.” Other fourfold schemes too are possible. The broader implication is that He who bears tenderness toward all grants every legitimate human goal to those who seek Him.

“By yoga”: *yōku* is *yoga*, here meaning the means or discipline. “Good yoga” is the true means uncontaminated by connection with other deities: devotion to Bhagavān and surrender (*bhakti* and *prapatti*). The third line indicates that the destruction of Hiraṇyāsura was carried out as a subsidiary act for the protection of the Veda-bearing worshipers. The fourth line simply recalls the same reclining manifestation treated in the preceding pāsurams.

## Pāsuram 24

### Tamil Mūlam

கங்கைநீர்பயந்தபாத பங்கயத்தெம்மண்ணலே!  
அங்கையாழி சங்குதண்டு வில்லும்வாளுமேந்தினாய்!  
சிங்கமாயதேவதேவ! தேனுலாவுமென்மலர்  
மங்கைமன்னிவாழுமார்ப வாழிமேனிமாயனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*kaṅkainīrpayantapāta paṅkayattemmaṇṇalē!*  
*aṅkaiyāḻi caṅkutaṇṭu villumvāḷumēntiṉāy!*  
*ciṅkamāyatēvatēva! tēṉulāvumeṉmalar*  
*maṅkaimaṉṉivāḻumārpa vāḻimēṉimāyaṉē!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O our Lord whose lotus-feet brought forth the waters of Gaṅgā! You bear in Your beautiful hands the discus and conch, the mace, the bow, and the sword. O God of gods who became the Lion! O Lord whose chest is the permanent dwelling of the Maiden of the delicate honey-haunted lotus! O wondrous One whose body is dark like the ocean!

### English Translation of the Commentary

The following pāsurams turn to the Vāmana incarnation. This pāsuram, however, deliberately brings Vāmana/Trivikrama and Narasiṃha together so that their flavors may be enjoyed in conjunction.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “The waters of Gaṅgā brought forth” — the divine feet that produced the Gaṅgā-tīrtha, or that produced the sacred water for Gaṅgā herself. “Lotus-feet.” “O our Lord!” — O our Master. “In the beautiful hands” You bear “the discus and conch,” “the mace” called Kaumodakī, and “the bow and sword,” Śārṅga and Nandaka. “O God of gods who became the Lion” — O Supreme Deity who assumed the Narasiṃha form. “Honey roaming” — bees moving about in quest of nectar; “the delicate flower” — the soft lotus; “the Maiden,” the great Goddess Śrī who shines upon that lotus; “abiding,” “living” — O One whose divine chest is the place where she permanently dwells. “O wondrous One with an ocean-like body!” — O astonishing Lord whose divine body is dark like the sea.

This pāsuram may be construed together with the following one. Otherwise, at “O chest where she lives—may [Your body] flourish,” divide *vāḻi* as the benedictive “may it live/flourish,” and complete the sentence: “O wondrous Lord, may Your body flourish!” The first half concerns Vāmana’s growth into Trivikrama; the latter half repeats the Narasiṃha incarnation mentioned earlier, precisely in order to relish the two incarnations together. The next pāsuram makes this linkage clearer.

“Brought forth the Gaṅgā-water.” In the Trivikrama incarnation Brahmā poured water in worship upon the Lord’s divine foot, and that water flowed as Gaṅgā. Thus one may say the foot “brought forth the water of Gaṅgā.” One may also construe it as “brought forth water for Gaṅgā.” When Brahmā’s ritual water was not sufficient for the immeasurably extended foot, Dharma-devatā is said to have flowed as additional holy water; and when the nail-tip of the great toe of Viṣṇu’s foot pierced the shell of the cosmic egg, the water of the outer cosmic covering entered through that opening and greatly swelled the Gaṅgā.

“O our Lord!” Without anyone’s prayer, merely because all beings happened to be subjects of the supremely generous Mahābali, He disregarded high and low, touched every sentient being with His divine feet, and manifested His lordship over them all. Praising this, the Āḻvār says, “You are the Master of all of us.” As the Lord grew to cosmic dimensions, His five weapons too grew proportionately, beautifying His divine hands; this underlies “in the beautiful hands, discus…” The same wonder is expressed in Nammāḻvār’s lines such as “the great, long battle-weapons” (Tiruvāymoḻi 1.10.1) and “the discus rose, the conch and bow rose, the cosmic sphere opened… as He measured the worlds” (Tiruvāymoḻi 7.4.1).

A question may arise: how could five weapons be borne in four hands? Perhaps to suggest the answer, the coordinating particle *-um* is attached only to “bow” and “sword”: those two may be understood as borne together in one divine hand. This interpretive principle may be retained in some later pāsurams as well.
