Because Vāmana appeared as a brahmacārin, one should not imagine that He was thereby separated from Śrī; nor, because Prahlāda approached Narasiṃha before the Goddess did, should one suppose that Śrī was absent from that incarnation. Hence the pāsuram declares through “the Maiden…abiding on the chest” the inseparable union of the Goddess who says, in the celebrated phrase, “I will never leave.” “Ocean-like body” may be common to both manifestations. As noted above, because of the alliteration with *vāḻum-mārba*, one may alternatively divide the next phrase as *vāḻi mēṉi*, “may the divine body flourish.”

## Pāsuram 25

### Tamil Mūlam

வரத்தினிற் சிரத்தை மிக்க வாளெயிற்று மற்றவன்  
உரத்தினிற் கரத்தைவைத்துகிர்த்தலத்தை யூன்றினாய்  
இரத்திநீ யிதென்னபொய் இரந்தமண் வயிற்றுளே  
கரத்தியுன் கருத்தையாவர் காணவல்லர் கண்ணனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*varattiṉiṟ cirattai mikka vāḷeyiṟṟu maṟṟavaṉ*  
*urattiṉiṟ karattaivaittukirttalattai yūṉṟiṉāy*  
*irattinī yiteṉṉapoy irantamaṇ vayiṟṟuḷē*  
*karattiyuṉ karuttaiyāvar kāṇavallar kaṇṇaṉē!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You pressed Your hand upon the chest of that other one—Hiraṇya, with sword-bright teeth, greatly confident in the boon he had obtained—and drove the full surface of Your nails into him. You who are such a One then beg! What is this—truth or deception? And the very earth You begged for, You hide within Your own belly at dissolution. O Kṛṣṇa! Who can fathom Your intention?

### English Translation of the Commentary

**Word-by-word gloss.** “In the boon” — in the boon obtained from Brahmā; “greatly firm in confidence” — placing immense trust in it; “with sword-bright teeth”; “that other one” — the hostile Hiraṇya. “Upon his chest,” “placing Your hand,” and there “the surface of Your nails,” “You drove in.” “You,” such a mighty One, “beg” — You ask Mahābali for land. “What is this? Is this falsehood/deception?” “The earth that You begged and received,” at the time of dissolution “within Your very belly,” “You conceal.” “O Kṛṣṇa!” — O Śrī Kṛṣṇa, vessel of all these wonders. “Your intention—who can see it?” Who can understand what is in Your divine mind?

“The boon” was the famous boon that Hiraṇya should not be destroyed by day or by night, by any species, by any weapon, and under the several other stipulated conditions. “Greatly confident”: the Supreme Lord is the father even of Brahmā, omnipotent and true in resolve; He can overcome even a boon granted by Brahmā. Yet Hiraṇya failed to understand Brahmā’s limited power and, relying firmly on that boon, opposed the Supreme.

“That other one” means one unlike Prahlāda, not belonging to the class of those who have sought refuge—the one who stands apart from the Lord’s dependents. The word “other” may also be taken as an indeclinable filler. Another possibility is a division yielding “one possessed of great strength and austerity,” or “one whose austerity itself was powerful.”

“Placing Your hand”: though outwardly Hiraṇya fought with great ferocity, perhaps there might still have been fear hidden in his heart. The Lord as though tested him by placing His hand upon his breast. Discovering that he was not frightened even then, He next drove in His nails. Such is the dramatic relish of the wording.

“You beg.” If You could destroy a Hiraṇya such as this, what is Mahābali to You? Why did You stretch out Your hand like a destitute beggar before him? Whose property was the earth in the first place? Should He whose chest is the permanent residence of the lotus-Goddess become a mendicant?

“The earth You begged…” Why did You stand as a dwarf-student, apparently casting aside Your dignity, and beg for the land? If the answer is that You desired to live upon the earth, You later placed that very earth inside Your stomach. Did You beg for earth merely in order to put it into Your mouth? More fundamentally, when You are capable of hiding the whole earth within Your belly, why should You need to beg at all? The One who tore Hiraṇya certainly cannot be unable to conquer Mahābali. Nor did the earth become Mahābali’s property merely because You had previously protected him by keeping him within Your own stomach. Or did You, who make even Yourself subordinate to Your devotees, lovingly regard Your own earth as Mahābali’s property? Your transcendent majesty toward opponents and Your astonishing humility toward those who stand before You in reverence are both beyond description.

## Pāsuram 26

### Tamil Mūlam

ஆணினோடு பெண்ணுமாகி அல்லவோடு நல்லவாய்  
ஊணொடோசை யூறுமாகி யொன்றலாத மாயையாய்  
பூணிபேணு மாயனாகிப் பொய்யினோடு மெய்யுமாய்  
காணிபேணு மாணியாய்க் கரந்து சென்ற கள்வனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*āṇiṉōṭu peṇṇumāki allavōṭu nallavāy*  
*ūṇoṭōcai yūṟumāki yoṉṟalāta māyaiyāy*  
*pūṇipēṇu māyaṉākip poyyiṉōṭu meyyumāy*  
*kāṇipēṇu māṇiyāyk karantu ceṉṟa kaḷvaṉē!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

Becoming male together with female, becoming what is not good together with what is good, becoming taste, sound, and touch, and becoming the primordial māyā that is not any single evolved thing; becoming the cowherd who lovingly tends the cattle, becoming both false and true; becoming the brahmacārin who desired a plot of land, You went concealing Yourself—O thief!

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār thinks: when You appeared as the dwarf-student and concealed who You were, this was only another instance of the way You hide Yourself within all the entities of the universe. Such concealment is natural to You.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Becoming male together with female” — being all the males and females in the world. “Together with what is not good, becoming what is good” — being both bad and good things. “Together with food/taste, sound, and touch” — being the sense-objects such as taste, sound, and tactile quality. “Becoming māyā that is not one [evolved] thing” — also being the primordial Prakṛti which, in that condition, has not evolved into any particular effect. “Tending with affection,” “becoming a cowherd” — becoming the herdsman who loves and protects the cows. “Becoming both false and true.” “Desiring a plot of land,” “becoming a brahmacārin” — the young religious student who sought the three paces of earth. “Going while concealed” — going to Mahābali while hiding Your true greatness. “O thief!” — O wondrous deceiver.

This pāsuram is to be syntactically connected with the preceding one. Pervading everywhere, You have as though hidden Yourself by means of the very entities in which You dwell. The learned, whenever they see anything, primarily perceive You and see that particular entity only as something dependent upon You. Those who do not possess that capacity see merely the object and fail to see You. Was Your concealment in the body of the dwarf only like this cosmic concealment? The worldly bodies are material, while the dwarf-form is non-material and divine. But those who can recognize You even within a material form will hardly fail to recognize You in a non-material divine form. They recognized You even when You became the cowherd Kṛṣṇa. This is one line of interpretation.

Alternatively, there is a deliberately Kṛṣṇa-centered reading. You were born as a male child, Kṛṣṇa, and at the same time You were born in Yaśodā’s house as a female child. Everything connected with You became good for some and the opposite for others. Your touch, Your speech, and the nectar-like flavor of Your coral-red lips became objects of supreme enjoyment. You also appeared as an incomprehensible *māyā*, manifesting and disappearing. Though born as a kṣatriya, You became a cowherd. If anyone asked, “Are You not Devakī’s son?” You could reply, “No.” In one sense that would be false; yet when one reflects that in truth no one can literally be the father or mother of the unborn Supreme, it is also true. The pāsuram thus plays on how difficult it is to determine the truth of Your manifestations.

Some explain “what is not [male or female]” as the neuter, thus: male, female, and those who are neither. They also explain “false” and “true” as “false to some, true to others,” recalling that the Lord makes Himself fully real to those who seek Him and seemingly unavailable to others. Another interpretation is ontological: the entities in the first two lines are forms of the *līlā-vibhūti* and, because material transformations contain defects and perish, they may be called “false”; the cowherd-form, being non-material, faultless, and full of auspicious qualities, is “true.”

## Pāsuram 27

### Tamil Mūlam

விண்கடந்த சோதியாய் விளங்குஞானமூர்த்தியாய்  
பண்கடந்ததேசமேவு பாவநாசநாதனே!  
எண்கடந்தயோகினோ டிரந்துசென்று மாணியாய்  
மண்கடந்த வண்ண! நின்னை யார்மதிக்க வல்லரே.

### IAST Transliteration

*viṇkaṭanta cōtiyāy viḷaṅkuñāṉamūrttiyāy*  
*paṇkaṭantatēcamēvu pāvanācanātaṉē!*  
*eṇkaṭantayōkiṉō ṭirantuceṉṟu māṇiyāy*  
*maṇkaṭanta vaṇṇa! niṉṉai yārmatikka vallarē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You are the self-luminous Radiance that transcends Prakṛti, the embodied Lord whose body consists of the conscious selves endowed with manifest knowledge; O Master who dwells in the realm beyond the reach of Vedic melody and destroys the sins of devotees! Though joined with qualities and powers beyond all counting, You became a brahmacārin, went begging, and then strode beyond the worlds. Who can ever measure You by the lowliness of that guise?

### English Translation of the Commentary

Even if You became the “thief” who concealed Yourself as a dwarf desiring a little land, who could truly estimate You by that appearance? Your greatness as Lord of both vibhūtis cannot be hidden.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Transcending the sky” — pervading beyond the unmanifest “space,” namely primordial Prakṛti. “Being Radiance” — being the self-luminous Self. “Manifest knowledge,” “having as Your body” — possessing as Your body the bound, liberated, and eternal selves, who are themselves conscious and self-revealing and possess *dharma-bhūta-jñāna*. “Beyond melody” — beyond what the Vedas, whose recitation is marked by tonal accent, can exhaustively reach. “Dwelling in that realm” — delighting in the Nitya-vibhūti. “Destroyer of sin” — One who removes the sins of devotees. “O Master!” — the Lord who is sought and prayed to. “You,” the Lord of all, “joined with yoga beyond enumeration” — possessing incalculable conjunctions of qualities and powers; “becoming a brahmacārin,” “going and begging” from Mahābali, and “crossing/measuring the earth.” “Who can measure You?” Who can confine or diminish their estimation of You because of this apparently lowly condition? Even when You conceal Yourself, Your greatness cannot be concealed.

“Viṇ,” sky, can denote ordinary ether, heaven, primordial Prakṛti, or Paramapada. Here it denotes Mūlaprakṛti. In the dialogue of Gārgī and Yājñavalkya the Upaniṣad uses *ākāśa* in this higher sense: “in that ākāśa this [universe] is woven lengthwise and crosswise.”

“Manifest knowledge” refers collectively to all jīvas—bound, liberated, and eternal. “Paṇ” is melodic tone, and by extension the Veda whose recitation is governed by *svara*. The Veda indeed teaches Paramapada extensively, but cannot verbally exhaust all its particular excellences; therefore that realm is described as “beyond Vedic melody.”

“Destroyer of sin.” Since He destroys the sins of others, it follows without separate statement that no sin can ever touch Him. “Nātha” means Lord or Master. By the derivation from the root “to seek or supplicate,” it can mean “He who is sought for every desired result”; more highly, “He who is sought as the supreme human end itself.”

“Joined with yoga beyond counting” may simply mean “while possessing this innumerable union of qualities, powers, and vibhūtis.” Or it may mean that, in order to bewilder Mahābali into granting the three paces without refusal despite the many reasons he might have for refusing, the Lord employed countless means and strategies; and that He possessed innumerable devices by which, without killing Mahābali, He could arrange the outcome so as to remain dear to both gods and asuras.

Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai, while giving substantially the same overall meaning, assigns different meanings to several individual expressions. He takes “sky” as Paramapada. “Having a form of manifest knowledge” then refers to the six principal divine attributes signaled by “knowledge”: *jñāna, śakti, bala, aiśvarya, vīrya,* and *tejas*. These six belong to the very nature of the Supreme Self. The *mūrti* is the divine auspicious body fashioned so that those qualities become manifest; the phrase thus means “He who possesses that divine auspicious body.” “A realm beyond melody” may then mean a radiance inaccessible even to the Veda.

## Pāsuram 28

### Tamil Mūlam

படைத்தபாரிடந்தளந்த துண்டுமிழ்ந்து பெளவநீர்  
படைத்தடைத் ததிற்கிடந்து முன்கடைந்தபெற்றியோய்!  
மிடைத்தமாலி மாலிமான் விலங்கு காலனூர் புகப்  
படைக்கலம் விடுத்தபற் படைத்தடக்கைமாயனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*paṭaittapāriṭantaḷanta tuṇṭumiḻntu peḷavanīr*  
*paṭaittaṭait tatiṟkiṭantu muṉkaṭaintapeṟṟiyōy!*  
*miṭaittamāli mālimāṉ vilaṅku kālaṉūr pukap*  
*paṭaikkalam viṭuttapaṟ paṭaittaṭakkaimāyaṉē!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O wondrous Lord of broad hands that bear many weapons! You created the earth, raised it up, measured it, swallowed it, and emitted it again; You created the ocean-waters, dammed them, lay in them before, and churned them. You discharged Your weapons so that the charging rākṣasa Māli and the golden-garlanded deer-form would enter the city of the shining Lord of Death. O One possessed of such astounding deeds!

### English Translation of the Commentary

Having spoken of His incarnations as Lion and as dwarf-student, the Āḻvār now begins to enjoy His incarnation as a human. First he gathers together the Lord’s various deeds in relation to the earth measured as Vāmana and to the ocean, using them to lead into the deeds of the Rāma incarnation. This pāsuram forms a single syntactic movement with the following pāsuram, which explicitly speaks of His human birth.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “The earth You created”; “raising it up” — in the Śrī Varāha incarnation tearing it free from the cosmic wall with Your tusk; “measuring it” — striding over it as Trivikrama; “swallowing it” — keeping it within Your divine stomach at the intermediate dissolution; “emitting it” — bringing it forth at the beginning of creation. “The ocean-water”: “creating” it; “damming” it — building the bridge as Śrī Rāma; “before that, lying in it” — reclining in the Milk Ocean; “the greatness of having churned it” — incarnating as Kūrma for the nectar and churning. “The charging Māli,” the rākṣasa who entered battle and pressed close; “the garlanded/golden deer”; “so that [they] entered the city of the shining Lord of Death” — so that they went to Yama’s city. “Weapons of battle,” “discharging them.” “Many weapons,” “broad hands” — O One whose vast divine hands bear many weapons. “O wondrous One!”

The participle “created” is an adjective on the earth, indicating that creation itself is one deed among the subsequent acts of raising, measuring, swallowing, and emitting it. After serially recounting His acts with respect to the earth previously introduced in connection with Vāmana, the pāsuram says that He has likewise performed many deeds with respect to the ocean-water; this serves to introduce Rāma through the mention of the bridge.

Some say that, because the ocean was created before the earth, “creating the ocean-water” ought logically to be moved to the beginning of the syntactic order. Others distinguish the flood-water of dissolution from the later ocean that was dammed and therefore take the first “ocean-water” as the single mass of dissolution-water. But there is no rule here that earlier deeds must be stated before later deeds; the method is to collect all acts concerning each object together. Therefore it is best to keep “creating and damming the ocean-water” together. The ocean in question may simply be the sea within the Brahmāṇḍa.

The phrase concerning Māli admits several textual analyses. Earlier teachers read it in a way that yielded Māli, Sumāli, and a great animal/deer. Later teachers explain it as Māli; *māli* = Sumāli; and *māṉ-vilaṅgu* = Mārīca in the form of a deer. Another possibility is that *Mālimān* is a phonetic variation of Mālyavān. “Animal” is the generic term and “deer” the specific one.

The historical background is given in the Uttarakāṇḍa of the Śrīmad Rāmāyaṇa. Sumāli, father of Kaikasī (Rāvaṇa’s mother), his younger brother Māli, and elder brother Mālyavān were sons of the rākṣasa Sukeśa. Empowered by austerity, they made Laṅkā, constructed by Viśvakarman, their capital, conquered the gods, and lived proudly as though supreme over all. When, at the gods’ request, Bhagavān Viṣṇu resolved to destroy them, they themselves marched upon Devaloka to humiliate the gods who had provoked the intervention. The fuller account is found in the Uttarakāṇḍa.

When Mālyavān, Sumāli, and Māli were attacked by Viṣṇu, Māli advanced against Him, was struck by many weapons, and was beheaded by the discus. Sumāli’s charioteer was also beheaded, while Sumāli and Mālyavān, severely wounded, abandoned the battle, fled to Laṅkā, and, afraid even to remain there, hid in the netherworld. Since the text therefore cannot straightforwardly say that Sumāli and Mālyavān “entered Yama’s city,” it is preferable here to take only Māli as the rākṣasa who was killed. The second word *māli* can then qualify him as “garlanded.” Because *māl* can also signify delusion, some Tamil scholars suggest “one who causes delusion”; it can equally qualify Mārīca.

The Rāmāyaṇa says of Mārīca, after he was struck by Rāma, that the rākṣasa fell in his own form adorned with gold: “the great-fanged rākṣasa, wearing a golden garland, fell struck by the arrow.” Mārīca is mentioned together with Māli in order to bring the Rāma narrative into the verse, just as “damming” in the preceding line does. The point is to celebrate that the slayer of ancient Māli and the slayer of Mārīca are one and the same Supreme Lord. This episode is also treated near the end of the eighth sarga of the Uttarakāṇḍa.

Pāsuram 107 explicitly names Sumāli; the interpretive issue there should be considered when we reach it. One could posit another occasion on which Sumāli was slain, but the present wording does not explicitly say Sumāli. If *Mālimān* is taken as Mālyavān, he in fact survived into the Rāma-Rāvaṇa war and offered Rāvaṇa salutary counsel. A different syntactic analysis is then possible: “Death, from whom Mālyavān had escaped, shone forth as Māli entered his city.”

The interpretation involving Mārīca is nevertheless the most natural. Even there, rather than treating *vilaṅgu* merely as “animal,” one can make it qualify Death/Yama. *Vilaṅgu* can mean obstacle, throwing, or shining. Yama, previously obstructed and made to tremble by figures such as Māli and Mārīca, now shone with delight when, after the Lord destroyed them, they arrived in his city to undergo the paths he administers. “Paṭaikkalam” means an implement of battle, a weapon; “many *paṭai*” likewise means many weapons.

## Pāsuram 29

### Tamil Mūlam

பரத்திலும்பரத்தையாதி பௌவநீரணைக்கிடந்து  
உரத்திலும் ஒருத்திதன்னை வைத்துகந்ததன்றியும்  
நரத்திலும் பிறத்திநாத ஞானமூர்த்தியாயினாய்  
ஒருத்தரும் நினாதுதன்மை யின்னதென்னவல்லரே.

### IAST Transliteration

*parattilumparattaiyāti pauvanīraṇaikkiṭantu*  
*urattilum oruttitaṉṉai vaittukantataṉṟiyum*  
*narattilum piṟattināta ñāṉamūrttiyāyiṉāy*  
*oruttarum niṉātutaṉmai yiṉṉateṉṉavallarē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You are supreme even over those who are themselves above Prakṛti. You recline upon the serpent-couch in the Milk Ocean, joyfully keeping the incomparable Goddess upon Your chest; and not stopping even with that accessibility, You are born among human beings. O Master, embodiment of knowledge! Who is capable of saying, “Such and such is Your nature,” and defining it?

### English Translation of the Commentary

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Even over the higher” — You possess supremacy over the sentient selves who stand above Prakṛti. “You are the Supreme.” “In the Milk Ocean,” “reclining on the couch,” the serpent-bed. “The one incomparable Lady,” “placing her also upon Your chest,” and “rejoicing” in complete bliss. “And not only that” — not stopping even with so much accessibility. “Among human beings also,” “You are born.” “O Master!” “You became the embodiment of knowledge” — O One whose very nature is consciousness. “Your nature”: who can say “it is precisely this” and adequately characterize it? Is even one person capable?

As scripture says, “higher than the imperishable,” You are above all jīvas, who themselves are above Prakṛti. As the Gītā says, “There is nothing higher than Me,” nothing exists above You. The Upaniṣadic expressions “in the supreme imperishable,” “in the supreme space,” and “beyond darkness” point to Your abiding manifestation in luminous Paramapada beyond Prakṛti. Yet You descend from there into the Milk Ocean and recline upon the serpent-bed solely in order to become accessible to Your creatures.

Even then, fearing that creatures burdened by sin might hesitate to approach You, You make the incomparable Goddess the *puruṣakāra*, the mediatrix who recommends them to You, place her permanently upon Your own chest, and thereby proclaim that You are ready to protect at every instant. When, after all this, You saw that such accessibility was still of use principally to the gods and not to ordinary human beings, You were born as a man in forms such as Rāma and Kṛṣṇa. Nor did You merely take birth and destroy the wicked; You taught saving knowledge in order to establish the beneficial path in the world. Who can describe the extraordinary qualities of You, whose tenderness is utterly without ulterior cause (*avyāja-vātsalya*)?

“Parattai āti.” *Parattan* means one who is supreme; in direct address or second-person predication it can become *parattai*. It may also derive from *parastha*, “one who abides in the supreme realm.” In that case it harmonizes especially well with the movement of the verse: “You who dwell in Paramapada descended from there into the ocean within Prakṛti.” The form *āti* is a present-tense second-person finite verb derived from the verbal base *ā*, “to become/be.” Such usages occur from the beginning of this Āḻvār’s hymns; compare “You are born” (*piṟatti*) later in this very pāsuram.

Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai explains the conjunctive particle in “upon the chest also” as coordinating the Goddess’s inclusion within the Lord’s very essential nature as well. That is: just as she embraces and is inseparable from His divine form, her divine self is inseparably united with His divine Self. Āḷavandār says in effect, “The śrutis speak of those forms and manifestations as deeply embraced by Your own counterpart,” and another celebrated statement says, “Because she is included within You, even scripture does not name her separately.”

In accordance with the sentiment, “Wherever I cry out, You are already before me,” the Supreme Self, through compassion, is everywhere inseparably conjoined with the divine Self of Śrī, the mediatrix. Both are all-pervasive and everywhere united; beyond this, the Lord has also arranged His divine form so that her divine form may visibly take refuge upon His chest.

“One woman”: the incomparable One. She is superior to all the other divine Consorts and, in a certain functional sense, stands even one step above the Lord, because in addition to sharing all His greatness she possesses the unique eminence of *puruṣakāratva*, intercession on behalf of the dependent soul. Even in bodily beauty she is preeminent, as the Rāmāyaṇa’s phrase “this dark-eyed lady” suggests. Recall Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai’s statement that the Lord’s status as the Husband of Śrī is itself the cause both of the distinctiveness of His nature described below and of the excellence corresponding to it.

“Born among men.” Scripture itself speaks of Him as the *nara* joined to the divine. “You became the embodiment of knowledge” may recall the teachings delivered in the Rāma and Kṛṣṇa incarnations. Or it may mean that He took His stand as the locus of devotion-shaped knowledge and protected those who surrendered. Another interpretation is that He incarnates as the many Ācāryas and teaches *tattva*, *hita*, and *puruṣārtha*. “Ninātu” is an extended phonetic form of *ninatu*, “Your.” “Can anyone?” means: no one can; is there even one who is capable?

## Pāsuram 30

### Tamil Mūlam

வானகமும் மண்ணகமும் வெற்புமேழ்கடல்களும்  
போனகம்செய்தாலிலைத்துயின்ற புண்டரீகனே!  
தேனகஞ்செய்தண்ணறு மலர்த்துழாய்நன்மாலையாய்!  
கூனகம்புகத்தெறித்த கொற்றவில்லியல்லையே.

### IAST Transliteration

*vāṉakamum maṇṇakamum veṟpumēḻkaṭalkaḷum*  
*pōṉakamceytālilaittuyiṉṟa puṇṭarīkaṉē!*  
*tēṉakañceytaṇṇaṟu malarttuḻāynaṉmālaiyāy!*  
*kūṉakampukatteṟitta koṟṟavilliyallaiyē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O unsurpassed, pure Lord who swallowed as food the heavenly realm, the earth, the mountains, and the seven oceans, and then slept upon a banyan leaf! O wearer of the excellent cool, fragrant garland of flowering tulasī that gives a dwelling to honey-bees! Are You not the victorious archer who shot so that the hunch sank back into the body?—the peerless master of the bow!

### English Translation of the Commentary

As the Gītā declares, “Among wielders of weapons I am Rāma,” the Rāma incarnation displayed the full power of the use of missiles and weapons. The Āḻvār therefore savors His distinction as the incomparable archer.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “The heavenly world” — the upper realm where the gods dwell; “the earthly world” — the earth where humans live; “the mountains”; “the seven oceans”: swallowing all of them “as food,” taking them into Himself; “upon a banyan leaf,” “sleeping.” “O Puṇḍarīka!” — O pure, spotless, supreme One. “Making a home for honey” — or for bees; “cool and fragrant,” “flowering,” “holy basil”; “O wearer of the excellent garland” made of that tulasī. “So that the hunch entered within” — so that the deformity sank into the body; “shooting.” “Are You not the victorious bowman?” — are You not the triumphant wielder of the bow, the incomparable master of archery?

You swallow the entire universe without any weapon at all and then sleep effortlessly. Once You have come bearing a bow in order to kill insignificant enemies such as Rāvaṇa, what possible doubt can there be about Your victory? Another sense is: You preserve indiscriminately gods and humans, sentient and insentient beings alike, keeping them all within Your stomach; therefore You are the One who helps everyone in time of danger.

The expressions “heavenly world,” “earthly world,” and so forth denote both the places and all the living beings contained within them. This is like the ordinary instruction “protect the curd from the crow,” which is understood to include protection from cats and other possible thieves too. To show the general linguistic principle that mention of a container or support can include what rests in it, the elders cite the classical example, “the beds cry out,” meaning the people lying on the beds cry out.

“Mountains and seven oceans.” Some explain this as “the seven mountains and seven oceans.” The seven great mountain-ranges are well known. The earth is described as containing seven island-continents: Jambū, Plakṣa, Śālmala, Kuśa, Krauñca, Śāka, and Puṣkara. Each is surrounded by its own ocean—of salt water, sugarcane juice, liquor, ghee, curds, milk, and pure water respectively. Jambūdvīpa lies at the center, with golden Mount Meru in its middle.

Jambūdvīpa itself is divided into seven regions or *varṣas*: Bhārata, Kimpuruṣa, Hari, Ilāvṛta, Ramyaka, Hiraṇmaya, and Uttarakuru. Mountain-ranges mark the divisions between them. On this side of central Meru are Niṣadha, Hemakūṭa, and Himālaya; on the other side are Nīlagiri, Kailāsa, and Śṛṅgin, also called Uttara-Vindhya. Together with Meru these make seven and are the *varṣa*-mountains. Other island-continents likewise have, for the most part, seven mountain ranges. Within Bhārata-varṣa too seven traditional mountains are counted, including Mahendra, Malaya, Sahya, Śuktimān, Vindhya, and Pāriyātra; the transcript is defective at the final name. These are called the *kula-parvatas*. Since there are many possible sets of mountains, perhaps the pāsuram deliberately says simply “mountains” in general and applies the number seven explicitly only to the oceans.

“Banyan leaf.” The marvel is precisely that, after making all of this His food and keeping it within His stomach, He appears as a baby resting on a single tender leaf. What are we to think of such divine wonder?

“Puṇḍarīka.” In Veda and Mīmāṃsā usage *puṇḍarīka* famously denotes the red lotus; this is also discussed in our refinement of the Chāndogya Bhāṣya. The Lord appears as though He were a forest of lotuses: lotus-like face, lotus-eyes, red fruit-like mouth, lotus-palms, lotus-feet, and the navel-lotus. Hence He may be called Puṇḍarīka. Alternatively, the Sahasranāma Bhāṣya cites “Puṇḍarīka is the supreme abode,” using the term for Paramapada; then “O Puṇḍarīka” can mean “O One who abides in that Puṇḍarīka.” In the Bṛhadāraṇyaka tradition of solar contemplation, a prayer asks to become “the one puṇḍarīka among human beings,” showing that the word can signify something outstanding or excellent. It is also a name of certain varieties of elephant and tiger. In the present verse it can therefore carry the sense “O supremely excellent, pure, spotless One.”

“Making a home for honey.” *Tēṉ* can mean honey or bee; thus the phrase may also mean “made a dwelling-place by bees.” The tulasī garland establishes itself in absolute exclusive service such that it belongs to no one but the Lord. It wards off all inauspiciousness and is supremely delightful. Its mention also reveals Him as Lord of the Nitya-vibhūti, complementing the preceding description of Him as Lord of the Līlā-vibhūti.

“So that the hunch entered within, He shot.” Kṛṣṇa’s straightening of the hunchbacked Trivakrā is famous. The point here is that, before that later human incarnation in which His divine sovereignty became more overt, He had already incarnated as Rāma in such a way that His lordship did not visibly obtrude: He appeared simply as a man and shone as the victorious archer.

There is also a later narrative, not found in the Vālmīki Rāmāyaṇa but said to underlie traditions such as Kampaṉ’s Rāmāyaṇa: while playing with a toy-bow in childhood, Rāma shot an arrow playfully at Mantharā’s hump, and for that reason she later incited Kaikeyī to deprive him of the kingdom. Some take that episode here. Yet the hump did not literally disappear “inside” her body; one could say it was struck inward and later rose again because it still had much work to perform. Another wordplay takes “the hunchback entered the interior” to mean that Mantharā entered Kaikeyī’s palace; because of that, Rāma entered the forest, loosed His bow, won victory, and became renowned as the victorious archer. Or the sense may be that when the hunchback who had intended Rāma’s destruction by sending Him to the forest heard that He had instead conquered Khara, Dūṣaṇa, Rāvaṇa, and the rest and become the triumphant bowman, shame made her very hump seem to sink back into her body.

## Pāsuram 31

### Tamil Mūlam

காலநேமிகாலனே கணக்கிலாத கீர்த்தியாய்  
ஞாலமேமுமுண்டுபண்டோர் பாலனாய பண்பனே!  
வேலைவேவவில்வளைத்த வெல்சினத்தவீர!நின்  
பாலராய பத்தர்சித்த முத்தி செய்யுமூர்த்தியே!

### IAST Transliteration

*kālanēmikālaṉē kaṇakkilāta kīrttiyāy*  
*ñālamēmumuṇṭupaṇṭōr pālaṉāya paṇpaṉē!*  
*vēlaivēvavilvaḷaitta velciṉattavīra!niṉ*  
*pālarāya pattarcitta mutti ceyyumūrttiyē!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O death of Kālanemi—or Lord who drives the wheel of Time! O One of immeasurable fame, whose nature it is to swallow all seven worlds at dissolution and become the peerless infant! O mighty hero of victorious wrath who bent Your bow so that the ocean itself would boil! O Lord whose manifested form removes distress from the minds of devotees who belong to You!

### English Translation of the Commentary

The next three pāsurams make explicit the title “victorious bowman” used above. This first one asks: when the bow that once shot at opponents is later directed toward someone who comes to refuge, what does the Lord do?

**Word-by-word gloss.** “O death of Kālanemi!” — O destroyer of the asura Kālanemi; alternatively, O One who sets the wheel (*nemi*) of Time in motion. “O One of uncountable fame!” “Long ago,” at the beginning of dissolution, “swallowing all seven worlds” and “becoming one infant” — O One whose wondrous nature is to become that incomparable child. “So that the ocean boiled,” “bending the bow”; “O hero of conquering wrath!” — O great warrior whose anger always achieves victory. “Those who belong to You,” “devotees,” their “mind,” “liberation” — freedom from distress; “O manifest Lord who causes [that freedom]!”

Since these hymns characteristically contemplate the Lord’s transcendence and accessibility together, the verse first recalls His transcendent greatness. Kālanemi was an asura, maternal uncle of Rāvaṇa, who was killed by the Lord in the war involving Tārakāsura. One may also remember the Bhāgavata account that the being formerly born as Kālanemi was later born as Kaṃsa. Because *nemi* means a wheel, the phrase can also be interpreted generally as “the One who turns the wheel of Time.” The following reference to dissolution makes this especially apt: He is the regulator of the times of creation and dissolution. He Himself is beyond destruction by time, the One whom time cannot prematurely consume. Scriptural statements express the idea: He cooks or consumes Time itself; there Time has no mastery, for He is its Lord. Hence, when all else dissolves, He remains and even appears as the infant.

“Making the ocean boil…” Rāma first employed the conciliatory means toward the Ocean-king and, as an act of surrender, lay upon the shore seeking passage, but the Ocean gave no response. Then Rāma reflected, in the famous words, “The gentle person is indeed despised,” grew intensely angry, bent His bow, and resolved to shoot so as to dry up the billowing ocean and allow the vānaras to cross on foot. At the very instant the arrow was about to be released, the Ocean himself appeared and sought refuge at His divine feet. Immediately Rāma did not make the Ocean the target of the already-nocked missile. Instead He destroyed the Ocean’s enemies elsewhere and thereby freed the Ocean’s mind from anxiety.

Thus the pāsuram teaches that even those who have been favorable to the Lord may, through some temporary confusion, behave in a way that makes Him angry; yet the very anger of the Lord, once they regain clarity and turn to Him, becomes the cause of immense benefit to them.

“Those who belong to You.” The Ocean-king had already been favorable to Rāma, born in the line of Sagara; this is evident from his sending Maināka mountain to give Hanumān a place to rest. Such a one became confused for some intervening reason and then again became a devotee; hence the compound “those who belong to You and [again] are devotees.” *Ninpāl* means “toward You”; *ninpālar* means “those who are on Your side,” those favorable to You.

Because the third line specifically concerns the Ocean-king, the fourth line is generalized while keeping him in mind. Earlier pāsurams showed the bow’s prowess against genuine enemies. Here its very different operation toward one who returns to refuge is made clear.

The elders also interpret the fourth line in a liberation-centered way: “O One who causes the minds of those devotees who seek You alone, with no independent end, to be freed from attachment to anything else.” On that interpretation surrenderers such as Vibhīṣaṇa can also be intended. “Mūrti” may mean Master. If it means the divine auspicious body, the sense is that He incarnated precisely to produce this freedom: on merely seeing that divine form, the mind no longer wanders toward other objects. The word thus praises the unsurpassed attraction of His body.

## Pāsuram 32

### Tamil Mūlam

குரக்கினப்படைகொடு குரைகடலின் மீதுபோய்  
அரக்கரங்கரங்க வெஞ்சரந்துரந்த வாதி நீ  
இரக்கமண்கொடுத்தவற் கிரக்கமொன்றுமின்றியே  
பரக்கவைத்தளந்துகொண்ட பற்பபாதனல்லையே.

### IAST Transliteration

*kurakkiṉappaṭaikoṭu kuraikaṭaliṉ mītupōy*  
*arakkaraṅkaraṅka veñcaranturanta vāti nī*  
*irakkamaṇkoṭuttavaṟ kirakkamoṉṟumiṉṟiyē*  
*parakkavaittaḷantukoṇṭa paṟpapātaṉallaiyē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

Taking an army of the monkey race, You crossed over the roaring ocean and there discharged fierce arrows so that the rākṣasas of Laṅkā were destroyed. You, foremost among heroes, are not You also the lotus-footed One who, when Mahābali gave the earth You begged for, showed not the slightest pity but spread Your foot everywhere and measured it all for Yourself?

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār explains “victorious bowman” in another way.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Taking the monkey race as an army,” “going over the roaring ocean” — crossing by the bridge; “there,” in Laṅkā, “so that the rākṣasas were destroyed,” “discharging fierce arrows.” “You, the foremost” — You, the chief among heroes. Earlier, “when You begged for earth,” “toward the one who gave it” without hesitation, Mahābali, “without even one measure of compassion,” “spreading” [Your foot] over all the worlds, “placing and measuring,” and thereby “taking” everything—“are You not the One with lotus-like feet?”

Because this pāsuram belongs to the Rāma section, we construe it as one sentence so that Rāma’s role is primary rather than breaking the opening lines into an independent sentence and supplying an unstated “You remain.”

To protect the gods who had sought refuge in You, You crossed the ocean with the monkeys massed as Your army, not leaving even one of them behind. Yet after crossing, You did not truly need to give those monkeys room to fight: before Rāvaṇa’s own eyes You personally destroyed the central forces and the other armies so that he trembled. Why marvel at this? On a former occasion, at the request of those same gods, You went as Vāmana to beg from Mahābali. That great donor gave despite the objections of his priest Śukrācārya, yet You did not leave him even a little place on the earthly realm to stand. Without apparent softheartedness You took mountains, oceans, and the entire earth.

Did You, because You appeared as a little Vāmana brāhmaṇa, fear the heroic men attached to Mahābali? Did compassion make You leave even a tiny portion unmeasured? Did You consider that placing Your lotus-soft feet over forests, rough ground, and hills might hurt them? Both Indra and Mahābali desired the sovereignty of the three worlds; both were in some sense devotees. Did You divide the realm into two shares for them? Born as a brāhmaṇa, the very class traditionally associated with compassion, You displayed no such leniency; then, born as the heroic kṣatriya Rāma, You acted as described. The apparent contradiction is the object of wonder.

“Aranga.” The elders note that where one might expect *arakka*, “to wear away/destroy,” the consonant has softened to *aranga*. *Arakkal* can mean rubbing or wearing away; *araṅgal* itself can bear the sense of perishing. “Āti” means principal or foremost—foremost among warriors.

“Without even one compassion, spreading…” It is improper, ordinarily, to ruin one who has given to you. Yet You did not think, “Let me leave him at least one pace and take the rest.” Without apparent pity You expanded Your foot and measured everything. A variant reading, “without leaving anything for him to remain in,” is found, but there is not sufficient basis to reject the received reading “without any compassion” in favor of that conjecture.

“Lotus-footed.” The Āḻvār is enraptured that for the sake of favoring the gods You even forgot the tenderness of Your own lotus-feet.

## Pāsuram 33

### Tamil Mūlam

மின்னிறத் தெயிற்றாக்கன் வீழவெஞ்சரந்துரந்து  
பின்னவற் கருள்புரிக் தரசளித்த பெற்றியோய்  
நன்னிறத்தோரின் சொலேழை பின்னைகேள்வ! மன்னுசீர்  
பொன்னிறத்த வண்ணனாய புண்டரீக னல்லையே.

### IAST Transliteration

*miṉṉiṟat teyiṟṟākkaṉ vīḻaveñcaranturantu*  
*piṉṉavaṟ karuḷpurik taracaḷitta peṟṟiyōy*  
*naṉṉiṟattōriṉ colēḻai piṉṉaikēḷva! maṉṉucīr*  
*poṉṉiṟatta vaṇṇaṉāya puṇṭarīka ṉallaiyē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O One of wondrous greatness who discharged fierce arrows so that the rākṣasa Rāvaṇa, with teeth flashing like lightning, fell, and who then bestowed grace upon his younger brother and gave him the kingdom! O beloved of Nappinnai, the incomparable maiden of lovely complexion and sweet speech, who stands needy only for You! You are indeed the Puṇḍarīka, possessing an enduringly auspicious form suffused with golden radiance.

### English Translation of the Commentary

By describing the principal slaying of Rāvaṇa, the Āḻvār continues to explain why Rāma is the “victorious bowman,” and at the same time begins the transition from the Rāma incarnation to Śrī Kṛṣṇa.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “With lightning-colored teeth,” “the rākṣasa” Rāvaṇa; “so that he fell,” “releasing fierce arrows.” “To the one after him,” his younger brother Vibhīṣaṇa, “showing grace” and “giving the kingdom” of the rākṣasas—O One of such greatness! “Of beautiful complexion,” “one incomparable [maiden],” “sweet in speech,” “needy” — without any independent resource, desiring You alone; “Nappinnai”; “O her beloved!” “Abiding excellences” — permanent auspicious qualities. “Having a form of golden hue”; “are You not the Puṇḍarīka?”

The preceding pāsuram indicated that, in response to the prayer of the immortals, the Lord destroyed not only the rākṣasa who abducted the Goddess but also his relatives, sons, and forces; for that very purpose the gods themselves had assembled in monkey forms. Here the focus is more direct: the rākṣasa who personally offended the Goddess is destroyed. The phrase “teeth like lightning” evokes Rāvaṇa’s ferocity: though his great minister Vibhīṣaṇa and even elders such as his maternal grandfather Mālyavān urged him to take the right course, he rejected their counsel, grinding and baring his teeth in anger.

Since “gave the kingdom” is stated separately, “showed grace” must refer to another favor. As Śrī Deśika explains in *Abhayapradāna-sāra*, Vibhīṣaṇa desired liberation and service to the Lord alone; the Lord granted him precisely that. The kingdom was then bestowed incidentally, as a secondary benefit. Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai likewise marvels: “Though he had no desire for a kingdom,” he had been driven out “humiliated like a servant,” and yet the Lord proceeded to give him the kingdom of Laṅkā. What sort of nature is this?

Mentioning grace to the younger brother (*pinnavan*) naturally brings to mind the help rendered for Pinnai, Nappinnai; hence the verse turns to her. Just as He subdued the seven bulls for Nappinnai, one may relish the killing of Rāvaṇa as ultimately serving the purpose of giving the kingdom to Vibhīṣaṇa. Until Vibhīṣaṇa arrived, Rāvaṇa’s death was thought of as being for Sītā’s sake; once Vibhīṣaṇa surrendered, it was as though the same death also became the means of giving him the kingdom.

The third line can alternatively continue to refer to Rāma. Vedavatī later incarnated as Sītā, so she too could be indicated by a term such as *Pinnai*. Or *Pinnai* can be a general name for Lakṣmī. “Golden-hued, sweet-spoken” fits Sītā beautifully, who is described in the Rāmāyaṇa as a charming lady of exceedingly sweet speech.

“Having a golden-hued form.” Since *vaṇṇam* follows *niṟam*, one may take *vaṇṇam* here to mean bodily form: “possessing a form whose hue is golden.” Others reverse the semantic assignment—*niṟam* as form and *vaṇṇam* as color—so that both the splendor of the limbs and the beauty of the complexion are praised. Another interpretation is: “the cloud-dark Lord whose complexion has taken golden radiance into itself.” Gold is thus mingled with His familiar dark hue.

This is especially beautiful when the Āḻvār contemplates the Lord together with Śrī. The statement “I saw Śrī; I saw the golden body” and the Upaniṣadic “the golden Person is seen” support such a vision. It also answers the question, “How can the dark-bodied Lord be described as golden?” Śrī Deśika speaks of this under the expression “eternal Śrī,” and in the *Tātparya-candrikā* he also observes that high-quality refined gold itself can display a dark-cloud tinge.

“Puṇḍarīka.” In the same Upaniṣadic passage about the golden Person, His divine eyes are described as lotus-like (*puṇḍarīka*). For the broader senses of the word *puṇḍarīka*, see the discussion under pāsuram 30.

## Pāsuram 34

### Tamil Mūlam

ஆதியாதி யாதிநீயொ ரண்டமாதி யாதலால்  
சோதியாத சோதிநீய துண்மையில் விளங்கினாய்  
வேதமாகி வேள்வியாகி விண்ணினோடு மண்ணுமாய்  
ஆதியாகி யாயனாய மாயமென்ன மாயமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*ātiyāti yātinīyo raṇṭamāti yātalāl*  
*cōtiyāta cōtinīya tuṇmaiyil viḷaṅkiṉāy*  
*vētamāki vēḷviyāki viṇṇiṉōṭu maṇṇumāy*  
*ātiyāki yāyaṉāya māyameṉṉa māyamē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You are the primary cause, the efficient cause, and the material cause; therefore You also become the incomparable cosmic egg, the effect. You are the Light that cannot be reached by probing thought, and in that very reality You manifest as Light. Becoming the Veda, becoming the sacrifice, becoming heaven and earth, and becoming the source of all these—what a wonder, what an immeasurable wonder, that You became a simple cowherd!

### English Translation of the Commentary

From here many pāsurams are devoted to the Lord of the cowherds.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Cause, cause, cause are You” — You Yourself are all three kinds of cause. “Therefore,” specifically because You are also the material cause, “You become one cosmic egg” — You become even the effect, the unique Brahmāṇḍa. “You are the Light not reached by investigation” — the supreme radiance inaccessible to mere reasoning. “In that reality You manifested” — while remaining in precisely that state, You appeared as luminous *vyūha* forms; there is no loss of Your nature. “Becoming the Veda,” the highest authority; “becoming the sacrifice,” the worship of You taught in it; “becoming heaven together with earth,” the places of liberation and enjoyment attained as fruits of worship; “becoming the source” of all of these. Yet “the wonder of becoming a cowherd” — appearing as an apparently ignorant herdsman. “What a wonder! What a māyā!”

Every produced thing in the world is explained through three types of cause: *upādāna* (material cause), *nimitta* (efficient cause), and *sahakārin* (auxiliary or instrumental cause). For a pot, clay is the material cause, the potter is the efficient cause, and the various tools required by the potter are auxiliary causes. Vedānta teaches that all the causes required for the universe are the Lord Himself.

Some schools, such as certain logicians, admit God only as an efficient cause analogous to the potter. That is not the Vedāntic truth. Since He is also the material cause, just as clay itself becomes the pot, the Lord—through the sentient and insentient reality constituting His body—becomes the effect called the Brahmāṇḍa. No ordinary being possesses this condition of being every kind of cause and also the effect.

Even while appearing as all this universe, You remain the immense Radiance untouched by the defects present in the world. No one can discover such a One by merely reasoning You out. The Upaniṣad says, “This understanding is not to be attained by reasoning,” and the *Śāstra-yonitva* section of the Brahma-sūtras establishes precisely this point. Remaining in such supreme greatness without diminution, You manifest as Vāsudeva, Saṅkarṣaṇa, Pradyumna, Aniruddha, and the other *vyūha* forms. That is fitting; one may say that You manifested similarly in the Rāma incarnation.

But then You, such a Lord, appeared as a cowherd who outwardly “knows nothing,” the crown-jewel of playful lovers and butter-thieves, in a birth that seems contrary to Veda, sacrifice, and exalted divine status and that onlookers could deride—yet because Your body is non-material and intrinsically pure, no defect adheres to You. What words can describe this māyā? The fact that one Being is every cause, becomes the whole effect without acquiring its faults, and yet remains transcendent is already astonishing; but even beyond those wonders, this becoming of the cowherd is the wonder that cannot be exhausted by description.

The repeated “ādi ādi ādi nī” can alternatively be parsed: *ādi* = primary or fundamental; *ādi* = as the cause; *nī āti* = You become. The form *āti* contains the verbal root *ā* with the second-person singular ending *-ti*. Some connect “therefore” with the upper line as well.

“Light not investigated/reached.” Śrī Rāmānuja, in the *Jyotir-adhikaraṇa*, teaches the unsurpassed brilliance implied by His being the cause in every way. It may also mean a self-luminous light that needs no other illuminator. Or it can designate the self-luminous Nitya-vibhūti. After the preceding reference to insentient forms, this may also indicate the conscious reality.

For “You manifested in that reality,” another explanation treats the locative ending with an instrumental force: “because that Light is established by valid revelation, You shine as the utterly distinctive One.”

## Pāsuram 35

### Tamil Mūlam

அம்புலாவு மீனுமாகி யாமையாகி யாழியார்  
தம்பிரானு மாகிமிக்கதன்புமிக்கதன்றியும்  
கொம்பராவு நுண்மருங்கு லாயர்மாதர் பிள்ளையாய்  
எம்பிரானு மாயவண்ண மென்கொலோ வெம்மீசனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*ampulāvu mīṉumāki yāmaiyāki yāḻiyār*  
*tampirāṉu mākimikkataṉpumikkataṉṟiyum*  
*komparāvu nuṇmaruṅku lāyarmātar piḷḷaiyāy*  
*empirāṉu māyavaṇṇa meṉkolō vemmīcaṉē!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O our Lord! You became the Fish that moves in water; You became the Tortoise; yet in all this You remained the Lord even of the sovereign Discus. And not merely because Your overflowing love became so great: You were born as the child of the cowherd-women whose exceedingly slender waists outdo both vine-shoot and serpent, and thereby became our own Benefactor. Why did You assume such a condition?

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord seems to answer the preceding question: “I have taken births even as very lowly animals such as fish, turtle, and boar. Why then do you call My birth as a human cowherd the greatest wonder?” The Āḻvār explains why.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “O our Master!” “Becoming the Lord even of the Discus” — remaining the beneficent Master of the divine Sudarśana. “Becoming a turtle,” incarnating as Kūrma; “becoming the fish that moves through the waters,” incarnating as Matsya. “Love overflowing” — such immense *vātsalya*. “Not merely that it became so great”: there was something still more. “Whose slender waist” is like—or surpasses—“a tender vine and a serpent”; “the cowherd-women”; “being born as their child.” “And becoming our benefactor/Lord.” “Why was Your state like this?”

Even when You incarnated as fish and turtle, Your condition remained exalted like that of the Lord of the divine Discus. Why? Because though You assumed animal forms, You did not appear in such a way that anyone could say, “He is the child of this fish” or “the child of this turtle.” You manifested independently. But when You appeared as the cowherd, You did not appear in that way. You remained ten months in the womb of a cowherd woman whose waist was slender as vine and serpent, emerged as though born from her, and made everyone say, “This is her child.” Why should the Supreme be born in the same fashion as ordinary living beings?

If You had to be born to bring Devakī’s austerity to fruition, why was it necessary to make Yourself famous specifically as the child of the women of the cowherd settlement? In truth all these acts were only to steal the hearts of people like me. If there is some other reason, tell me what it is.

“Moving in water” grammatically could qualify the tortoise too, but it is connected specifically with the Fish to evoke the Matsya narrative. When the Lord became the Fish, He successively moved and grew in the joined palms of King Satyavrata while he performed his daily rite in the river Kṛtamālā, then in a water-pot, a larger vessel, a great pool, and finally the vast ocean.

“Lord of the Discus.” The phrase respectfully pluralizes the divine Discus as “Āḻiyār,” honoring Sudarśana as king of all weapons, and says that even he has Bhagavān as his Lord. It thus reveals the greatness of the Lord’s transcendent nature. Grammatically “Āḻiyār-tam pirān” is “the Lord belonging to the Discus.” Alternatively, “Āḻiyār” can denote the Nityasūris who eternally bear the conch and discus; He is their Lord too. This is mentioned here to show that such sovereignty is never lost even in incarnations such as the Fish.

“Became great.” The animal incarnations came from overflowing love, and therefore even there His superiority, rather than abasement, is what shines. The verbal form *mikkatu* can be construed predicatively: “You became exceedingly great,” just as in “you came” or “you spoke.” It may also be nominal: “apart from the greatness that was [already Yours].” Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai separates the expression as “that which became great; love; overflowing; that; apart from.” Some explain: “Your greatness increased; Your love too You caused to appear greatly”—a third-person-like form applied to the second person, comparable to Sanskrit *bhavān abhavat*.

“The slender waist that outdoes vine and serpent.” The compound contains an omitted conjunction: “like both the tender vine and the serpent.” *Aravu* means serpent, and *arāvu* is a lengthened form; compare Tiruvāymoḻi 1.10.3. Tamil grammarians note a rule under which final *u* can disappear and the preceding vowel lengthen; here the *u* is retained alongside lengthening in the transmitted form. Alternatively, following the *Īṭu*, “the waist that *arāvu*—makes light of, puts to shame—the vine-shoot,” means one even more beautiful than the creeper.

Some may say there is no need to inquire about His being carried in the womb, because that is not literally true of the divine body. Yet His being visibly carried at the side or hip of cowherd women is certainly true; unlike the Fish and Turtle He did not keep growing beyond them. The plural “cowherd women” celebrates this fortune: many women held Him at their sides and raised Him as though He were each one’s own child.

“Our Benefactor.” Seeing Your accessibility as You grew among cowherd women who scarcely knew left from right, we too have gained the courage to approach You without fear. “Our” includes all who, like the Āḻvār, boldly immerse themselves in You—sages, Āḻvārs, and others. The inner purport is that He took precisely this birth for their sake.

## Pāsuram 36

### Tamil Mūlam

ஆடகத்த பூண்முலை யசோதையாய்ச்சி பிள்ளையாய்  
சாடுதைத்தோர் புள்ளதாவி கள்ளதாய பேய்மகள்  
வீடவைத்த வெய்யகொங்கை யையபாலமுதுசெய்து  
ஆடகக்கை மாதர்வா யமுதமுண்ட தென்கொலோ.

### IAST Transliteration

*āṭakatta pūṇmulai yacōtaiyāycci piḷḷaiyāy*  
*cāṭutaittōr puḷḷatāvi kaḷḷatāya pēymakaḷ*  
*vīṭavaitta veyyakoṅkai yaiyapālamutuceytu*  
*āṭakakkai mātarvā yamutamuṇṭa teṉkolō.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

Born as the child of the cowherdess Yaśodā, whose breasts were adorned with golden ornaments, You kicked the cart; then from the scorching breast of the demoness who came deceitfully as a mother, with the intent of a fierce swooping bird, You drank the subtle milk that had been prepared to kill You. Why then did You also drink the nectar of the mouths of the cowherd-women whose hands bore golden ornaments?

### English Translation of the Commentary

Having spoken of His being raised at the hips of the cowherd women, the Āḻvār now describes how He grew by drinking breast-milk and the nectar of their mouths.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Breasts adorned with ornaments of gold”; “the cowherdess Yaśodā”; “being her child” — appearing as her son. “Kicking the cart” — when He cried for milk, kicking Śakaṭāsura with His divine foot and destroying him. “With the spirit/intention of one fierce bird” — possessing the murderous intent of a uniquely cruel swooping bird. “The demoness,” Pūtanā, “deceitfully becoming a mother.” “The hot breast” — the poison-filled, burning breast “set in place for [His] death.” “The delicate/subtle milk,” “making it nectar,” drinking it as though delightful. “The women whose hands bear gold” — cowherd women wearing golden ornaments on their hands; “the nectar of their mouths,” “drinking it.” “Why indeed?”

Yaśodā, wearing splendid ornaments, lovingly fed You the nectar of her breast as You grew. Yet while crying for milk You kicked and in an instant destroyed the rolling cart. That is one kind of divine wonder. Pūtanā came as swiftly and unexpectedly as a bird swooping down and, disguised as a loving mother, offered You her poisoned breast; You drank her milk with apparent affection. That too is wondrous. But the greatest wonder is that You assumed different playful guises and delighted in drinking the nectar of the lips of the cowherd women who carried You at their sides. What purpose did all this serve?

In the preceding pāsuram “cowherd women” was plural because their slender waists were mentioned and many women could be intended. Here the specific mention of the breast points especially to Yaśodā. *Āṭakam* is Sanskrit *hāṭaka*, gold; “āṭakatta” means made of or connected with gold.

The kicking of the cart is introduced because the child was crying for milk. “Puḷḷatu āvi.” Since everywhere the tradition says that the demoness merely assumed the deceptive appearance of a mother, it is not being claimed that she literally came in bird form. Rather, she came like a predatory bird that descends upon children. Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai gives *āvi* the sense “intention, thought.” *Āvi*, self, mind, and intention can overlap semantically.

The phrase “one bird-like [spirit]” may evoke the Vedic example used for the Śyena sacrifice: “just as a hawk swoops down and seizes, so this [rite] swoops upon and seizes the enemy.” Pūtanā arrived with just such unseen suddenness, as a hawk falls upon a fish. *Vīṭa* is an extended form of *viṭa*, “to let go,” here “to cause to die.”

## Pāsuram 37

### Tamil Mūlam

காய்த்தநீள் விளங்கனி யுதிர்த்தெதிர்ந்த பூங்குருந்து (தம்)  
சாய்த்துமா பிளந்தகைத் தலத்த கண்ண னென்பரால்  
ஆய்ச்சிபாலை யுண்டுமண்ணை யுண்டுவெண்ணெ யுண்டுபின்  
பேய்ச்சிபாலை யுண்டுபண்டோ ரேனமாய வாமனா!

### IAST Transliteration

*kāyttanīḷ viḷaṅkaṉi yutirttetirnta pūṅkuruntu (tam)*  
*cāyttumā piḷantakait talatta kaṇṇa ṉeṉparāl*  
*āyccipālai yuṇṭumaṇṇai yuṇṭuveṇṇe yuṇṭupiṉ*  
*pēyccipālai yuṇṭupaṇṭō rēṉamāya vāmaṉā!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

They say You are Kṛṣṇa, whose great palms made the densely ripened fruits of the tall wood-apple tree fall, felled the flowering kuruntu tree that confronted You, and split the horse-demon. Yet You drank the cowherdess’s milk, ate earth, ate butter, and afterwards even drank the demoness’s milk—O Vāmana who long before had become the incomparable Boar!

### English Translation of the Commentary

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Drinking the cowherdess’s milk” — in this incarnation, drinking Yaśodā’s breast-milk. “Eating earth” — as a child, naturally putting earth into His mouth. “Eating butter” — stealing and eating the butter throughout the cowherd settlement. “Then drinking the demoness’s milk” — not stopping there, even drinking Pūtanā’s milk as though it were nectar. “Long before,” at the beginning of the cosmic cycle, “becoming one incomparable Boar.” “O Vāmana!” — O One who also performed the Vāmana incarnation. “The tall wood-apple tree bearing dense ripe fruit,” “making [the fruits] fall”; “the flowering kuruntu tree that opposed [You],” “felling it”; “with the great palm that split [the horse],” Keśin. “They call [You] Kṛṣṇa” — the wise identify You as Kṛṣṇa, the One who sustains and governs us.

Because the present sequence concerns the events of the Kṛṣṇa incarnation, the purpose of this pāsuram is not primarily to relish the Varāha and Vāmana incarnations independently. It praises the fact that the very One who appeared in those forms long ago has now become Kṛṣṇa: one Lord appears again and again in many forms.

The acts of eating in the latter half all belong to Kṛṣṇa’s childhood and could have been linked directly with the acts of His hands in the first half. Yet the verse instead says “having eaten… long ago becoming a Boar, O Vāmana!” linking the eating to earlier incarnations. The implication is that these Kṛṣṇa episodes too have already been mentioned and are now recalled like the Varāha and Vāmana episodes; the principal new focus is the greatness of the hands described in the first two lines. No strict chronological sequence is intended between the acts of eating and His becoming Varāha. To make clear that only identity of agent matters, the word “long ago” appears after “having eaten.”

Some explain “eating earth” as preserving the worlds in His divine stomach at dissolution. But because the surrounding acts are specifically Kṛṣṇa’s childhood eatings, the natural meaning is the familiar episode in which the child Kṛṣṇa actually ate soil. The mention of eating earth then calls to mind His earlier taking possession of the earth, and so Varāha and Vāmana are brought into the pāsuram.

The playful sequence can be savored thus: Yaśodā’s milk was not enough, so You ate earth. Perhaps to digest the earth You ate all the butter. Even that did not suffice, so You drank the milk of the demoness who had entered with apparent affection like a cowherdess. In earlier Varāha and Vāmana incarnations You took possession of the earth; now You actually ate earth and drank milk. And You did not stop there. Demons came in forms that outwardly seemed favorable, like trees offering fruits and flowers, just as Pūtanā came like a tiger in cowhide; You used Your hands to destroy them too. Keśin, who came openly spewing hostility in horse form, You likewise slew with Your hand. Thus You destroyed every wicked one and made the name “Kṛṣṇa” fully meaningful.

The proper reading appears to be “making [the fruits] fall, [the tree] opposing.” A variant reads “making [them] fall by throwing.” On that reading some explain that He finished the wood-apple demon and threw him. There may also be an allusion to the episode of Vatsāsura: Kṛṣṇa seized the calf-demon, whirled him around, and threw him upon the wood-apple tree; all its fruits fell and Vatsāsura died. Tradition sometimes speaks of both the calf and the wood-apple tree as asuric manifestations. One need not object that the text does not elsewhere explicitly say He broke the tree: the demon within the tree could have perished when the calf was thrown into it and the fruits fell. This topic is also discussed in our commentary on Periyāḻvār Tirumoḻi.

## Pāsuram 38

### Tamil Mūlam

கடங்கலந்த வன்கரி மருப்பொசித்தோர் பொய்கைவாய்  
விடங்கலந்த பாம்பின்மேல் நடம்பயின்ற நாதனே!  
குடங்கலந்த கூத்தனாய கொண்டல்வண்ண! தண்டுழாய்  
வடங்கலந்த மாலைமார்ப! காலநேமி காலனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*kaṭaṅkalanta vaṉkari maruppocittōr poykaivāy*  
*viṭaṅkalanta pāmpiṉmēl naṭampayiṉṟa nātaṉē!*  
*kuṭaṅkalanta kūttaṉāya koṇṭalvaṇṇa! taṇṭuḻāy*  
*vaṭaṅkalanta mālaimārpa! kālanēmi kālaṉē!*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord who broke the tusk of the mighty rut-filled elephant and practiced Your dance upon the heads of the venom-filled serpent in that peerless pool! O cloud-hued One who became the dancer amid the pots! O Lord whose chest bears garlands mingled with strands of cool tulasī! O death of Kālanemi!

### English Translation of the Commentary

Entranced by the deeds of Kṛṣṇa’s hands, the Āḻvār now moves to deeds performed by His feet in conjunction with them and celebrates several of His sports.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Filled with rut-fluid,” “the strong elephant” Kuvalayāpīḍa; “breaking its tusk.” “In one peerless pool,” the Yamunā pool; “the venom-filled serpent,” Kāliya; “upon [his heads],” “practicing the dance.” “O Lord!” — O Master of all. “Becoming the dancer joined with pots” — dancing the *kuṭa-k-kūttu*. “O cloud-colored One!” “Strands of cool tulasī,” joined with other “garlands”; “O One whose chest wears them!” “O death of Kālanemi!” — O destroyer of the asura Kālanemi.

“Filled with rut” also hints at Kṛṣṇa’s running between the legs of the elephant and playing there in order to inflame Kuvalayāpīḍa’s rage until its rut overflowed even more.

“Dance with pots.” A traditional cowherd dance is performed with pots stacked on the head and also placed upon the hands and shoulders. Kṛṣṇa entered into that dance and delighted everyone at the crossroads. In the same way He delighted all through the subjugation of Kāliya.

The cowherd men and women had crowded on the bank in anguish, thinking, “He has been caught by Kāliya, who rises hissing and spitting poisonous fire!” Yet Kṛṣṇa made them forget that terror. He pressed every one of the serpent’s heads down with His feet so that not a single hood could rise and performed a marvelous dance. Was not the very purpose of His sport to immerse those terrified spectators in the aesthetic joy of witnessing that dance?

“O Lord.” A proprietor must protect what belongs to him; hence You did this even though no one formally asked You. How is He their Lord? Because He is the Lord of all. The words about the cool tulasī garlands indicate this sovereign status.

“O cloud-colored One.” The pots used in the dance, Kāliya under His feet, and Kṛṣṇa Himself could all be savored as sharing a dark coloration. He resembles the cloud not merely in hue but in raining down delight and aesthetic sweetness.

## Pāsuram 39

### Tamil Mūlam

வெற்பெடுத்து வேலைநீர் கலக்கினாய தன்றியும்  
வெற்பெடுத்து வேலைநீர் வரம்புகட்டி வேலை சூழ்  
வெற்பெடுத்த விஞ்சிசூ ழிலங்கைகட் டழித்தநீ  
வெற்பெடுத்து மாரிகாத்த மேகவண்ண னல்லையே.

### IAST Transliteration

*veṟpeṭuttu vēlainīr kalakkiṉāya taṉṟiyum*  
*veṟpeṭuttu vēlainīr varampukaṭṭi vēlai cūḻ*  
*veṟpeṭutta viñcicū ḻilaṅkaikaṭ ṭaḻittanī*  
*veṟpeṭuttu mārikātta mēkavaṇṇa ṉallaiyē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You lifted a mountain and churned the ocean-waters. Nor only that: bringing mountains, You built a causeway through the ocean-waters, and You destroyed the fortifications of Laṅkā, surrounded by the sea and enclosed by the lofty walls raised upon its mountain. Are You not that cloud-hued One who later lifted a mountain and protected the cattle from the rain?

### English Translation of the Commentary

Entering into the wonder of the lifting of Govardhana, the Āḻvār combines it with several other sports involving mountains.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Lifting a mountain” — placing Mount Mandara in position; “the ocean-water,” “You churned” — turning what was above to below and below to above. “And not only that.” “Taking up mountains” — having the vānaras bring many mountains; “in the ocean-water,” “building a boundary/causeway,” the bridge. “Surrounded by the sea”; “the mountain,” Trikūṭa; “the raised wall” — the lofty fortification constructed upon it; “Laṅkā surrounded [by that wall]”; “You destroyed its structure” with Your arrows. Then, in the Kṛṣṇa incarnation, “lifting the mountain,” Govardhana, on Your finger, “protecting from the rain.” “Are You not the cloud-colored One?”

*Iñci* means a fort-wall. Some also use “mountain” itself metaphorically for a wall. In accordance with the word “raised,” the elders explain the phrase as “Laṅkā encircled by the wall built by Viśvakarman upon Mount Trikūṭa.”

“O cloud-colored One.” There is special wonder in the cloud-colored Lord protecting His devotees from the very cloud and its rain.

## Pāsuram 40

### Tamil Mūlam

ஆனைகாத்தோ ரானைகொன் றதன்றியாயர் பிள்ளையாய்  
ஆனைமேய்த்தி ஆநெயுண்டி அன்று குன்றமொன்றினால்  
ஆனைகாத்துமையரிக்கண் மாதரார் திறத்துமுன்  
ஆனையன்று சென்றடர்த்த மாயமென்ன மாயமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*āṉaikāttō rāṉaikoṉ ṟataṉṟiyāyar piḷḷaiyāy*  
*āṉaimēytti āneyuṇṭi aṉṟu kuṉṟamoṉṟiṉāl*  
*āṉaikāttumaiyarikkaṇ mātarār tiṟattumuṉ*  
*āṉaiyaṉṟu ceṉṟaṭartta māyameṉṉa māyamē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You protected an elephant and slew an elephant; then, becoming a cowherd-child, You tended the cattle and ate cow’s ghee. On that day You protected the cattle with a single mountain. And earlier, for the sake of the dark-lined, red-streaked-eyed maiden of perfect excellence, You went forth and subdued the seven bulls. What a wonder—what an astonishing māyā!

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār now celebrates Kṛṣṇa’s subduing of the seven bulls.

**Word-by-word gloss.** “Protecting an elephant” — saving Gajendra. “Slaying one elephant” — destroying the fierce Kuvalayāpīḍa after incarnating as the cowherd-boy. “And not only that.” “Tending the *āṉ*” — grazing the cattle. “Eating cow-ghee.” “On that day,” during His life as herdsman, “with one mountain,” Govardhana, “protecting the cattle.” “On that day,” when the bride-price condition had been established, “for the sake of the maiden whose eyes are lined with collyrium and spread with red streaks,” Nappinnai, “whose excellence is full and complete”; “going before/going forward,” “the *āṉ*” — the seven bulls; “subduing [them].” “What a marvel! What a māyā!”

In the first line the occurrences of *āṉai* mean elephant. In the later occurrences, *āṉ* is the stem meaning cow or bull and *-ai* is an ending. In the fourth line it specifically denotes the bulls. Likewise the *ā* in “cow-ghee” means cow. In the forms *mēytti* (“You grazed”) and *uṇṭi* (“You ate”), *-ti* is a second-person singular verbal ending. Because a present-tense sense is unsuitable in context, they are interpreted with past reference.

“Going before the maiden’s excellence” means proving Yourself superior in capability to her standard or challenge. In “the maiden’s full excellence,” the element *ār* qualifies *tiṟam*, excellence or capacity: *ārutal* means to be full or to fit, so her excellence was complete. If one simply takes “for the maiden” as “for Nappinnai,” then “going before” may mean going before everyone else, going in front of the bulls, or stepping forward in Nappinnai’s presence.

“What a wonder!” Even if the seven bulls stood one behind another in support of each other, or stood arranged side by side, it was an extraordinary feat to subdue all seven in a single effort.

## Pāsuram 41

### Tamil Mūlam

ஆயனாகி ஆயர்மங்கை வேயதோள் விரும்பினாய்  
ஆயநின்னை யாவர்வல்ல ரம்பரத்தொ டிம்பராய்  
மாயமாய மாயைகொல் அதன்றி நீ வகுத்தலும்  
மாயமாய மாக்கினா யுன் மாயமுற்று மாயமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*āyaṉāki āyarmaṅkai vēyatōḷ virumpiṉāy*  
*āyaniṉṉai yāvarvalla ramparatto ṭimparāy*  
*māyamāya māyaikol ataṉṟi nī vakuttalum*  
*māyamāya mākkiṉā yuṉ māyamuṟṟu māyamē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

Becoming a cowherd, You desired the bamboo-like shoulders of the cowherd-maiden. Who in the upper world or this lower world can ever be capable of comprehending You? Is this the wondrous māyā that itself is a marvel? And beyond that, O wondrous One, even what You Yourself have apportioned and ordered You make into a wonder. Your māyā in its entirety is nothing but wonder!

### English Translation of the Commentary

Reflecting on the fruit of Kṛṣṇa’s subduing the bulls, the Āḻvār asks: when You are fully capable of winning Nappinnai by Your power, why did You first become a cowherd at all?

**Word-by-word gloss.** “The cowherd-maiden,” Nappinnai; “her bamboo-like shoulders”; “becoming a cowherd, You desired [them].” “Together with the upper world,” “and being in this lower world,” “who is capable of [examining] You?” “Is it a māyā that has itself become a marvel?” “Beyond that,” “even Your apportioning/ordering,” “O wondrous One,” “You have made wondrous.” “All Your māyā,” Your entire mode of divine contrivance, “is wonder indeed.”

Why should You, Lord of all, have to become a cowherd? One might answer: in order to embrace the shoulders of Nappinnai, a cowherd-maiden. Yet it was only after becoming a cowherd that You saw her bamboo-like shoulders and desired them; it is not as though You first desired them and therefore became a cowherd. If that explanation were literally the whole truth, why in turn did Nappinnai herself have to be born as a cowherd-girl?

When You could obtain her effortlessly merely by subduing the seven bulls, why was a cowherd birth needed? What was Your age? Why did You, the eternal Lord, enter into a marriage arrangement requiring the breaking of seven bulls? If one is determined on marriage without waiting upon rites such as *upanayana*, what significance can caste difference have for that Lord? Even while remaining a kṣatriya You could, according to ordinary social possibilities, have married a beautiful woman of a lower varṇa. Who can reason out all these deliberate arrangements of Yours?

You are the One who manifests here after coming from the Nitya-vibhūti. Even Brahmā and the others in the upper worlds lack the power to investigate Your acts fully; those in the lower world certainly lack it. Even one who could somehow enter both worlds would find this nature difficult to know. The Upaniṣads say in effect: “If you think, ‘I know Brahman well,’ you know only a small aspect of Its form—whether in yourself or among the gods; therefore it still remains for you to inquire into It,” and, “It is unknown to those who think they know It [as an object]; it is known in the proper way to those who recognize that it is not so objectifiable].”

Two distinct things are called *māyā* in connection with You. One is Prakṛti, the material cause of the universe, together with its capacity to conceal the real nature of things or make one thing appear as another. The other is Your own *māyā* in the sense of sovereign resolve, *saṅkalpa*, the wondrous power by which You manifest as You will. We jīvas are subject to the first māyā; You are not. Therefore You cannot say, “I became a cowherd because I was deluded by Prakṛti.” Since this manifestation occurred by Your own sovereign resolve, only You can know its final reason. It is Your own wondrous māyā, a marvel wrought by Yourself.

Let even that stand aside. It is admittedly difficult for ordinary people to investigate deeds that are unique to You. But more than this, even the ordinary order of things that You Yourself have assigned to the world becomes inexplicable and marvelous through Your involvement. How then could Your greatness ever be measured?

The phrase “māyam-āya māyai kol” admits two explanations. It may ask, “Can this be the māyā of ignorance—Prakṛti?” Answer: no. Or it may mean, “It must be that wondrous māyā which is Your own *saṅkalpa*.” In the fourth line, *māya!* is a vocative: “O wondrous One!”

“You make even what You have apportioned into a wonder” can mean: even the domain You assigned not for Yourself but for bound souls is made astonishing when You enter it; the actions normally performed by saṃsārins become wondrous through You and cannot be definitively reduced to a simple explanation. Many examples could be given; the next pāsuram gives one.

Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai’s introductory connection permits substantially this sense, though his detailed gloss differs slightly. He takes *māya* in the third line as a vocative and “is it the māyā of ignorance?” as a question answered negatively. “Though You have apportioned [things]” means: even after You created and provided bodies and instruments so that the jīvas could function, the jīvas follow whatever occurs to them and perish; thus “You make [them] disappear,” causing all to dissolve back into Prakṛti. On this explanation “apportioning” denotes creation and “making disappear” denotes dissolution.

Because creation and dissolution are thus mentioned, the next pāsuram removes a possible doubt that Rudra, the agent of dissolution, might therefore be the supreme deity. This also explains why the Śiva episode appears in the middle of a sequence otherwise devoted to Kṛṣṇa. In the second line *āy* can also be read as the verbal participle of “to investigate.” *Ambaram* is the sky/upper world; *imbar* is “here,” this world.

## Pāsuram 42

### Tamil Mūlam

வேறிசைந்தசெக்கர்மேனி நீறணிந்த புன்சடைக்  
கீறுதிங்கள்வைத்தவன்கை வைத்தவன்கபால்மிசை  
ஊறுசெங்குருதியால் நிறைத்த காரணந்தன்னை  
ஏறுசென்றடர்த்தவீச! பேசுகூசமின்றியே.

### IAST Transliteration

*vēṟicaintacekkarmēṉi nīṟaṇinta puṉcaṭaik*  
*kīṟutiṅkaḷvaittavaṉkai vaittavaṉkapālmicai*  
*ūṟuceṅkurutiyāl niṟaitta kāraṇantaṉṉai*  
*ēṟuceṉṟaṭarttavīca! pēcukūcamiṉṟiyē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord of all who went to where the seven bulls stood and subdued them! Tell me without embarrassment the reason why You filled the hard skull held in the hand of that Rudra—who delights in being different, whose body is red, who adorns himself with ash, and who keeps the crescent moon in his lowly matted hair—with the red blood that welled from Your own divine body.

### English Translation of the Commentary

Let the wondrous conduct shown toward Nappinnai, who like You took a special incarnation, stand aside. Even Your dealings with ordinary saṃsārins are equally marvelous. To establish this by example, the Āḻvār recalls the story of the release of Śiva from the skull that clung to his hand: You who went and subdued the bulls rendered a supreme favor to one who rides a bull. This Śiva narrative occurs amid the Kṛṣṇa episodes precisely to illustrate the statement, “even what You have apportioned You make wondrous.”

**Word-by-word gloss.** “O Lord who went forth and subdued the bulls!” — O Lord of all who went to the place of the seven bulls and crushed them. “One who delighted in difference” — Rudra, who desired to make himself distinct from others in every way. “Of red body”; “wearing ash” as ornament; “in lowly matted hair,” “placing the sliced/crescent moon,” one lunar digit appearing like a line. “By him, in his hand, held”; “the hard skull.” “Upon/above it,” “with the red blood that oozed” from Your divine body, “the reason for filling it” — tell me what that reason was. “Without shrinking/embarrassment,” speak openly.

“Delighting in difference.” Rudra desired in every respect to distinguish himself from others; his red body, ash-ornament, and matted hair make that disposition visible. Unlike all others he accepted destruction as his special cosmic function. Further, thinking it inappropriate that both he and his father Brahmā should possess five heads, he chose difference by plucking off one of Brahmā’s heads, reducing Brahmā to four. That skull then clung to his hand and would not come away.

His severe austerity and yoga—performed while smeared with ash, wearing rough matted locks, and cooling his heat with the crescent moon—could not remove the sin of brahmahatyā. The gods and sages assembled and, out of compassion for his distress, advised him: “Go forth for alms. When this skull is filled by begging, it will release you and the sin will depart.” He wandered everywhere begging, but the skull would not fill.

At last he went to Badarikāśrama and cried out to the cloud-colored Lord in the spirit of the Tamil prayer, “O cloud-hued One! Even the sea, mountains, and seven worlds together are not enough to fill this skull—alas!” Then You, Kṛṣṇa, showed compassion.
