O You whose nature is to stand on equal terms in every condition and yet thereby become higher and higher! You sleep upon the serpent-bed in the ancient, mighty ocean of threefold waters. For those whose minds have been firmly formed by devotion to You and who have abandoned every other attachment, bliss arises in every way - here, there, and everywhere.

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord's extraordinary *sauśīlya* and *saulabhya* are indicated first. Whatever lowly birth or social condition He enters in an incarnation, He stands as though equal to those around Him. He does not merely tolerate their level from a distance. He assumes the same kind of birth, moves among them, befriends persons such as Guha and the cowherds, and conceals the majesty that would otherwise separate Him from them. Yet precisely through this condescension His excellence rises higher and higher. His greatness is not diminished by accessibility; accessibility itself becomes a still greater greatness.

*Muttirattu mūri-nīr* can be taken as the ocean made up of three kinds of waters - waters arising from springs, from rain, and from rivers. *Mūri* may mean ancient, mighty, or abundant. In that vast ocean He reclines upon the serpent couch.

The distinction between *pattu* and *pācam* is deliberate. *Pattu* is attachment in the noble sense - devotion or *bhakti* directed to the Lord. *Pācam* is the binding attachment that ties one to other objects. Hence "a mind in which *pattu* has become firm" means a mind formed and stabilized by devotion to Him; "abandoning *pācam*" means releasing the bonds of other goals and objects. One may recall the familiar expression describing those devotees who possess attachment to the Lord's feet.

For such a person, Bhagavad-anubhava is bliss "in every way." It is not restricted to one geographical place or one state of existence. "Here, there, and everywhere" may first contrast this world and the other world: the devotee tastes Him here and in Paramapada there. But an intense devotee can experience Him wherever he happens to be, because the Lord, out of love, makes Himself present where His devotee is. The wording therefore also establishes an equality between "here" and "there" so far as the essential sweetness of the Lord's experience is concerned.

Yet the abandonment of other attachments does not arise independently. The mind stands firmly in Him because He has made Himself accessible in this astonishingly condescending way. Since His own accessibility produces the devotion that displaces worldly bonds, the resulting bliss is secure.

## Pāsuram 83

The Āḻvār now compares even the imperishable bliss of heaven with the extraordinary sweetness of binding the mind to the Lord here through unwavering love.

### Tamil Mūlam

மட்டுலாவு தண்டுழாய் அலங்கலாய்! புலன்கழல்  
விட்டுவீழ் விலாதபோகம் விண்ணில்நண்ணியேறினும்  
எட்டினோ டிரண்டெனும் கயிற்றினால் மனந்தனைக்  
கட்டிவீ டிலாதுவைத்த காதலின்ப மாகுமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*maṭṭulāvu taṇṭuḻāy alaṅkalāy! pulaṉ-kaḻal*  
*viṭṭu vīḻvilāta pōkam viṇṇil naṇṇi ēṟiṉum*  
*eṭṭiṉōṭu iraṇṭeṉum kayiṟṟiṉāl maṉantaṉaik*  
*kaṭṭi vīṭilātu vaitta kātal-iṉpam ākumē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord adorned with a cool tulasī garland in which honey and bees abound! Even if, leaving the feet that are perceptible here, one ascends to heaven and attains an enjoyment that never falls away, can it equal the bliss of love by which the mind is tied fast, never released, with the rope called "eight together with two"?

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord's cool tulasī garland overflows with honey, and bees immerse themselves in it. One might almost say that the bees prefer the tulasī touched by Him even to the Lord Himself. In the same manner, embodied devotees here are able to experience His accessible form with these very bodily senses and eyes. Some might nevertheless hasten toward the imperishable bliss of Paramapada. The Āḻvār asks whether even that surpasses the distinctive sweetness of holding the mind fast to Him here.

In Paramapada there is no need for these material organs, nor for the strenuous restraint of an unruly mind. Here, however, body, senses, and mind are required for worship, while those very instruments continually run toward other objects. Of them, the mind is especially difficult to control. To bind such a mind to the Lord and prevent its departure is therefore an astonishing achievement, and the love that accomplishes it has a special relish.

"Eight together with two" gives ten. The expression deliberately avoids the direct word for ten, *pattu*. Once the hearer arrives at *pattu* by calculation, the identical-sounding word *pattu*, "attachment, devotion," is suggested. The literary device resembles riddling expressions in which an object is indicated indirectly through an equivalent expression. The hidden form also conveys reverence for *bhakti*: so exalted a thing is not tossed out casually by name. Moreover, a rope strong enough to bind the mind must be twisted out of many strands, and the numerical wording suggests that labor.

The earlier teachers offer several further interpretations of the numbers. "Eight" may be the eight syllables of the *Aṣṭākṣara*, and "two" the two members of the *Dvaya*; thus mantra-anusandhāna itself becomes the rope. Or the eight may be eight virtues of the self - compassion toward all beings, forbearance, freedom from envy, purity, freedom from exhausting strain, auspiciousness, freedom from despondency, and detachment. They may instead be the eight excellences of intelligence: apprehension, retention, recollection, exposition, inference, exclusion or discrimination, understanding of meaning, and knowledge of reality. One may also take the eight limbs of yoga. Another traditional enumeration is the eight "flowers" offered in worship: non-injury, control of the senses, compassion, patience, knowledge, austerity, meditation, and truth.

The "two" can likewise be taken in more than one way: *śravaṇa* and *manana*; *pāṇḍitya* and *mauna*; or karma-yoga and jñāna-yoga. The point common to these explanations is the arduous discipline required to form an unbreakable attachment of the mind to the Lord. Thus the verse need not be reduced to only one numerical scheme.

## Pāsuram 84

Those listening ask: "If the bliss of love for His feet in this world is even greater than the bliss of Paramapada, what then do you actually desire? Is it proper for one who has performed prapatti for mokṣa to wish for liberation?" The Āḻvār answers them, rather than directly addressing the Lord.

### Tamil Mūlam

பின்பிறக்க வைத்தனன்கொல் அன்றிநின்று தன்கழற்கு  
அன்புறைக்க வைத்தநாள் அறிந்தனன்கொல் ஆழியான்  
தன்றிறத்தோர் அன்பிலா அறிவிலாத நாயினேன்  
என்றிறத்தில் என்கொல் எம்பிரான்குறிப்பில் வைத்ததே.

### IAST Transliteration

*piṉpiṟakka vaittaṉaṉkol aṉṟi niṉṟu taṉkaḻaṟku*  
*aṉpuṟaikka vaittanāḷ aṟintaṉaṉkol āḻiyāṉ*  
*taṉtiṟattōr aṉpilā aṟivilāta nāyiṉēṉ*  
*eṉtiṟattil eṉkol empirāṉ kuṟippil vaittatē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

Does the bearer of the discus intend to make me be born again? Or does He know that the day has arrived when, without another birth, I shall stand and love will remain fixed at His feet? I am a dog-like creature with not even a little love or knowledge concerning Him. What, regarding me, has my beneficent Lord placed in His divine intention?

### English Translation of the Commentary

If knowledge and love were certain to increase continually and, through birth after birth - even seven times seven births - one would remain exclusively His, then however many such births occurred could themselves be welcome. But those who cannot confidently expect such a condition see that they possess hardly any knowledge or love, perform prapatti, and seek at least to reach Paramapada and serve Him there.

The Āḻvār therefore says: "I do not know whether He intends to give me further births so that my experience of Him may continue and deepen here, or whether He sees that the exhaustion of my *prārabdha* is near and intends to bring me to Paramapada without another birth." If he considers himself one devoid of sufficient love and knowledge, then reaching Him through prapatti becomes especially praiseworthy. Although the Āḻvār in truth possesses ample spiritual power to be absorbed in the Lord through devotion, he does not display that qualification ostentatiously. For the benefit of the world he speaks gravely from the standpoint of the helpless soul.

"Has He made me be born afterward?" can also mean: "Has He resolved to grant me a succession of excellent births in which devotion may grow ever more intensely?" Or, from the opposite standpoint: "Does He intend still to give a lowly person like me births in which I must suffer further pains?" If those painful births come without knowledge of Him, they are scarcely worthy of the name birth; as the Āḻvār elsewhere says, "That day I was not born." Since even the small measure of knowledge present now would then disappear, such a birth may be regarded as a kind of death. On that basis some divide the words as *piṉpu iṟakka*, "has He made me perish afterward?"

An objection may be raised: if the Āḻvār has already performed *bhara-nyāsa* seeking mokṣa at the end of this body, how can he seriously wonder whether he will be born again? The answer is that the verse dramatizes the movement of the heart before the decisive adoption of the means: "Will He place me in bhakti-yoga? Will He leave me to the sufferings of saṃsāra without bringing me into either bhakti or prapatti? Or will He graciously establish me in some powerful means that yields mokṣa at the end of this body?" It is this pre-surrender uncertainty that is being exhibited for instruction.

## Pāsuram 85

Having answered those who questioned him, the Āḻvār resumes speaking directly to the Lord. Even a person in whom a little knowledge has arisen may again be made to forget, for heaps of old karma remain. He therefore asks the Lord not to let attachment to other objects seize his mind and produce contrary births at death.

### Tamil Mūlam

நச்சரா வணைகிடந்த நாத! பாதபோதினில்  
வைத்தசிந்தை வாங்குவித்து நீங்குவிக்க நீயினம்  
மெய்த்தன்வல்லை யாதலால் அறிந்தனன் நின்மாயமே  
உய்த்துநின் மயக்கினில் மயக்கலென்னை மாயனே.

### IAST Transliteration

*naccarāvaṇai kiṭanta nāta! pāta-pōtiṉil*  
*vaitta cintai vāṅkuvittu nīṅkuvikka nīyiṉam*  
*meyttaṉ vallai ātalāl aṟintaṉaṉ niṉ-māyamē*  
*uyttu niṉ mayakkiṉil mayakkal eṉṉai māyaṉē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord reclining upon the poisonous serpent-bed! Even now You truly possess the power to draw away the mind that has been placed upon Your lotus feet and make it depart from You. I have understood this power of Your māyā. Therefore, O wondrous Lord, do not deploy Your māyā and bewilder me among the objects that lie under Your power.

### English Translation of the Commentary

Why does the Lord who reclines choose a serpent as His bed, and why a thousand-headed serpent filled with venom? The Āḻvār playfully suspects that even this is part of the Lord's mysterious strategy: "Those who see it, grow frightened, and withdraw may withdraw!" The Lord certainly leads the worthy higher and higher and has declared, *dadāmi buddhiyogaṃ tam* - "I grant that discipline of understanding." Yet before one reaches the mature condition in which such grace is fully enjoyed, many obstacles and examinations remain.

Thus even one who has learned that the Lord's lotus feet are filled with nectar and are the sole desirable goal may, through the Lord's governance of karma, have the mind temporarily pulled away toward other objects and only later be recovered. Concerning the fallen yogin the Gītā says, in substance, that after attaining the worlds of the meritorious and dwelling there for long years he is born again and resumes the path. Since the Lord possesses this power to turn the mind away and later restore it, the Āḻvār begs Him not to exercise that power in his own case by sending forth the delusive objects of māyā.

The request is therefore: "Do not place me among worldly objects and use them to bewilder me. I know that You are truly capable of doing so; precisely because You are capable, I seek Your protection from that possibility."

The phrase interpreted above as "because You are capable" follows the earlier teachers, who take *ātalāl* causally. Others treat the final *-āl* as an expletive and construe the words as "I have understood that You are capable." This also gives an elegant sense. *Mayakkal* is a negative imperative: "do not delude."

## Pāsuram 86

The Lord is imagined to reply: "Why say, 'Do not delude me'? Merely because I am powerful, do I injure anyone without cause? What you call delusion is only the fruit of your own powerful karma." The Āḻvār responds.

### Tamil Mūlam

சாடுசாடு பாதனே சலங்கலந்த பொய்கைவாய்  
ஆடரவின் வன்பிடர் நடம்பயின்ற நாதனே  
கோடுநீடு கைய! செய்ய பாதநாளும் உள்ளினால்  
வீடனாக மெய்செயாத வண்ணமென்கொல் கண்ணனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*cāṭu-cāṭu pātaṉē calaṅkalanta poykaivāy*  
*āṭaraviṉ vaṉpiṭar naṭampayiṉṟa nātaṉē*  
*kōṭunīṭu kaiya! ceyya pāta-nāḷum uḷḷiṉāl*  
*vīṭaṉāka meyceyāta vaṇṇam eṉkol kaṇṇaṉē!* 

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord whose foot struck down the cart-demon! O Master who repeatedly danced upon the hard hoods of the writhing serpent in the water-filled pool! O You whose hand ever bears the conch! Kṛṣṇa, if I keep Your red feet within my thought every day, why do You not truly make me one who is freed?

### English Translation of the Commentary

It is the Lord's nature to remove the enemies of those who have sought Him. He kicked and destroyed the cart because He wished to preserve those whose very life consisted in enjoying Him. He danced on Kāliya's powerful hoods in order to remove the serpent that had ruined the deep pool. In the same way, why should He not free the Āḻvār from karmas that obstruct the enjoyment of Him?

*Cāṭu* may denote the cart, yielding "the foot that struck the cart"; the verb *cāṭutal* can also mean killing. *Calam kalanta* means a pond continually filled with water, but the wording can suggest how completely Kāliya's poison had entered it: not merely poison mixed into water, but as though water itself had become only a small admixture in poison. The word *āṭu*, "dancing or moving," can grammatically qualify either the serpent or its hood. A reading *arāviṉ* for *araviṉ* is also possible. *Kōṭu* means the conch, and *nīṭu* means to remain or stand permanently.

"Your red feet." They have become red from kicking the cart and from dancing for so long upon the serpent's hard hoods. Out of tender concern the devotee almost wishes to hide those feet safely inside his own heart rather than leave them exposed outside. "If I hold even those feet inwardly and You still disregard me, what am I to do?"

*Vīṭaṉāka* can mean freed from sins; it may also mean released from prakṛti and established in Paramapada. The pāsuram therefore asks that the karmas which are the root cause of future delusion be removed.

"If I think daily" does not yet signify the perfect, uninterrupted meditation later requested explicitly. It means remembering the feet whenever possible, day after day, even while obstacles to full meditation remain. Another implication is possible: "Why, even when persons think of You, do You sometimes bewilder them by granting such fruits as aiśvarya or kaivalya rather than Yourself?"

Parāśara says: *prāyaścittāny aśeṣāṇi tapaḥ-karmātmakāni vai | yāni teṣām aśeṣāṇāṃ kṛṣṇānusmaraṇaṃ param* - "Of all expiations, consisting of austerities and rites without remainder, remembrance of Kṛṣṇa is supreme." The Āḻvār's implication is: "What expiation could exceed remembrance of Your feet? The sin must therefore be removed." Remembering the word *Kṛṣṇa* in that śloka, he fittingly ends this pāsuram by calling, "Kṛṣṇa!" *Anusmaraṇa* is precisely such repeated, daily remembrance.

## Pāsuram 87

The next three pāsurams remove the suggestion that, even if mokṣa itself must come through the Lord, one might pray to other deities for the removal of sins or for subsidiary benefits.

### Tamil Mūlam

நெற்றிபெற்ற கண்ணன் விண்ணின் நாதனோடு போதின்மேல்  
நற்றவத்த நாதனோடு மற்றுமுள்ள வானவர்  
கற்றபெற்றியால் வணங்குபாத நாத! வேத! நின்  
பற்றலாலோர் பற்றுமற்றது உற்றிலேன் உரைக்கிலே.

### IAST Transliteration

*neṟṟipeṟṟa kaṇṇaṉ viṇṇiṉ nātaṉōṭu pōtiṉmēl*  
*naṟṟavatta nātaṉōṭu maṟṟumuḷḷa vāṉavar*  
*kaṟṟapeṟṟiyāl vaṇaṅku-pāta nāta! vēta! niṉ*  
*paṟṟalāl ōr paṟṟu maṟṟatu uṟṟilēṉ uraikkilē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

Rudra with the eye gained upon his forehead, the lord of heaven Indra, the lord Brahmā who performs exalted austerity upon the lotus, and all the other gods worship Your feet according to the measure of what they have learned. O Master! O One who is the import of the Veda! To speak plainly, apart from taking hold of You I have entered into no other refuge whatsoever.

### English Translation of the Commentary

Beginning with Brahmā, Rudra, and Indra, all the deities worship the Supreme Lord. They do not know Him in His completeness; each worships according to the measure of the qualities and truth he has learned. How then could the Āḻvār leave the One whom even these exalted gods worship and seek those gods themselves?

"O Veda!" means "O supreme object known and celebrated by all the Vedas." The expression *uraikkilē*, "if one speaks of it," allows another subtle observation. Even if one happened to utter the name of another deity, that name, at its deepest level, can still refer to the Supreme Lord because He is the inner ruler of all deities. Thus even their names ultimately terminate in Him. When the Āḻvār knowingly thinks of Him with words that directly and unambiguously denote Him, how could that possibly count as taking another deity as refuge? To suppose otherwise would be like hearing the name "Kaṇṇan" - Kṛṣṇa - and claiming that it denotes the deity who possesses an eye on his forehead merely because *kaṇ* means eye.

There is also a stricter interpretation of *uraikkil*: the Āḻvār is so exclusively dependent that even verbally formulating, "I do not take those other deities as refuge," is distasteful to him, because directly bringing them into the discourse at all feels contrary to his exclusive orientation. The very act of speaking about such an alternative is unwelcome.

## Pāsuram 88

The other gods worship the Lord not merely out of duty but because they themselves depend on Him for relief from distress. The Āḻvār reasons: why should a beggar go begging from another beggar? As the maxim says, *na hi bhikṣuko bhikṣukāntaraṃ yācate* - "A beggar does not beg from another beggar."

### Tamil Mūlam

வெள்ளைவேலை வெற்புநாட்டி வெள்ளெயிற்றரா வளாய்  
அள்ளலாக் கடைந்தவன்று அருவரைக்கோர் ஆமையாய்  
உள்ளநோய்கள் தீர்மருந்து வானவர்க்கு அளித்த எம்  
வள்ளலாரை அன்றிமற்றோர் தெய்வநான் மதிப்பனே.

### IAST Transliteration

*veḷḷaivēlai veṟpunāṭṭi veḷḷeyiṟṟarā vaḷāy*  
*aḷḷalāk kaṭaintavaṉṟu aruvaraikkōr āmaiyāy*  
*uḷḷa nōykaḷ tīrmaruntu vāṉavarkku aḷitta em*  
*vaḷḷalārai aṉṟi maṟṟōr teyvam nāṉ matippaṉē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

In the white ocean You planted the mountain as the churning staff and wound round it the white-fanged serpent; when You churned the sea into a turbulent mass, You became the incomparable tortoise beneath that mountain, so difficult to support, and gave the gods the nectar that cured their inner afflictions. Apart from our munificent Lord who did all this, shall I esteem some other deity?

### English Translation of the Commentary

Who planted the mountain? Who wound the serpent around it? Who churned? Who became the tortoise beneath the mountain? Who finally distributed the nectar? Our generous Lord performed all of these. The other gods, powerless to accomplish the undertaking themselves, remained involved chiefly so that they might receive and consume the result. Did they then become *mumukṣus*, desiring from this infinitely affectionate benefactor the medicine that would cure the disease of saṃsāra itself? They did not. Why should the Āḻvār esteem them as independent refuges?

The tradition and śāstra teach that *paramaikāntins* keep apart from those who are primarily seekers of worldly fruits, as though saying, "You remain you; we remain we." Their goals and orientations are different.

"White-fanged serpent." The commentator playfully suggests that Vāsuki may have held his poison in, lest it spill into the Milk Ocean, and thus his teeth appeared white. *Aḷḷal* signifies a thick, churned slush or mire; alternatively *aḷḷalā* may describe the waves rising densely under the churning. The ending *-ā* is understood as a contracted form of *āka*, "becoming."

The title *vaḷḷal*, "generous giver," reveals a still subtler generosity. The Lord Himself grants all fruits, yet in the Gītā He calls those who approach Him *udārāḥ sarva evaite* - "all these are generous." In ordinary human life, when we give someone a donation, merit may later return to us many times over; on that ground we might flatter the recipient as our benefactor. But what future profit can the Lord possibly gain from those to whom He gives? None. Even so, He calls the recipients "generous," as though their acceptance had given Him something. He treats the fame, "He is the giver of all fruits," which their receiving makes manifest, as a gift they have bestowed upon Him. Such is the nature of the true *vaḷḷal*. This pāsuram too may be read as a direct address to the Lord.

## Pāsuram 89

The Lord is imagined to object: "I have granted the gods powers to distribute the fruits desired by human beings, while I directly give the gods what they require. They should not therefore be regarded as beggars exactly like you. It may be proper to honor them and receive through them." The Āḻvār answers that the Lord directly grants even human goals, as He did for Arjuna and the Pāṇḍavas; hence the Lord alone remains his refuge.

### Tamil Mūlam

பார்மிகுத்த பாரமொன்று ஒழிச்சுவான் அருச்சுனன்  
தேர்மிகுத்து மாயமாக்கி நின்றுகொன்று வென்றிசேர்  
மாரதர்க்கு வான்கொடுத்து வையம் ஐவர் பாலதாம்  
சீர்மிகுத்த நின்னாலோர் தெய்வம் நான் மதிப்பனே.

### IAST Transliteration

*pārmikutta pāramoṉṟu oḻiccu-vāṉ aruccuṉaṉ*  
*tērmikuttu māyamākki niṉṟukoṉṟu veṉṟicēr*  
*māratarkku vāṉkoṭuttu vaiyam aivar pālatām*  
*cīrmikutta niṉṉāl ōr teyvam nāṉ matippaṉē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

To remove the unparalleled burden that had increased upon the earth, You mounted Arjuna's chariot, became greater than the warrior Himself as his guide and teacher, performed wondrous stratagems, stood unfailingly beside him and slew; You granted heaven to the victorious great warriors and gave the earth into the keeping of the five Pāṇḍavas. Apart from You, whose excellence so surpasses all, shall I esteem another deity?

### English Translation of the Commentary

*Oḻiccu-vāṉ* is a purposive form: "in order to remove." Though Arjuna was the warrior seated in the chariot and the Lord outwardly only his charioteer, when Arjuna became confused through misplaced affection, compassion, and erroneous judgment and bowed before Him, the Lord rose above him as his *ācārya*. In that sense He "became greater upon Arjuna's chariot."

"Making wondrous devices." The Mahābhārata war contains many examples. For the killing of Jayadratha He used the radiance of Sudarśana to conceal the sun and create apparent darkness. In the encounter with Bhīṣma He appeared to break His own vow and rushed forward wielding Sudarśana. For Droṇa's fall He arranged the declaration "Aśvatthāmā is slain" and had Pāñcajanya sounded so that the qualifying word *kuñjaraḥ*, "the elephant," would not be heard. These and similar acts are the "māyā" or extraordinary stratagems intended here.

"Great warriors joined to victory" can mean warriors such as Bhīṣma and Droṇa who had until then known victory everywhere. It need not mean that they won this particular war. Alternatively, because the Lord sent such heroes to heaven through extraordinary means, their death itself may be regarded as a victory.

The wicked party had declared, in effect, *sūcyagraṃ na pradāsyāmi* - "I shall not give even so much land as can be pierced by the point of a needle." The Lord destroyed those who refused even that much and bestowed the whole earth upon the five Pāṇḍavas, who were like the five vital breaths. What greater generosity toward human beings could be asked?

The forms meaning "exceeded" in the three lines all indicate surpassing excellence. Earlier interpreters also take some of them in senses such as "caused to proceed" or understand the enemy warriors as those who confidently imagined victory to be theirs. Whatever the grammatical refinement, the doctrinal conclusion of these three pāsurams is one: other deities do not possess independent power to bestow fruits. Even if some limited fruit is received through them, the Āḻvār, established in *ananya-gatitva*, refuses such mediation and takes only the Supreme Lord as refuge.

## Pāsuram 90

The Lord is imagined to ask: "Even if you worship Me alone, why not examine your individual sins, perform the corresponding expiations one by one, and then obtain My grace?" The Āḻvār answers by declaring his complete lack of independent qualification - *ākiñcanya* - and his sole reliance on the Lord's feet.

### Tamil Mūlam

குலங்களாய ஈரிரண்டில் ஒன்றிலும் பிறந்திலேன்  
நலங்களாய நற்கலைகள் நாலிலும் நவின்றிலேன்  
புலன்களைந்தும் வென்றிலேன் பொறியிலேன் புனித! நின்  
இலங்குபாதம் அன்றிமற்றோர் பற்றிலேன் எம் ஈசனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*kulaṅkaḷāya īriraṇṭil oṉṟilum piṟantilēṉ*  
*nalaṅkaḷāya naṟkalaikaḷ nālilum naviṉṟilēṉ*  
*pulaṉkaḷ aintum veṉṟilēṉ poṟiyilēṉ puṉita! niṉ*  
*ilaṅku-pātam aṉṟi maṟṟōr paṟṟilēṉ em īcaṉē!* 

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

I have not been born in any one of the four distinguished social orders. I have not mastered even one among the four auspicious bodies of sacred learning. I have not conquered the five senses; I possess no worthy mark or resource, but have become a lodging for their objects. O Purifier! O our Lord! Apart from Your radiant feet I possess no other refuge.

### English Translation of the Commentary

**Word-by-word sense.** The "two times two" distinguished *kulas* are the four *varṇas*. To one born in any of them, śāstra provides appropriate ways of removing sin. Because the Āḻvār regards himself as not securely established in any such qualified social condition, he says he has not duly studied the corresponding sacred disciplines either. Without such disciplined learning, how could he have conquered the five senses? Instead, he finds himself crowded by sense-objects, almost as though he were their proprietor or dwelling-place. Lacking eligibility, knowledge, and power, how could he perform separate expiations for each accumulated sin? For one who is *akiñcana*, possessing nothing to offer as an independent means, the Lord's shining feet alone are the support. The implication is that surrender to them is the means.

The syntax may also be reversed: "I possess no *poṟi*; why? because I have not conquered the senses; why? because I have not studied; why? because I lack the requisite birth." The wording *nālilum*, rather than merely *nālum*, means "not even one among the four," and the word "one" from the first line may mentally be carried into the second.

One might object that only the first three *varṇas* possess authority for Vedic study and for the Vedic disciplines of karma-yoga and bhakti-yoga; why, then, does the Āḻvār speak of all four? Precisely this shows that the verse need not be restricted to eligibility for bhakti-yoga. Its immediate subject is the many forms of expiation. Alternatively, the later discussion supplies the answer: even the fourth *varṇa* has prescribed *pāka-yajñas* and disciplines employing the namaskāra-mantra according to authorities such as Yājñavalkya, so "good learnings" may denote the forms of sacred knowledge appropriate to each order rather than simply the four Vedas.

Can *kula* mean *varṇa*, when lineage and social order are not identical? Here *kula* signifies a distinguished, unmixed line of descent. Just as learning and observance lend excellence to a lineage, freedom from *varṇa-saṅkara* does so as well. One born from mixed conditions would not ordinarily boast, "I was born in a distinguished kula." When the four principal categories are sought, the four *varṇas* are therefore intended.

A biographical difficulty also arises. The Āḻvār is traditionally connected with the Bhārgava brāhmaṇa line. How can he say that he was not born in any of the four? One explanation is that although born in that line he was reared in the household of a bamboo-working cultivator family; just as Kṛṣṇa, though born in the Yadu line, grew up among cowherds and is called Gopāla, the Āḻvār speaks from the social condition in which he was raised. Another traditional account says that the Bhārgava sage, while engaged in austerity, was distracted by the celestial woman Kanakāṅgī and that the Āḻvār arose from that union. On such a narrative he may lament that he lacks an unimpeachably distinguished birth. This also explains why he says not merely "the four varṇas" but "the four that are distinguished kulas."

The second line can, in one interpretation, refer directly to the four Vedas. A further reconciliation is possible: even if the Āḻvār, as the son of Bhārgava, could in principle enter brāhmaṇa status - just as Śakuntalā, though of unusual birth, entered kṣatriya status - his unusual upbringing prevented the timely performance of *upanayana* and systematic Vedic study. The lament is then not a metaphysical denial of ancestry but a statement that neither his birth circumstances nor his education gave him the conventional qualifications upon which ordinary expiatory disciplines depend.

*Poṟi ilēṉ* admits several nuances. A *poṟi* can be a device or hook by which a fish is caught; the Āḻvār may be saying that he is like a fish caught by sense-objects. The word can also mean mark, intelligence, wealth, merit, or distinguishing qualification; thus "I have none of the identifying excellences of a proper servant." Since *veṉṟilēṉ*, "I have not conquered," points to commissions of prohibited conduct, *poṟiyilēṉ* may additionally suggest failure in required duties.

Some take the verse as a statement of incapacity for bhakti-yoga. Others, more immediately in accord with the preceding pāsurams, take it as incapacity to carry out a vast catalogue of separate *prāyaścittas*, making prapatti itself the comprehensive expiation. Scriptural statements such as "by dharma one casts off sin" and the requirement that the contemplative be peaceful, self-controlled, and withdrawn show why sins obstruct the higher disciplines. The Gītā's final teaching, as explained in the tradition, likewise supports surrender as the refuge of one unable to perform all other duties separately. The *arcā*-like radiance of the Lord's feet thus stands in the place of all other means for one who has acknowledged *ākiñcanya* and *ananya-gatitva*.

## Pāsuram 91

Having established both *ananya-gatitva* - that he has no refuge other than the Lord - and *ākiñcanya* - that he possesses no independent means - the Āḻvār now takes the Rāma narrative as his assurance that the Lord personally does everything necessary for one who depends on Him. He also begins to state the precise result he seeks.

### Tamil Mūlam

பண்ணுலாவு மென்மொழிப் படைத்தடங்கணாள் பொருட்டு  
எண்ணிலா வரக்கரை நெருப்பினால் நெருக்கினாய்  
கண்ணலால் ஓர்கண்ணிலேன் கலந்தசுற்றம் மற்றிலேன்  
எண்ணிலாத மாய! நின்னை என்னுள்நீக்கல் என்றுமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*paṇṇulāvu meṉmoḻip paṭaittaṭaṅkaṇāḷ poruṭṭu*  
*eṇṇilā arakkarai neruppiṉāl nerukkiṉāy*  
*kaṇṇalāl ōr kaṇṇilēṉ kalanta cuṟṟam maṟṟilēṉ*  
*eṇṇilāta māya! niṉṉai eṉṉuḷ nīkkal eṉṟumē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

For the sake of that lady of broad, weapon-like eyes and soft speech moving like melody, You pressed down and destroyed countless rākṣasas with the fire of Your arrows. O immeasurable wondrous Lord! I have no other eye, path, or refuge than You, and no other kindred truly joined to me. Never, at any time, remove Yourself from within me.

### English Translation of the Commentary

Rāma was captivated by Sītā's speech and eyes. The epic represents Him remembering her sweet utterance and declaring that He could not live even for a moment without the dark-eyed lady. Her eyes and words had such force because she was not a stranger: she was His wife, joined to Him by an established relationship - *dārāḥ pitṛkṛtā iti*, a union ratified by the fathers. For her sake He destroyed an innumerable host of rākṣasas with arrows blazing like fire.

The Āḻvār says, "Protect me in that same way." He too offers, according to his capacity, gentle words filled with musical cadence, but all of them concern the Lord. He too wishes to see with the inner eye. In relation to the Supreme Person the whole remaining universe is, as the traditional expression has it, *strī-prāyam itaraj jagat* - "the rest of the universe is, as it were, feminine." Thus even a male devotee stands toward Him in the receptive position represented by Sītā.

The Āḻvār too has rākṣasas blocking his way: the terrible mind with its ten sense-faces, as suggested by the expression *daśendriyānanaṃ ghoraṃ yo mano rajanīcaram*. These inner enemies must be scorched by the fire of knowledge whose source is the Lord Himself. Just as He was Sītā's "eye" or sole protector, He is the Āḻvār's eye and refuge.

There is, however, a difference that makes the Āḻvār's need greater. Sītā possessed power. She says to Rāvaṇa:

*asandeśāt tu rāmasya tapasas cānupālanāt |  
na tvā kurmi daśagrīva bhasma bhasmārha-tejasā ||*

"Because I have no command from Rāma, and because I preserve the discipline of my austerity, I do not, O ten-headed one, reduce you to ashes with the fire that could fittingly burn you." She refrained from protecting herself by her own power because, having taken refuge in Rāma, she would not diminish His glory as protector. The Āḻvār, by contrast, says he possesses no such independent power at all.

There is another difference. While Sītā was in the Aśoka grove she had at least some well-disposed connections through Vibhīṣaṇa, such as Trijaṭā; and Rāma sent Hanumān to help her. When she was on the verge of abandoning life, Hanumān came near and revived her with Rāma's message. The Āḻvār says: "I have no truly congenial relative concerned for my spiritual welfare. There may be innumerable persons called relatives whose minds do not agree with mine; of what use is that? You must therefore protect me Yourself."

A final distinction sharpens the prayer. Sītā wanted to come and remain where Rāma was. The Āḻvār asks the Lord to come and remain inside him without departure. If Sītā merely had to be brought to Rāma, Rāma could in principle have sent someone to escort her, yet in accordance with her heart He Himself came. But if the Lord must be placed inside the Āḻvār, where can the Āḻvār go to accomplish that? The Lord Himself must come. Hence: "Never remove Yourself from within me."

## Pāsuram 92

The Āḻvār is not content with the Lord silently doing what is needed. Even if the Lord can, through sheer grace, enter and remain within him just as He subdued the seven bulls for Nappinnai without first delivering a formal promise, the devotee's heart might still fear later separation. He therefore asks for the explicit word of assurance: "Do not fear."

### Tamil Mūlam

விடைக்குலங்கள் ஏழடர்த்து வென்றிவேற்கண் மாதரார்  
கடிக்கலந்த தோள்புணர்ந்த காலியாய! வேலைநீர்  
படைத்தடைத் ததிற்கிடந்து முன்கடைந்து நின்றனக்கு  
அடைக்கலம் புகுந்தவென்னை அஞ்சலென்ன வேண்டுமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*viṭaikkulaṅkaḷ ēḻaṭarttu veṉṟivēṟkaṇ mātarār*  
*kaṭikkalanta tōḷpuṇarnta kāliyāy! vēlai-nīr*  
*paṭaittaṭaittu atiṟkiṭantu muṉkaṭaintu niṉṟaṉakku*  
*aṭaikkalam pukuntaveṉṉai añcaleṉṉa vēṇṭumē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O cowherd who subdued seven noble bulls and embraced the fragrant shoulders of the lady whose victorious eyes are spears! You first created the ocean, reclined upon it, churned it, and later dammed it with a bridge. I have entered into You as a thing entrusted for protection, taking refuge in You alone. You must say to me, "Do not fear."

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Āḻvār had spoken of the Lord destroying the rākṣasas for the sake of the Great Goddess. But He performed far more for Her. He did not merely bridge the ocean in order to reach Sītā. He churned that same ocean, reclined upon it, and, if one examines the matter in the spirit of divine love, one may say that He created the ocean itself so that He might recline there together with Her. Nor is His infatuated effort restricted to Śrī alone. For Nappinnai He labored to subdue seven terrible bulls. More than laboring, He altered His very social appearance and became a cowherd in order to win and embrace her. The Āḻvār therefore reasons that he too can become a recipient of such grace.

The phrase *aṭaikkalam pukutal* is especially important. Periyavāccāṉ Piḷḷai explains it as *bhara-samarpaṇa*: "My affair, my burden, is Yours alone." To enter refuge is to place oneself before Him as the object that must be protected and to transfer the burden of protection to His feet. This also clarifies the preceding example of the Great Goddess. The Lord's protection there is not cited to establish that He saves those who never seek Him in any manner whatsoever; rather, Her own relation of refuge is presupposed. Surrender consists in placing one's burden at His feet.

*Kulaṅkaḷ* means kinds or breeds, not merely a collection. Each of the seven bulls was a distinguished bull of its own formidable type. Those who take *kula* in the sense of class or *varṇa* arrive at essentially the same idea of distinguished kinds.

In *mātarār*, the ending *-ār* can simply be honorific, without assigning it a separate lexical meaning. One may also construe *kaṇ-mātar* as "the lady distinguished by the beauty of her eyes."

The accepted reading is *kaṭaintu*, "having churned," which is both widespread and contextually natural. A conjectural reading *kaṭainta*, adopted merely to simplify syntactic transposition, is not found in the received copies examined by the commentator.

*Añcal* is a negative imperative of the same grammatical type as the earlier *mayakkal*, "do not delude," and *nīkkal*, "do not remove." It means *añcēl*: "Do not fear." The Āḻvār wants not only protection but the audible divine assurance that removes the fear of future abandonment.

## Pāsuram 93

The Āḻvār now makes explicit which present form he has in mind when he speaks of Rāma, Kṛṣṇa, and the Lord who must grant fearlessness. He prays to the Lord of Śrīraṅgam for the compassion that underlies the word "fear not."

### Tamil Mūlam

சுரும்பரங்கு தண்டுழாய் துதைந்தலர்ந்த பாதமே  
விரும்பிநின்று இறைஞ்சுவேற்கு இரங்கரங்கவாணனே!  
கரும்பிருந்த கட்டியே கடற்கிடந்த கண்ணனே!  
இரும்பரங்க வெஞ்சரந் துரந்தவில்லி ராமனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*curumparaṅku taṇṭuḻāy tutaintalarnta pātamē*  
*virumpiniṉṟu iṟaiñcuvēṟku iraṅkaraṅkavāṇaṉē!*  
*karumpirunta kaṭṭiyē kaṭaṟkiṭanta kaṇṇaṉē!*  
*irumparaṅka veñcaram turantavilli rāmaṉē!* 

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord of Raṅgam, have compassion on me, who stands desiring and surrendering only to the feet where cool tulasī grows thick and blossoms, while bees sink into it! O primordial block of sweetness like sugar itself! O Kṛṣṇa reclining in the Milk Ocean! O Rāma, bowman who shot fierce arrows that made even iron split and perish!

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord who is supremely enjoyable in Paramapada reclined in the Milk Ocean for the protection of those who seek Him. Still not satisfied with that accessibility, He assumed incarnations such as Rāma and Kṛṣṇa. Still not satisfied, He now lives at Śrīraṅgam so that persons like the Āḻvār may see and enjoy Him directly. Therefore the Āḻvār addresses precisely the Lord of Raṅgam and asks Him to show compassion.

The bees that sink into the tulasī at His feet are a model for the devotee. By contact with those sacred feet, the tulasī becomes cool, tender, flowering, and overflowing with honey. The bees treat it as their entire enjoyment and lose themselves in it. Likewise the Āḻvār desires nothing beyond those feet and has surrendered there.

"The block of sugar that has existed." If one merely said "a sugar lump," that would ordinarily be a lump processed out of sugarcane. Here the wording suggests an impossible but expressive comparison: an uncreated, beginningless block of sweetness, not something manufactured from another source. The Lord's sweetness is original and self-existent.

"Kṛṣṇa reclining in the ocean." A traditional Sanskrit verse says:

*eṣa nārāyaṇaḥ śrīmān kṣīrārṇava-niketanaḥ |  
_nāga-paryaṅkam utsṛjya hy āgato madhurāṃ purīm ||*

"This glorious Nārāyaṇa, whose dwelling is the Milk Ocean, abandoned the serpent couch and came to the city of Mathurā." Thus the One reclining in the Milk Ocean is the very One who appeared as Kṛṣṇa.

Because Brahmā could not repeatedly obtain leisure to visit the Milk Ocean for worship, that same Lord was established for him in an *arcā* form in Brahmā's own realm. He then came to Ayodhyā, and ultimately to the midst of the Kāverī, where He is known as Raṅganātha. The forms are not unrelated deities; the same Supreme Lord moves through these modes of accessibility.

"So that iron should be destroyed" may refer to iron forts, armor, weapons, or to the iron-hard bodies of the rākṣasas. His arrows were so fierce that whatever they penetrated became heated and split apart. Thus Rāma's martial power and Raṅganātha's tender accessibility belong to one and the same Lord whom the Āḻvār approaches.

## Pāsuram 94

The Lord is imagined to say: "You ask Me to have compassion, to say 'fear not,' and to remain within you. Why must I be within you? A person living in this world cannot simply cease seeing all worldly things and think only of Me." The Āḻvār replies by explaining how all things are experienced as dependent modes of the Lord rather than as independent rivals to Him.

### Tamil Mūlam

ஊனின்மேய வாவிநீ உறக்கமோடுணர்ச்சிநீ  
ஆனின்மேய வைந்துநீ அவற்றுள்நின்ற தூய்மைநீ  
வானினோடு மண்ணுநீ வளங்கடற் பயனுநீ  
யானுநீ யதன்றியெம் பிரானுநீ யிராமனே.

### IAST Transliteration

*ūṉiṉmēya āvinī uṟakkamōṭu uṇarccinī*  
*āṉiṉmēya aintunī avaṟṟuḷniṉṟa tūymai nī*  
*vāṉiṉōṭu maṇṇunī vaḷaṅkaṭaṟ payaṉunī*  
*yāṉunī yataṉṟiyem pirāṉunī yirāmaṉē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Rāma! You are the vital breath that excels within this fleshly body; You are sleep and waking awareness. You are the five products that are excellent in the cow, and You are the purifying efficacy abiding in them. You are heaven and earth; You are the fruits obtained from the abundant ocean. I too am You - yet beyond all such unity, You are also our Lord.

### English Translation of the Commentary

It is certain that one living in the world cannot simply stop perceiving the world. Moreover, one who undertakes yoga for the experience of the Lord requires a body and vital breath, sleep and waking in proper measure - as the Gītā says, *yukta-svapnāvabodhasya* - and also means such as *pañcagavya* for removing ritual and bodily impurities. When examined carefully, all these things are necessary within embodied practice.

But if the Lord is firmly established in the mind, every object can be received with the understanding expressed in *brahmārpaṇaṃ brahma havir brahmāgnau brahmaṇā hutam*: the offering, the oblation, the fire, and the offerer are all dependent upon Brahman. Worldly things are then not grasped as autonomous objects for private enjoyment; their experience is understood as governed by Him and ordered to Him. That is the foundation of the experience the Āḻvār seeks.

"The vital breath that excels in the body." The Upaniṣads explicitly teach the superiority of *prāṇa* among body, senses, and other functions. The "five that excel in the cow" are the five *pañcagavya* substances, and the Lord is not only those substances as their inner ruler but the purifying power associated with them.

The final words are crucial: "I too am You; and besides that, You are our Lord." They prevent the preceding identifications from being misunderstood as absolute identity in which the world literally becomes the substance of Brahman or Brahman transforms into the world. The unity intended is the Viśiṣṭādvaita relation of body and self, *śarīra-ātma-bhāva*. The world and the individual self are His body: sustained by Him, governed by Him, existing for His purposes. Even their properties are dependent upon Him. Yet He remains their Lord and inner Self, distinct from them as their ruler. Thus the verse simultaneously affirms unity and preserves the real distinction required by lordship.

## Pāsuram 95

The Āḻvār continues the preceding experience and declares that even if a new obstacle arises, the Lord alone must remove it.

### Tamil Mūlam

அடக்கரும் புலன்களைந்தடக்கி யாசையாமவை  
துடக்கறுத்து வந்துநின் தொழிற்கணின்ற வென்னைநீ  
விடக்கருதி மெய்செயாது மிக்கோரோசை யாக்கிலும்  
கடற்கிடந்த நின்னலாலோர் கண்ணிலேன் எம் அண்ணலே.

### IAST Transliteration

*aṭakkarum pulaṉkaḷ aintaṭakki yācaiyāmavai*  
*tuṭakkaṟuttu vantuniṉ toḻiṟkaṇniṉṟa veṉṉainī*  
*viṭakkaruti meyceyātu mikkōr ācai yākkilum*  
*kaṭaṟkiṭanta niṉṉalāl ōr kaṇṇilēṉ em aṇṇalē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O our Lord! I have restrained the five senses that are so difficult to restrain, cut off at the root the desires that arise for their objects, come to You, and stood in Your service. Even if You should intend to let me go and, instead of causing the true course to occur, produce in me some other overpowering desire, I have no refuge whatever apart from You who recline in the Milk Ocean.

### English Translation of the Commentary

The preceding pāsuram established that the Lord is the inner Self of body, senses, vital breath, self, and all auxiliary instruments. With that knowledge, the practitioner performs action with *sāttvika-tyāga*: the understanding that the Lord, as inner ruler, causes all action for His own purpose, and that the individual is not an independent agent or proprietor of the fruit. The senses, which drag one toward the fruits of action, must therefore be restrained, and desire for their objects must be severed together with its *vāsanā* or latent root.

The Āḻvār says that even after one has come this far, the Lord may, in accordance with karma, allow some new desire to arise, just as He has allowed various experiences in the past. Such a desire may in turn bring pleasure or pain. Even then, the Āḻvār has no second remedy: the same Lord must remove the obstacle. Therefore he asks the omnipotent inner ruler to remain unmistakably present within him, so that His service may proceed properly, the senses may not enter contrary objects, and the knowledge of Him as the inner Self may remain stable.

*Tuṭakku* can mean entanglement, connection, or the beginning of something. Here it indicates the subtle beginning or root of desire - the *vāsanā* that must be cut away.

"You who recline in the ocean" reminds the Lord of the very purpose of His accessible manifestation: He has taken that position in order to fulfill the needs of those who seek Him. "Our Lord" likewise expresses the natural relation: asking belongs to the dependent self; protecting belongs to the omniscient Master. The individual is *śeṣa*, a dependent servant; He is the *śeṣin*, the one to whom that servant belongs.

## Pāsuram 96

The Lord is imagined to say: "Your wish to remain in My service and to preserve knowledge of Me is proper. But if you ask that I prevent every contrary desire from ever arising while you remain embodied, that is not possible. So long as there is a body, pleasure and pain necessarily arise: *na ha vai saśarīrasya sataḥ priyāpriyayor apahatir asti*. If some contrary desire creates fresh fault, you can later resort to Me as expiation." The Āḻvār replies that such a cycle would have no end and therefore asks for liberation from further birth.

### Tamil Mūlam

வரம்பிலாத மாய! மாய! வையமேழும் மெய்ம்மையே  
வரம்பிலூழி யேத்திலும் வரம்பிலாத கீர்த்தியாய்!  
வரம்பிலாத பல்பிறப் பறுத்துவந்து நின்கழல்  
பொருந்துமா திருந்தநீ வரஞ்செய் புண்டரீகனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*varampilāta māya! māya! vaiyam ēḻum meymmaiyē*  
*varampil ūḻi ēttilum varampilāta kīrttiyāy!*  
*varampilāta palpiṟap paṟuttu vantu niṉkaḻal*  
*poruntumā tiruntanī varañcey puṇṭarīkaṉē!* 

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord of boundless māyā! O wondrous One! O Puṇḍarīka, pure and supreme! Even if all seven worlds praised You truthfully through limitless aeons, Your fame would still have no boundary. Cut off my boundless succession of many births, bring me to You, reform me so that I may remain fitted to Your feet, and grant me that boon.

### English Translation of the Commentary

There is no lower boundary to the abyss called primordial prakṛti, nor is there a terminus in time from the soul's point of view so long as bondage continues. Because this māyā consists of the three *guṇas*, experiences of pleasure, pain, and delusion cannot be wholly avoided while one remains bound to it. The soul has wandered in it from beginningless time, and its powerful karmas are themselves without a beginning that can be located. Accordingly it falls without end among divine, animal, human, and immobile births. If a boundary is ever to be placed upon that process, the boon must come from the Lord who transcends māyā and is therefore the great wondrous *Mahāmāyin*.

His fame is as unbounded as His power. Suppose all seven worlds, through every limitless kalpa, praised Him exactly as He is, with each worshipper always finding something not already said by another. Even then they could never reach the shore of His greatness. No one therefore obtains His grace by first completing an exhaustive and adequate praise. Rather, He magnifies some tiny utterance of praise into a great pretext and, on that slight occasion, cuts away the series of births Himself.

The prayer is thus: "Take this small act of praise as Your pretext; terminate the births that would otherwise be endless; bring me to Paramapada; remove every contrary taste and deficiency; make me fully fit; and establish me at Your feet in service." Having tasted service to the Lord here and discovered its obstacles, the Āḻvār now desires service in the higher world where those obstacles do not arise.

## Pāsuram 97

The Lord is imagined to object that the Āḻvār has shifted positions: earlier he praised the love that binds the mind to the Lord here even above heavenly enjoyment; then he sought the Lord's indwelling presence so that service could continue here; now, because uninterrupted love is difficult, he asks for mokṣa. Yet liberation cannot be demanded in violation of śāstra without adopting a revealed means. The Āḻvār therefore asks the Lord to disclose and grant the means that is actually possible for him.

### Tamil Mūlam

வெய்யவாழி சங்குதண்டு வில்லும் வாளுமேந்துசீர்  
கைய! செய்ய போதின்மாது சேருமார்ப! நாதனே!  
ஐயிலாய வாக்கைநோய் அறுத்துவந்து நின்னடைந்து  
உய்வதோர் உபாயம் நீ எனக்கு நல்க வேண்டுமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*veyyavāḻi caṅkutaṇṭu villum vāḷum ēntucīr*  
*kaiya! ceyya pōtiṉmātu cērumārpa! nātaṉē!*  
*aiyilāya vākkainōy aṟuttuvantu niṉṉaṭaintu*  
*uyvatōr upāyam nī eṉakku nalka vēṇṭumē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O You whose beautiful hands bear the terrible discus, conch, mace, bow, and sword! O You whose chest is joined forever by the Lady who dwells in the red lotus! O Master! Cut away the diseases of this body, which is an abode of phlegm, bring me to You, and grant me one means by which I, in precisely my condition, may reach You and truly live.

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Lord cannot plead lack of instruments for removing obstacles. His sacred hands perpetually bear the five weapons, even though four hands carry five weapons in differing arrangements. What is needed is only the divine intention to employ His power. Nor is there any lack of the *puruṣakāra*, the mediating compassion that awakens such an intention, because the Goddess is already present.

Why has She abandoned the exceedingly delicate red lotus and taken permanent residence on His chest, declaring in effect, "I will not leave even for an instant"? If She were elsewhere, the Lord might say, "First ask the Goddess; then I shall unite with Her and show grace." To prevent even that delay, She never separates from Him. She brings Him into the condition of being the saving means; and since the goal is to attain the divine Couple together, She too joins Him as part of the means to that goal. Thus nothing is lacking except their gracious resolve.

He is also the *nātha*, the responsible Master to whom the work of protection belongs. His willingness to receive prayer despite possessing all power displays His innate *vātsalya*. Everything on His side is therefore ready.

On the Āḻvār's side, the body, subject to vāta, pitta, and śleṣma and their diseases, continually supplies the detachment that makes release desirable. He has also understood that śāstra requires some slight act serving as a *vyāja*, a pretext, on the part of the soul. Scripture teaches two great means, bhakti and prapatti. Which one is proper for him? Since he has confessed, "I was not born in the four qualified kulas," and declared his *ākiñcanya*, is it prapatti? Or, because yogic power inherited from former births is visibly active in him, is it bhakti? "You know my exact condition; therefore You Yourself grant me the one means suitable *for me*." The emphatic "for me" is intentional.

## Pāsuram 98

The Lord is imagined to ask: "Why suppose that you could have qualification for anything other than prapatti? If you really believed that, why did you ask only in general for 'one means'?" The Āḻvār describes the spiritual capital he already possesses, yet still refuses to trust in another birth.

### Tamil Mūlam

மறந்துறந்து வஞ்சமாற்றி ஐம்புலன்களாசையும்  
துறந்துநின்கண் ஆசையே தொடர்ந்துநின்ற நாயினேன்  
பிறந்திறந்து பேரிடர்ச் சுழிக்கணின்று நீங்குமா  
மறந்திடாது மற்றெனக்கு மாய! நல்க வேண்டுமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*maṟantuṟantu vañcamāṟṟi aimpulaṉkaḷ ācaiyum*  
*tuṟantuniṉkaṇ ācaiyē toṭarntuniṉṟa nāyiṉēṉ*  
*piṟantiṟantu pēriṭarc cuḻikkaṇiṉṟu nīṅkumā*  
*maṟantiṭātu maṟṟeṉakku māya! nalka vēṇṭumē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

I have abandoned anger, removed deceit, renounced the desires of the five senses, and through many births have stood following only desire for You. Yet I am a dog-like creature who has incurred the sin of birth. O wondrous Lord, after this birth and death, do not neglect me: grant me another way by which I may escape the great whirlpools of suffering and never revolve in them again.

### English Translation of the Commentary

To become established in bhakti-yoga, a person must already have performed disciplines such as karma-yoga through many births, ceased to be prey to anger and agitation, removed the hypocrisy in which one thing is thought, another spoken, and a third done, conquered enslavement to each sense, and transferred every attachment from worldly objects to the Lord. The Āḻvār can recognize these marks in himself. Even so, he laments as "this dog-like one": he does not regard his present birth as a secure platform upon which the discipline can simply be developed indefinitely.

Nor does he confidently say, "If it is incomplete now, I will arrange everything properly in another birth." Why should the next birth not be worse than this one? What if he falls again into the enormous whirlpools of saṃsāra? He therefore asks: "Whether by the devotion already present or by some other means, do not neglect me; grant that I no longer revolve in those vortices."

When pāsurams 98-100 are examined together, it becomes clear that the speaker is already a yogin and asks in a manner appropriate to that condition. The commentary therefore prefers the more exact reading given above. Earlier teachers also give a simpler exposition: the Āḻvār reports whatever spiritual capital he possesses and asks generally to be removed from the whirl of repeated birth and death. On that interpretation *maṟṟu*, "another/besides," may add the positive half of mokṣa - attainment of *kainkarya* - rather than merely cessation of saṃsāra.

The thought of this pāsuram is strikingly refined and expanded in a verse of Kulaśekhara Āḻvār (Perumāḷ Tirumoḻi 1.7), which deliberately echoes some of its vocabulary:

**Original Tamil quotation**

மறந்திகழுமனமொழித்து வஞ்சமாற்றி ஐம்புலன்களடக்கி யிடர் பாரத்துன்பம்  
துறந்திருமுப்பொழுதேத்தி யெல்லையில்லாத் தொன்னெறிக்கண் நிலைநின்ற தொண்டரான  
அறந்திகழு மனத்தவர்தம் கதியைப் பொன்னி யணியரங்கத் தரவணையிற் பள்ளிகொள்ளும்  
நிறந்திகழு மாயோனைத் கண்டென் கண்கள் நீர்மல்க வென்றுகொலோ நிற்கு நாளே.

**IAST transliteration**

*maṟam tikaḻum maṉam oḻittu vañcam āṟṟi aimpulaṉkaḷ aṭakki iṭar-pārat tuṉpam*  
*tuṟantu iru-muppoḻutum ētti ellaiyillāt toṉṉeṟikkaṇ nilai-niṉṟa toṇṭarāṉa*  
*aṟam tikaḻu maṉattavartam katiyaip poṉṉi aṇi araṅkattu aravaṇaiyiṟ paḷḷikoḷḷum*  
*niṟam tikaḻu māyōṉaik kaṇṭeṉ kaṇkaḷ nīrmalka eṉṟukolō niṟku nāḷē.*

**English translation of the quotation**

"Casting away the mind in which anger flourishes, removing deceit, restraining the five senses, abandoning the heavy suffering of distress, praising Him at the appointed times, and standing established in the beginningless, boundless ancient path - when shall the day come when my eyes, overflowing with tears, stand beholding the radiant wondrous One who reclines upon the serpent couch at beautiful Raṅgam on the Kāverī, the refuge of those servants whose minds shine with righteousness?"

In Kulaśekhara's verse the second segment especially praises distinction in actual observance, while the first describes freedom from fault. This may imply that when Tirumaḻisai Āḻvār says here that he has removed anger and deceit yet calls himself a dog, his grief is that he lacks the kind of birth and circumstance in which exceptional observance can be brought to its fullest visible perfection. The special implications of Kulaśekhara's verse belong to its own commentary, but the verbal and doctrinal connection should not be forgotten.

## Pāsuram 99

The Lord is imagined to say: "You need not fall into terrible whirlpools. First enjoy the fruits of the powerful merit already begun; afterward you may attain Me, and your devotion will meanwhile become complete." The Āḻvār rejects even such postponement as equivalent to being cast into hell.

### Tamil Mūlam

காட்டிநான்செய் வல்வினைப் பயன்தனில் மனந்தனை  
நாட்டிவைத்து நல்லவல்ல செய்யவெண்ணினார் எனக்  
கேட்டதன்றி என்னதாவி பின்னைகேள்வ! நின்னொடும்  
பூட்டிவைத்த வென்னைநின்னுள் நீக்கல் பூவைவண்ணனே!

### IAST Transliteration

*kāṭṭi nāṉcey valviṉaip payaṉtaṉil maṉantaṉai*  
*nāṭṭivaittu nallavalla ceyyaveṇṇiṉār eṉak*  
*kēṭṭataṉṟi eṉṉatāvi piṉṉaikēḷva! niṉṉoṭum*  
*pūṭṭivaitta veṉṉai niṉṉuḷ nīkkal pūvaivaṇṇaṉē!* 

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O Lord of Nappinnai! O One of kāyā-flower hue! I heard that, showing me the powerful karmas I myself have done, You might fix my mind upon their fruits and intend that I undergo things that are not the true good. From the time I heard this, I have fastened my very self together with You. Do not remove me from within You.

### English Translation of the Commentary

When the Āḻvār wondered what the divine intention behind this birth might be, learned persons examining horoscopic and karmic indications could say: "The Lord will show you certain powerful karmas. Because they are *prārabdha* and have begun to bear fruit, they must be exhausted by experience. He will therefore show their corresponding results, fix your mind upon them, and for a time keep you away from exclusive absorption in Himself." Having heard such a possibility, the Āḻvār became afraid and deliberately fastened his mind to the Lord so that it would not unite with those fruits. His prayer is: "Do not remove me from Yourself."

A traditional interpretation takes *valviṉai* specifically as powerful sins, their fruit as hell, and "those who intended to make me undergo what is not good" as the servants of Yama. On that account the Āḻvār asks not to be cast into hell. But difficulties arise. "Fixing my mind upon the fruit" is not an obvious way to describe being dragged unwillingly to hell. One can rescue the syntax by saying that Yama's servants fix *their* minds upon administering the results, but the subject is not expressed.

Moreover, *valviṉai* need not mean sin alone; it can simply mean powerful karma. "Things not good" can therefore include heavenly and other non-mokṣa fruits. For a *paramaikāntin* who desires only the Lord, even Svarga is "not good." Scriptural tradition also states that the mere utterance of the Lord's names drives away the servants of Yama and that the path to hell is blocked even before higher yogic disciplines are complete. Since this Āḻvār has already renounced anger and other faults and placed the Lord within himself, it is not especially necessary to make this pāsuram merely a prayer to avoid hell. Nor, after asking in the previous verse for the ending of birth itself and deliverance from all great whirlpools, is there need to single out hell once more. The more general interpretation therefore fits better.

*Kēṭṭataṉṟi* may be divided as *kēṭṭu atu aṉṟi*: "having heard this, and apart from that fruit..." Others explain it as "having abandoned what I had heard." The adopted construction follows the first possibility.

The contrast with pāsuram 91 is deliberate. There the Āḻvār said, "Do not remove *You* from *within me*." Here he says, "Do not remove *me* from *within You*." Mere remembrance of the Lord could theoretically be used to obtain aiśvarya or another subordinate fruit. Now the Āḻvār clarifies his intention: "I have offered this self solely for Your enjoyment. Do not connect it to any other fruit." Periyāḻvār similarly says, "I took You and placed You within me; and I placed myself before You." Thus the focus is exclusively mokṣa as belonging to and enjoying the Lord.

## Pāsuram 100

The Āḻvār now states both the desired fruit and the desired means with complete explicitness.

### Tamil Mūlam

பிறப்பினோடு பேரிடர்ச் சுழிக்கணின்று நீங்குமஃது  
இறப்பவைத்த ஞானநீசரைக் கரைக்கொடேற்றுமா  
பெறற்கரிய நின்னபாத பத்தியான பாசனம்  
பெறற்கரிய மாயனே எனக்குநல்க வேண்டுமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*piṟappiṉōṭu pēriṭarc cuḻikkaṇiṉṟu nīṅkumaḵtu*  
*iṟappavaitta ñāṉanīcaraik karaikkoṭēṟṟumā*  
*peṟaṟkariya niṉṉapāta pattiyāṉa pācaṉam*  
*peṟaṟkariya māyaṉē eṉakku nalka vēṇṭumē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O wondrous Lord who are so difficult to attain! Grant me that rare vessel called devotion to Your feet - the means capable of destroying the very recurrence of birth together with the great whirlpools of suffering and of carrying even persons poor in spiritual knowledge across to the farther shore.

### English Translation of the Commentary

To transcend primordial prakṛti and attain the Lord who rules it is not easy. Even learned devotees who know the greatness of bhakti often pray merely, "In whatever birth I receive, grant that I may remember You," because they chiefly wish to avoid the terrible whirlpools of suffering. They do not necessarily add, "Let birth itself cease." From the Āḻvār's present standpoint, such persons become "poor in knowledge" precisely because they have failed to ask for the ending of birth as well.

The Āḻvār therefore asks: "Whatever form of devotion You regard as a vessel capable of carrying such persons across, ending not only suffering but birth itself, grant that to me. I do not ask merely for painless birth. I do not want distress; and whether a birth causes obvious distress or not, I do not want another birth at all." No embodied condition can in fact be guaranteed wholly free of pain.

This may indicate devotion combined with contemplation of the secret of the Lord's incarnations. The tradition teaches that a devotee who truly knows the divine nature of His birth and deeds can reach mokṣa without another birth. The Gītā says:

*janma karma ca me divyam evaṃ yo vetti tattvataḥ |  
_tyaktvā dehaṃ punarjanma naiti mām eti so 'rjuna ||*

"One who knows in truth My divine birth and action, on leaving the body is not born again; he comes to Me, O Arjuna."

The word "together with birth" is therefore not redundant. It expressly rejects even an apparently favorable birth produced by good karma. "I do not want merely the removal of pain while another good birth is supplied."

Other interpretations are also transmitted. The difficult phrase concerning the "knowledge-poor" may mean those who have forgotten the *tattva* and *hita* that enable escape yet imagine themselves omniscient; the Āḻvār asks for the special devotion that can carry even such people across and confesses that he too remained in such poverty of knowledge until now. Earlier teachers sometimes introduce the pāsuram by imagining the Lord saying, "Only *paramabhakti* can yield direct experience," whereupon the Āḻvār asks Him to grant that very *paramabhakti*. Another compatible reading is: if a yogically qualified person who has performed prapatti nevertheless asks for no further birth, there must be a form of devotion capable of producing precisely that result.

Still another explanation deserves special respect in the Śrīvaiṣṇava tradition. All the Āḻvārs are understood as *prapannas* who regard the Lord's compassion itself as the true saving means, as expressed in the line *kṛtinaḥ kamalāvāsa kāruṇyaikāntino bhaje* - "O abode of Lakṣmī, I worship those blessed ones who are exclusively dependent upon compassion." On this view, the bhakti requested here need not be *upāya-bhakti*, devotion functioning as an independently cultivated means. It can be *phala-bhakti*, devotion desired simply as the enjoyment and natural condition of the liberated servant. The word *bhakti* in this pāsuram may even denote prapatti itself in an extended sense.

The tradition also cites:

*svatantrāṅga-prapattibhyāṃ prapannāv atra tāv ubhau |  
phala-sādhanaṃ bhaktibhyāṃ bhaktāv api ca darśitau ||*

and

*sādhya-bhaktis tu sā hantrī prārabdhasyāpi bhūyasī.*

The doctrinal point drawn from such statements is that the "bhakti" which uproots the entire succession of birth, even the already-operative karmic course, is effectively the surrender that entrusts liberation to the Lord. Thus the pāsuram deliberately leaves room for the relation between bhakti, prapatti, and the Lord's own compassion without flattening them into one undifferentiated notion.

## Pāsuram 101

By "bhakti" in the previous pāsuram the Āḻvār does not mean a momentary feeling of affection, love, or fondness. He now clarifies that what he seeks is a special form of meditation in which remembrance of the Lord is uninterrupted and has matured into the form of love.

### Tamil Mūlam

இரந்துரைப்பதுண்டு வாழி ஏமநீர்நிறத்தமா!  
வரந்தரும் திருக்குறிப்பில் வைத்ததாகில் மன்னுசீர்  
பரந்தசிந்தை ஒன்றிநின்று நின்னபாத பங்கயம்  
நிரந்தரம் நினைப்பதாக நீ நினைக்க வேண்டுமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*iranturaippatuṇṭu vāḻi ēmanīr-niṟattamā!*  
*varantarum tirukkuṟippil vaittatākil maṉṉucīr*  
*parantacintai oṉṟiniṉṟu niṉṉapāta paṅkayam*  
*nirantaram niṉaippatāka nī niṉaikka vēṇṭumē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

Live forever, O Lord whose hue is like gold and the deep sea! I have one thing to beg and submit. If in Your gracious intention, which grants boons to those who seek You, You have stored enduring good for me, then You Yourself must resolve that my mind, now wandering everywhere, become one-pointed, remain fixed, and remember Your lotus feet without interruption.

### English Translation of the Commentary

Ordinary affection for the Lord may be obtained comparatively easily. But to meditate upon Him without interruption is not easily achieved. The Āḻvār therefore introduces the request solemnly: "I have something to beg and say."

"Live!" or "May You flourish!" indicates that even the fruit of the requested blessing belongs to the Lord. The Āḻvār is asking for continuous remembrance so that he may continue to praise and serve Him; the supposed beneficiary of the gift is therefore, in the devotee's understanding, the Lord Himself.

*Ēma-nīr* may evoke the mixed brilliance of gold (*hema*) and the dark sea, recalling descriptions in which both are used for the Lord's body. *Ēmam* can also suggest vastness or depth, yielding "the One whose color is like the great ocean." Other lexical possibilities include agitation, joy, or surging movement, all of which can enrich the image of the sea-colored body.

The "enduring excellences" placed in His intention are qualities and blessings that accompany one toward mokṣa rather than perishing as worldly gains do.

The essential request is not merely, "I shall remember You continuously." It is: "You must think, 'Let this person remember Me continuously.'" The Āḻvār openly confesses that without the Lord's resolve he possesses no power to establish such meditation by himself. This is why, whether the adopted discipline is bhakti or prapatti, the Lord remains the principal saving means: even the capacity to perform the subordinate means comes from Him.

## Pāsuram 102

The Lord is imagined to respond: "How can I grant uninterrupted meditation while the bodily and sensory instruments naturally suffer disease and interruption?" The Āḻvār replies that the removal of those diseases too lies within the Lord's grace.

### Tamil Mūlam

விள்விலாத காதலால் விளங்குபாத போதில்வைத்து  
உள்ளுவேனது ஊனநோய் ஒழிக்குமாறு தெழிக்குநீர்  
பள்ளிமாய! பன்றியாய வென்றிவீர! குன்றினால்  
துள்ளுநீர் வரம்புசெய்த தோன்றல் ஒன்று சொல்லிடே.

### IAST Transliteration

*viḷvilāta kātalāl viḷaṅkupāta pōtilvaittu*  
*uḷḷuvēṉatu ūṉanōy oḻikkumāṟu teḻikkunīr*  
*paḷḷimāya! paṉṟiyāya veṉṟivīra! kuṉṟiṉāl*  
*tuḷḷunīr varampuceyta tōṉṟal oṉṟu colliṭē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O wondrous One reclining in the roaring ocean! O victorious hero who became the Boar! O noble Lord who made a boundary in the tossing sea with mountains! I wish, with love that never departs, to place my mind upon Your radiant lotus feet and contemplate them. Tell me one way by which the diseases belonging to this body may be removed.

### English Translation of the Commentary

It is not natural to interpret the pāsuram as asking the Lord to destroy the body itself merely because uninterrupted remembrance is difficult while embodied. The more fitting sense is narrower and more practical: a body tormented by disease cannot function as an instrument of continuous contemplation, so the Āḻvār asks for the illnesses to be removed.

*Ūṉa-nōy* means "the disease of the flesh/body," with the intervening *-a* functioning like a genitive. Some explain *ūṉam* itself as the body and then take the expression as "the body's disease"; others say the body itself can be called a disease because embodiment is inherently defective. But in the present context the direct request is for the removal of the illnesses that obstruct meditation.

The examples chosen in addressing the Lord prove that no obstacle is too great. He reclines calmly in the roaring ocean. As Varāha He raised the earth and won victory over the crisis that had submerged it. As Rāma He crossed the tossing sea by constructing a boundary of mountains - the bridge to Laṅkā. If He can stabilize oceans, lift the world, and make a road through the sea, He can surely remove the bodily disturbance that prevents His devotee from fixing the mind.

Periyāḻvār's teaching in the decad beginning with the image of the "pot of ghee" is relevant: when the Lord enters and takes up His residence, illnesses flee. That same theological confidence operates here. The Āḻvār therefore does not turn away from the body in disgust; he asks that it be made a serviceable instrument until its appointed end.

## Pāsuram 103

The Lord is imagined to say: "You ask Me to teach you one method. Do you not know the power of reciting My names? Remain continually uttering My many names." The Āḻvār asks that even this practice be graciously taught and made continuous, and that name never be separated from the contemplated form.

### Tamil Mūlam

திருக்கலந்து சேருமார்ப! தேவ தேவ தேவனே!  
இருக்கலந்த வேதநீதி யாகிநின்ற நின்மலா!  
கருக்கலந்த காளமேக மேனியாய! நின்பெயர்  
உருக்கலந்து ஒழிவிலாது உரைக்குமாறு உரைசெயே.

### IAST Transliteration

*tirukkalantu cērumārpa! tēva tēva tēvaṉē!*  
*irukkalanta vētanīti yākiniṉṟa niṉmalā!*  
*karukkalanta kāḷamēka mēṉiyāy! niṉpeyar*  
*urukkalantu oḻivilātu uraikkumāṟu uraiceyē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O You whose chest is inseparably joined by Śrī! O God even of the gods of gods! O stainless One who stands as the true conclusion established by Vedic reasoning joined with the Ṛks! O You whose body is like a dark rain-cloud filled with majesty! Teach me how to utter Your names without interruption, joining each name with the contemplation of Your form.

### English Translation of the Commentary

The Tamil *iruk* is a phonetic adaptation of Sanskrit *ṛk*. The Upaniṣads characteristically cite Ṛgvedic verses as evidence for the truths they teach; the phrase therefore emphasizes not merely "the Veda" in the abstract but the Vedic method of establishing doctrine through revealed testimony.

"Standing as the rule or conclusion of the Veda" means that proper inquiry through Vedic reasoning establishes the Lord's supremacy. The commentary recalls the sense of a verse: "For those who inquire into reality from every side, the one supreme reality is the great yogin Hari, Nārāyaṇa." When the Veda is investigated in that manner, He shines as "the God of the gods of gods." Since *Devadeva* can be used as a title of Rudra, saying that the Lord is the God of *Devadeva* also pointedly expresses His supremacy over Rudra.

"The chest joined by Śrī" indicates that the Goddess is not an occasional associate but inseparably united with Him in every mode in which He is encountered. The form in which the devotee meditates is therefore never the Lord isolated from Śrī.

*Karuk kalanta* may mean filled with greatness or density, giving the image of a massive dark rain-cloud. Since *karu* can also carry the sense of gold in some usage, earlier interpreters also imagine a black cloud lit by golden lightning - a fitting image for the Lord's dark body adorned by the golden Goddess.

"Joining the name with form." When a name such as "Nārāyaṇa" or "Kṛṣṇa" is pronounced, one should not leave it as a bare sound. The form denoted by the name is to be contemplated at the same time, as when one remembers "a body like a great green mountain." One may broaden the explanation: while uttering the name, contemplate His qualities and divine sports as well and combine the name with words that evoke those attributes, so that the recitation blossoms into meaningful praise.

In yogic terminology *uru kalantu* can also indicate *ālambana-saṃśīlana*: repeated familiarization with the supporting object of meditation. Thus nāma-saṅkīrtana, form-contemplation, and yogic recollection are integrated rather than treated as unrelated exercises.

## Pāsuram 104

Even if formal yoga is practiced only at certain seated times, the form that serves as the support of meditation must be kept in mind throughout ordinary activity. This continuous familiarization is *ālambana-saṃśīlana*. The Āḻvār therefore asks for remembrance while lying down, sitting, standing, and moving.

### Tamil Mūlam

கடுங்கவந்தன் வக்கரன் கரன் முரன் சிரமவை  
இடந்துகூறு செய்தபல் படைத்தடக்கை மாயனே  
கிடந்திருந்து நின்றியங்குபோது நின்னபொற்கழல்  
தொடர்ந்துவிள்விலாததோர் தொடர்ச்சிநல்க வேண்டுமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*kaṭuṅkavantaṉ vakkaraṉ karaṉ muraṉ ciramavai*  
*iṭantu kūṟuceyta palpaṭait taṭakkai māyaṉē*  
*kiṭantiruntu niṉṟiyaṅkupōtu niṉṉa poṟkaḻal*  
*toṭarntu viḷvilātatu ōr toṭarcci nalka vēṇṭumē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

O wondrous Lord of broad hands bearing many weapons, who tore apart fierce Kabandha and the heads of Vakra, Khara, and Mura! While I lie down, sit, stand, and move about, grant me an incomparable continuity of thought that follows Your golden feet and never breaks away.

### English Translation of the Commentary

Kabandha and Khara belong to the Rāma incarnation. Dantavaktra - also called Dantavakra or simply Vakra - was the brother or close associate of Śiśupāla, and Mura was the demon connected with Narakāsura; their destruction belongs to the Kṛṣṇa incarnation. The verse gathers enemies from different incarnations in order to invoke the Lord's power to remove all obstacles to remembrance.

Kabandha's story requires grammatical care. Cursed by the sage Sthūlaśiras, he possessed a grotesque form and an exceedingly long life. When at one time he opposed Indra, the thunderbolt drove his head into his torso; an eye remained in his chest, and his arms extended for a yojana so that he could seize lions, elephants, and other animals and devour them effortlessly. Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa were caught between those arms and each cut off one arm. They did not at that moment split his head. Therefore *Kabandha* should not automatically be joined syntactically with "the heads" in the first line. If one insists upon that connection, one may recall that after his arms were cut he was thrown into a pit and burned, so his head too ultimately perished.

*Vakkaraṉ* is most naturally a Tamil form of *Vakra*, referring to Dantavaktra/Dantavakra. A reading *vakkiraṉ* is also conceivable.

Some manuscripts or reciters use a form corresponding to *vīḷvu*, but the meaning required is "departure, falling away," equivalent to *vīḻvu* or *viḷvu*. The first *toṭarntu* describes the constant following of the meditative support - *ālambana-saṃśīlana*. The phrase "an unbroken continuity" then specifies the true continuity of *dhyāna* within yoga. The Āḻvār asks the Lord to make the preparatory recollection and the formal meditation flow into one another without rupture.

## Pāsuram 105

The meditation now focuses on the divine forms of the Lord and the Goddess together.

### Tamil Mūlam

மண்ணையுண்டுமிழ்ந்துபின் நிரந்துகொண்டளந்துமண்  
கண்ணுளல்ல தில்லையென்று வென்றகால மாயினாய்  
பண்ணைவென்ற வின்சொல்மங்கை கொங்கைதங்கு பங்கயக்  
கண்ண! நின்ன வண்ணமல்ல தில்லையெண்ணும் வண்ணமே.

### IAST Transliteration

*maṇṇaiyuṇṭumiḻntupiṉ nirantukoṇṭaḷantumaṇ*  
*kaṇṇuḷ allatu illaiyeṉṟu veṉṟakālam āyiṉāy*  
*paṇṇaiveṉṟa viṉcolmaṅkai koṅkaitaṅku paṅkayak*  
*kaṇṇa! niṉṉa vaṇṇamalla tillai eṇṇum vaṇṇamē.*

### English Translation of the Pāsuram

You swallowed the earth, brought it forth again, later begged it from Bali, received it, and measured it, establishing that the earth is nowhere outside Your domain and glance. You are the ruler of every such age and circumstance. O lotus-eyed Lord whose gaze rests upon the bosom of the Lady whose sweet speech surpasses music itself! The form my mind contemplates is none other than Your form.

### English Translation of the Commentary

He is the administrator of every age in which the earth is swallowed at dissolution, emitted again at creation, begged from Bali, received, measured, and brought completely under His manifest sovereignty. "The earth is not outside the eye" admits two related explanations.
